# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Brick, K., Visser, M., & Van der Hoven, Z. (2016). Cooperation and Climate Change: Can Communication Facilitate the Provision of Public Goods in Heterogeneous Settings?. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Communication (chat) between group members in a heterogeneous public good game increases average contributions and the likelihood of meeting a collective target, but does not fully solve the free-rider problem. Even with communication, many groups fail to reach the target, and free-riding remains common, especially among high marginal cost players. The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests, based on prior literature, that punishment opportunities may be necessary to ensure compliance and improve efficiency, especially in heterogeneous settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of communication (chat) in a heterogeneous public good game, but does not provide any experimental evidence on punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly informative, as no punishment treatment is implemented. However, it does provide a useful control case: in a 4-player, one-shot, linear PGG with MPCR=0.42, no punishment, and no feedback on others' contributions, average group contributions are well below the social optimum, and communication increases but does not maximize efficiency. The paper's discussion and literature review suggest that punishment is likely to further increase cooperation and efficiency, but this is not tested here. Thus, this paper is best used as a control or baseline for settings without punishment.

- source: Riedel, N., & Schildberg-Hörisch, H. (2013). Asymmetric obligations. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that both high and low minimum contribution obligations (even when only weakly enforced) increase contributions in the initial periods compared to no obligation, but this effect is not sustained in the long run. Asymmetric obligations are as effective as symmetric ones: for a given own obligation, the partner's obligation does not affect behavior. The effect of obligations is interpreted as arising from an emotional cost of disobeying, not from expectations about others' behavior. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that weakly enforced minimum contribution obligations can temporarily increase contributions in public goods games, regardless of whether obligations are symmetric or asymmetric. However, since the study does not include a standard punishment institution (where players can assign costly punishment to others), and the 'punishment' here is a very weak, probabilistic, externally imposed fine for not meeting a minimum, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that expressive law (weak obligations) can have a short-term effect on cooperation, but do not inform about the efficiency effects of endogenous peer punishment. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of a standard punishment treatment limits direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under peer punishment.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2015). INCOMPLETE INFORMATION STRENGTHENS THE EFFECTIVENESS OF SOCIAL APPROVAL. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling subjects to express social approval (assigning 0-10 stars to each group member after observing contributions) significantly increases contributions to the public good compared to a control with no approval. This effect is stronger when subjects have only partial information (see only others' contributions) than when they have full information (see both contributions and endowments). The study isolates the consumption value of social approval by using stranger matching and anonymity, ruling out reputation effects. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings are reported; all main results are in terms of contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-monetary, symbolic social approval can increase contributions in public good games, but the effect is sensitive to the information structure. However, because the study does not include monetary punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is otherwise closely matched to standard PGGs, so the behavioral findings may be relevant for understanding how non-monetary incentives (like approval) interact with information, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes from punishment interventions.

- source: Yang, C. L., Zhang, B., Charness, G., Li, C., & Lien, J. W. (2018). Endogenous rewards promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The endogenous, budget-balanced reward mechanism in a repeated four-player PGG leads to a substantial and sustained increase in contributions compared to the standard PGG. The mechanism works by taxing each player's income and allowing players to allocate their share of the tax fund as rewards to others. Contributions remain high and even increase over time with rewards, while they decline in the control. The paper finds that most subjects are conditional cooperators, and that reward assignment is strongly correlated with contribution. Net profits (payoffs) are higher under the reward mechanism than in the control, and the mechanism is more efficient than punishment-based mechanisms reported in the literature, which often fail to increase net payoffs due to the cost of punishment. The study does not test punishment directly, but positions endogenous reward as a more efficient and socially desirable alternative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a budget-balanced, endogenous reward mechanism can substantially increase both contributions and net payoffs in a repeated PGG compared to a standard control. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling such a reward mechanism (with a 20% tax, peer allocation, and 1:1 reward effectiveness) will lead to higher efficiency than the control, and likely higher than typical punishment treatments (based on literature comparisons). However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, so its value for predicting the effect of punishment per se is indirect: it shows that reward can outperform punishment in terms of efficiency, especially when punishment is costly. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, feedback, etc.) are well specified and can inform parameterization for similar prediction tasks involving reward mechanisms, but not for punishment.

- source: Hassan, M., Amin, E., Mansour, S., & Voigt, S. (2023). Incentivizing cooperation against a norm of defection: Experimental Evidence from Egypt. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that (1) pride primes can increase high contributors' willingness to contribute, but with a lagged effect, and (2) appeal messages (religious, patriotic, social harm, or custom) sent by high contributors to low contributors increase the recipients' subsequent contributions by 2–5 points on average. However, most high contributors choose not to send appeals, and there is no direct monetary punishment or reward. The study does not report group efficiency or total group payoff, so the effect of these interventions on efficiency is not directly assessed. The interventions are non-monetary and adjacent to punishment, as they are costly to the sender but do not reduce the recipient's payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that non-monetary, costly appeals can increase individual contributions in a public goods game, especially in a context with a strong norm of defection. However, because the interventions do not reduce the recipient's payoff and group efficiency is not reported, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of monetary punishment on efficiency. The design is most informative for settings where costly communication or social sanctions (not payoff-reducing punishment) are available. The results suggest that such appeals can increase contributions, but the effect on efficiency remains unquantified. For prediction tasks focused on monetary punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2005). Reputation is valuable within and outside one's own social group. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study demonstrates that reputation built through cooperation in a public goods game increases the probability of being helped in subsequent indirect reciprocity rounds, and this effect is equally strong whether the helper is from the same or a different PGG group. Cooperation in the PGG is maintained at a high level while IR rounds are expected, but drops sharply when only PGG rounds remain. The experiment does not include punishment or sanctioning options, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, show that reputation-based reward mechanisms (via indirect reciprocity) can sustain high cooperation rates in PGGs, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and reward can substitute for or complement punishment, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment and lack of efficiency outcomes limit the paper's direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Akpalu, W., Abidoye, B., Muchapondwa, E., & Simbanegavi, W. (2017). Public disclosure for carbon abatement: African decision-makers in a PROPER public good experiment. *Climate and Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that public disclosure of individual pollution levels (via visible color-coded cards) reduces pollution (i.e., increases cooperation) in a linear public good game. The effect persists even after disclosure is withdrawn. The study does not use monetary punishment or reward, but rather a reputational/shame mechanism. The main outcome is the number of red cards retained (pollution), not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that public disclosure can be an effective non-monetary sanction to promote cooperation in public goods settings, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that public disclosure (a reputational/shame-based sanction) can reduce free-riding in a linear public good game, even among experienced policy-makers. However, it does not test monetary punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the effect of monetary punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-monetary sanctions can increase cooperation, but does not quantify efficiency gains or compare to monetary punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR) are well described, but the absence of monetary punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings with explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Peysakhovich, A., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Humans display a 'cooperative phenotype' that is domain general and temporally stable. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that individuals' cooperative behavior is consistent across different cooperation games (including the PGG, Dictator Game, and Trust Game) and over time, supporting the existence of a 'cooperative phenotype.' However, this cooperative tendency does not extend to norm-enforcing punishment games (Ultimatum Game, 2PP, 3PP), as there is little to no correlation between cooperation and punishment behaviors. The study also finds that cooperation in games predicts real-world helping and generalized trust, but not self-reported cooperative behaviors. The research is correlational and does not examine the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGGs or related games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it does not manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in PGGs, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that cooperative and punishment behaviors are distinct at the individual level in one-shot games, suggesting that the presence of punishment may not be strongly linked to underlying cooperative tendencies. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers little direct guidance on how punishment affects efficiency, but it does caution against assuming that those who cooperate are also those who punish, or that punishment and cooperation are tightly coupled in one-shot settings.

- source: Gächter, S., & Fehr, E. (1999). Collective action as a social exchange. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that social approval opportunities alone (i.e., the ability to express approval/disapproval after the game) do not significantly increase cooperation in a standard public goods game among strangers. However, when minimal group familiarity is introduced (via a brief pre-game interaction), the combination of social approval and familiarity leads to a large and significant increase in contributions. The effect is not observed for social approval or group familiarity alone. The main outcome is the contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that social approval can be a powerful motivator for cooperation, but only when social distance is reduced. The study does not test or report on material punishment or pecuniary sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of material punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of non-pecuniary social incentives (approval/disapproval) and their interaction with group familiarity, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that social mechanisms can increase cooperation, but only under certain social conditions. For the downstream prediction task (punishment-enabled efficiency), this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency as a primary outcome.

- source: Koukoumelis, A., & Levati, M. V. (2019). An experiment investigating the spillover effects of communication opportunities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that recipients of unfair offers in a mini dictator game reduce their contributions in a subsequent public goods game. However, if they are allowed to send a message to the dictator, this reduction is significantly smaller—only in treatments without belief elicitation. When beliefs about the partner's contribution are elicited, the messaging opportunity does not significantly affect contribution changes for the full sample, but does for the subsample of recipients who actually reduce their contributions. The mechanism appears to be emotional relief from having a 'voice,' not a change in beliefs about partner behavior. The study does not analyze group efficiency or total payoff, and there is no monetary punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the effects of non-monetary, symbolic communication (emotional expression) on cooperation, but not for monetary punishment or efficiency outcomes. The design and findings may be informative for adjacent questions about communication, emotion, and cooperation, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that displaying the identities of all contributors or only the lowest contributors (bottom two) significantly increases individual contributions to the public good, compared to a baseline with no recognition. Recognizing only the highest contributors (top two) does not significantly increase contributions. The effect is attributed to shame aversion rather than prestige seeking. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increased contributions in the ALL and BOTTOM treatments suggest that such recognition mechanisms could improve group outcomes in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-monetary, social recognition mechanisms—especially those that expose low contributors—can substantially increase contribution rates in public goods games. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The recognition mechanism here is a form of social sanction (shame), not costly punishment. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of non-monetary, reputational sanctions on cooperation, and less relevant for monetary punishment interventions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social identification of low contributors (shame) is a powerful lever for increasing cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or on the effect of monetary punishment.

- source: Grund, C., Harbring, C., Thommes, K., & Tilkes, K. R. (2020). Decisions on Extending Group Membership-Evidence from a Public Good Experiment. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that temporary group members contribute marginally more when the possibility of group membership extension is decided endogenously by a group member, compared to an exogenous random draw. However, this effect is weak and does not translate into significantly higher overall group contributions. Permanent members' contributions are not significantly affected by the extension mechanism. When a temporary member is replaced (especially exogenously), the newcomer initially contributes more than other group members, but this effect diminishes quickly. The study discusses monetary incentives and payoffs, but the main focus is on contribution behavior rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no direct analysis of how the extension or replacement mechanisms affect group efficiency relative to the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that mechanisms resembling social sanctions (replacement or exclusion) can have incentive effects on contribution behavior, but these effects are modest and short-lived. The design does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction, the results suggest that changing the group membership mechanism (endogenous vs. exogenous extension) has limited impact on overall group efficiency, and that replacement (as a form of exclusion) may temporarily boost newcomer contributions. However, these effects are primarily on contribution rates, not on efficiency or total group payoff. The study is most informative for understanding how exclusion or replacement (as a weak form of sanction) affects cooperation, rather than for predicting the efficiency impact of explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Li, J. B., Liu, X. L., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., Wang, G. R., Niu, X. F., & Zhu, C. K. (2018). Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation Altered Voluntary Cooperative Norms Compliance Under Equal Decision-Making Power. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating rDLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects voluntary cooperative norm compliance (measured as contribution) in a linear public goods game with four players, asymmetric endowments, and no punishment or sanction options. Anodal stimulation increases contributions, cathodal decreases them, and sham is intermediate. The effect is observed for both high and low endowment players. The study also finds that participants' own norm perceptions (norm.own) are more predictive of compliance than their perceptions of others' norms (norm.other), and that anodal stimulation changes the relative influence of these norm perceptions. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported, and the game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the effect of neural stimulation on voluntary cooperation (contribution) in a standard linear PGG. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are reported and may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as a control or baseline for voluntary cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

- source: PARKS, C. D., & HULBERT, L. G. (1995). HIGH AND LOW TRUSTERS RESPONSES TO FEAR IN A PAYOFF MATRIX. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in both public goods and resource dilemma games, the presence of fear (risk of zero payoff for cooperation) interacts with individual trust to affect cooperation rates. High trusters cooperate more than low trusters when fear is present, but this difference disappears when fear is absent. Removing fear increases cooperation among low trusters in resource dilemmas, but decreases cooperation among high trusters in public goods games. The paper does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, but discusses its theoretical relevance. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported; all results are in terms of cooperation frequency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how trust and fear interact to affect cooperation rates, but does not manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The discussion suggests that punishment may be more relevant in no-fear situations, but this is not tested. Thus, the paper offers only indirect, contextual support for prediction tasks involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Martinsson, P., & Persson, E. (2019). Public Goods and Minimum Provision Levels: Does the Institutional Formation Affect Cooperation?. *Scandinavian Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Imposing a minimum contribution level (5 tokens out of 20) in a linear public goods game increases average contributions compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. This effect is robust to whether the minimum is imposed exogenously or chosen endogenously by group vote. The increase in contributions is mainly due to free riders being forced to contribute at least the minimum; there is little evidence of crowding out among those who previously contributed more than the minimum. The mode of institutional formation (democracy vs. authority) does not significantly affect the outcome. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results suggest that minimum contribution rules can increase public good provision in settings where punishment is not feasible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of minimum contribution rules in public goods games, especially in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that institutional interventions (minimum levels) can increase contributions, mainly by raising the floor for low contributors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that minimum contribution rules can be effective in increasing contributions, but does not inform about the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, no chat, continuous contributions, no punishment/reward) are clearly specified, which helps in mapping to other studies. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction.

- source: Gneezy, U., Leibbrandt, A., & List, J. A. (2016). ODE TO THE SEA: WORKPLACE ORGANIZATIONS AND NORMS OF COOPERATION. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Fishermen in group-based (sea) societies exhibit higher trust, trustworthiness, pro-sociality, and cooperation in economic games—including the public goods game—than those in individual-based (lake) societies. These differences are robust to controls for selection and other confounds. The study provides evidence that workplace organization (group vs. individual work) is a key driver of cooperative norms. However, the public goods game does not include punishment or reward options, and the main outcomes are behavioral (contributions, trust, etc.), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the effect of group-based workplace organization on cooperation and contribution behavior in public goods games, but it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It does not manipulate or include punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods games. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR) are reported and can be extracted, but the absence of punishment means this paper is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency with punishment enabled.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & D'Amico, V. (2021). A preference to learn from successful rather than common behaviours in human social dilemmas. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Participants in a repeated linear public goods game (MPCR=0.4, groups of 4, 6 rounds) were more motivated to learn from and copy successful (high-earning) behaviors than to conform to common behaviors. When shown both the average (common) contribution and the average contribution of the highest earners from a previous experiment, participants contributed significantly less than when shown only the common behavior. This decline in cooperation was not explained by simply knowing about free riders, but specifically by the desire to learn from success. Most participants preferred to see information about successful behaviors over common behaviors. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the lower contributions in the 'success' information condition imply lower efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods games without punishment or reward, providing information about successful (high-earning) behaviors leads to lower cooperation and thus likely lower efficiency, compared to providing only information about common behaviors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of success-based social information (even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward) can undermine efficiency. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment directly, and so its direct relevance to predicting the effect of enabling punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of social information and learning in public goods games, and suggest that efficiency may be lower when participants can observe and learn from successful free-riding.

- source: Alenaizi, R., Contu, D., Elshareif, E., Kaya, O., & Kaya, I. (2024). Assumed similarity in personality traits has a short-lived positive effect on cooperation in absence of reciprocity. *Applied Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are told they are matched with others who have similar personality traits, their contributions to the public good increase in the short term (notably in round 7 compared to round 4). When told they are matched with dissimilar personalities, contributions decrease. However, the effect of similarity framing is short-lived and diminishes quickly. The game includes a penalty for non-contribution, but this is a fixed, automatic penalty rather than a peer-administered punishment. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The penalty mechanism is automatic and not peer-administered, so it does not map directly to the punishment dimension of interest. The findings are relevant for understanding how framing and perceived similarity affect contributions, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment mechanisms. The game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, and penalty structure are described, but the absence of a peer punishment treatment means this paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2025). Empirical evidence for the spread of Cooperation through copying successful groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that providing information about a successful, cooperative out-group increases participants' contributions in a repeated PGG, but this effect is transient and does not persist or spread within the group. The absence of mechanisms such as punishment means that cooperation is not stably maintained. The authors argue that additional mechanisms (e.g., punishment, reputation) are likely necessary for the stable diffusion and maintenance of cooperation. The cross-cultural survey finds that greater press freedom is associated with higher impersonal prosociality (e.g., general trust), but not with personal prosociality (e.g., family trust). No direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment or on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as punishment is not present in the experimental design. However, it provides indirect support for the idea that, in the absence of punishment, even interventions that increase cooperation (such as exposure to successful out-groups) do not lead to sustained or spreading cooperation or higher group payoffs. The discussion and literature review suggest that punishment may be necessary for stable, high-efficiency outcomes in repeated PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, without punishment, efficiency gains from social learning or imitation are likely to be transient. The experimental design and results can help calibrate the expected baseline (control) efficiency in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the treatment effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., Holt, C. A., Rodgers, M. S., & Thomas-Hunt, M. C. (2020). The impact of leader communication on free-riding: An incentivized experiment with empowering and directive styles. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that empowering leader messages reduce free-riding (increase contributions) more than directive messages when there is no two-way leader-follower communication. When two-way communication is allowed, both leadership styles lead to higher contributions, and the difference between styles disappears. The presence of two-way communication is especially beneficial for directive leadership. The main dependent variable is contribution rate; earnings are reported but not the focus. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that communication (especially two-way leader-follower communication) can increase contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the context in which punishment is or is not necessary. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, communication, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means the findings are only indirectly relevant for the prediction task.

- source: Guillen, P., Fatas, E., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2010). Inducing efficient conditional cooperation patterns in public goods games, an experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a grim trigger strategy threat (via possible automata) leads to a substantial increase in average contributions (by 40% before a surprise restart and 50% after) compared to a standard repeated public goods game. Contributions in the threat treatment are highly polarized (either full or zero), and the pattern of conditional cooperation changes: in the threat treatment, the minimum contribution in the previous round predicts current contributions, consistent with grim trigger logic. However, contributions still decline over time, and the effect is driven by belief manipulation rather than actual punishment or sanctions. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the higher contributions imply higher group payoffs, net of the absence of punishment costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that belief in the possibility of strict conditional cooperation (grim trigger) can substantially increase contributions in repeated public goods games, even without actual punishment. However, because there is no real punishment mechanism (no cost, no assignment of punishment, no reduction of others' payoffs), the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The results suggest that the mere threat or belief in sanctions can have large effects on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the efficiency impact of actual punishment mechanisms, especially considering the cost of punishment. The design dimensions are well specified, but the absence of real punishment means the findings should be used as indirect evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Guo, J. D. R. (2025). Doing good in the digital world. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both identification and attribution of individual contributions increase average contributions in a finitely repeated public goods game. The effect is strongest when both are present, but even imprecise identifiers (numbers, avatars) and partial attribution (not perfectly linking actions to identifiers) increase contributions relative to baseline. The effect is primarily behavioral: more subjects contribute their full endowment when individual contributions are revealed. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the game, and the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Theoretical analysis considers the possibility of punishment outside the game, but the experimental data do not support a punishment-based explanation for the observed effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how identification and attribution affect contribution behavior in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Since no punishment or reward mechanism is present, the findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with punishment enabled. However, the results do suggest that increasing transparency and reducing anonymity can increase contributions, which may interact with the effects of punishment in other designs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence on the effects of information and identification, but not on punishment or efficiency.

- source: Wei, C. (2020). Can job rotation eliminate the Ratchet effect: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that ethnic dominance (caste hierarchy within groups) has a large negative effect on contributions in a public goods game, while ethnic diversity alone does not. Individuals display strong in-group bias, increasing their contributions in response to co-ethnic peers but not to others. The experiment did not include punishment or reward options, and all quantitative results are about contribution levels, not payoffs or efficiency. The authors discuss antisocial punishment and discrimination as real-world mechanisms but these are not present in the experimental design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms were present in the experiment. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition (especially ethnic dominance) can affect baseline cooperation rates, which may be relevant for interpreting control group efficiency in similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the effect of enabling punishment, but it does highlight that group structure (e.g., ethnic dominance) can strongly affect baseline cooperation, which could interact with punishment effects in other studies.

- source: Shirado, H., & Christakis, N. A. (2020). Network Engineering Using Autonomous Agents Increases Cooperation in Human Groups. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing bots that facilitate the breaking of ties with defectors ('disengaged' intervention) in dynamic network public goods games leads to a substantial increase in cooperation rates among human participants, compared to controls (no bots, bots that always cooperate, or tit-for-tat bots). The effect is robust even when only a single bot is present and when humans are aware of the bots' presence. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative players per round. There is some supplementary evidence that, under the disengaged intervention, cooperators earn more than defectors, but group-level efficiency or total group payoff is not the main focus. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the network exclusion of defectors is interpreted as a form of decentralized ostracism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Instead, it provides evidence about the effect of network engineering (ostracism via tie-breaking) on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that enabling players to break ties with defectors (or having bots facilitate this) can increase cooperation and, to some extent, the earnings of cooperators. However, because efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is not directly measured or reported, and because punishment is not present, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited. It may be useful as adjacent evidence for the effects of exclusion or ostracism mechanisms.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2016). Second-order beliefs in reputation systems with endogenous evaluations - an experimental study. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated two-player public good game, providing information about both one's own and the partner's evaluation (reputation) increases contributions compared to control and to the treatment where only the partner's evaluation is shown. When only the partner's evaluation is available, contributions do not increase significantly relative to control. The effect is explained by the role of second-order beliefs in conditional cooperation. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the BOTH-INFO treatment imply higher group payoffs. There is no punishment or costly sanctioning; evaluations are costless, non-binding signals.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of reputation-based information (not costly punishment) on contribution rates in repeated two-player public good games. It suggests that simply providing information about others' evaluations is insufficient to increase cooperation, but providing both own and partner's evaluation can increase contributions. However, because the study does not include a costly punishment mechanism, its direct relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social image or reputation feedback (not material punishment) is the main intervention. For prediction tasks involving costly punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Adams, Z., Ludwiczak, A., Sharma, D., & Osman, M. (2022). Verbal interaction in a social dilemma. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the quality of communication—specifically, consensus-building language and alignment in chat—significantly predicts increases in cooperative behavior in a repeated public goods game. Groups with higher consensus scores in chat showed larger increases in contributions to the group pot in the next round. The analysis also shows that certain registers (gameshow, familial, socialist) are associated with more successful cooperation, while business and political registers are associated with less success. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that allowing chat/communication between rounds in a public goods game can increase cooperation, but the effect depends on the quality and style of the communication. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the importance of communication (chat) as a moderator of cooperation, but does not address punishment or efficiency directly. It is most useful for understanding the role of chat/communication as a game design dimension.

- source: Liu, X. L., Li, J. B., Wang, G. R., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., & Fu, X. G. (2020). Transcranial direct current stimulation of the rLPFC shifts normative judgments in voluntary cooperation. *Neuroscience Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that modulating rLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects both participants' normative judgments about appropriate contributions and their actual contributions in a one-shot, linear, asymmetric public goods game. Anodal stimulation (increasing rLPFC excitability) leads to higher normative judgments and higher contributions, while cathodal stimulation (decreasing rLPFC excitability) leads to lower judgments and contributions, relative to sham. The effect is robust across both 'rich' and 'poor' player types. No punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms are present in the game, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant to understanding the neural basis of normative judgment and voluntary cooperation, but not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, number of rounds) may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this study should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Grayson, S., Feinberg, M., Willer, R., & Zaki, J. (2025). Ironic effects of prosocial gossip in driving inaccurate social perceptions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game, participants overwhelmingly use gossip to highlight free-riders rather than cooperators, even when most group members are cooperative. When a new set of participants reads these gossip notes, they systematically underestimate the cooperativeness of the original group, becoming more cynical and less accurate in their perceptions. However, this increased cynicism does not translate into lower expectations for their own group's cooperation or reduced contributions in their own public goods game. There is some evidence that exposure to negative gossip polarizes behavior, increasing the likelihood of both full cooperation and full defection, but the average contribution is not significantly affected.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or gossip on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it provides evidence that gossip (as a reputational mechanism) is negatively skewed and can distort outsiders' perceptions of group cooperativeness, but does not affect actual contribution behavior in subsequent games. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that reputational mechanisms like gossip may increase cooperation (as shown in the referenced Feinberg et al., 2014 study), but the current paper's main contribution is to highlight the informational and perceptual side effects of such mechanisms, not their impact on efficiency.

- source: Gächter, S., Herrmann, B., & Thöni, C. (2004). Trust, voluntary cooperation, and socio-economic background: survey and experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in a one-shot public goods game (n=3, MPCR=0.5), both students and non-students contribute substantial amounts, with non-students contributing more on average. However, after controlling for socio-economic background, these differences disappear. Trust attitudes—specifically, beliefs that others are fair, helpful, and that strangers can be trusted—are significantly positively correlated with higher contributions. The widely used GSS trust question is not significantly correlated with contribution. Socio-economic background affects trust attitudes but not contribution behavior. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the analyzed experiment, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as the analyzed experiment does not include a punishment condition and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. It does, however, provide informative context about baseline contribution behavior in a standard one-shot public goods game (n=3, MPCR=0.5) with a diverse subject pool. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper can inform the expected baseline (control) contribution rates in the absence of punishment, but not the treatment effect of punishment. The paper also notes (in passing) that punishment can reduce the trust problem in repeated games, but this is only discussed, not tested.

- source: Coutts, A. (2022). Identifying communication spillovers in lab-in-the-field experiments&x2729;. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that communication spillovers between villages (i.e., when participants in one village can talk to those in a neighboring village who have already played) lead to substantial increases in contributions to the public good. The effect is robust to different matching strategies and distance thresholds. In the second round, when some villages played alternative versions of the public goods game (including punishment, reward, or risk), the positive effect of these treatments on contributions was only observed in villages not exposed to communication spillovers. The main implication is that communication between sessions can significantly increase cooperation, potentially biasing estimates of treatment effects in field experiments. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that communication (even unstructured, informal communication between sessions) can increase cooperation in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that allowing or enabling communication (or failing to prevent spillovers) may increase contributions, which could translate into higher efficiency if all other game parameters are held constant. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, and only analyzes contribution rates, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a main treatment, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of controlling for communication and spillovers when interpreting the effects of any treatment (including punishment) in field experiments.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, Y. B., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Peng, J., Wang, H. Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2020). Co-Evolution of Complex Network Public Goods Game under the Edges Rules. *Entropy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to break unsatisfactory connections and form new ones (edge rules) in a public goods game on a network leads to higher and more stable cooperation, especially at low gain factors (r=2). Both simulation and a 30-person lab experiment show that the fraction of cooperators increases and stabilizes under these rules, compared to a static network. The mechanism is interpreted as self-organized punishment (breaking ties with defectors) and reward (forming ties with cooperators), but there is no explicit costly punishment. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that endogenous network adaptation (breaking and forming ties based on satisfaction) can promote cooperation in public goods games, even at low MPCR. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency. The 'punishment' here is not a costly sanction but the breaking of ties, which is structurally different from standard punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the effects of network fluidity or endogenous partner choice, but not for predicting efficiency changes from adding explicit punishment to a fixed-group PGG.

- source: Reigstad, A. G., Stromland, E. A., & Tinghög, G. (2017). Extending the Cooperative Phenotype: Assessing the Stability of Cooperation across Countries. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals' cooperative behavior is positively correlated across different economic games (Public Goods Game, Dictator Game, Prisoner's Dilemma) and is stable over time and across countries (Norway, Sweden, Austria, United States). Punishment behavior (measured in a Dictator Game with Punishment) is not correlated with prosociality in other games. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate punishment in a PGG context, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that punishment behavior and cooperation are uncorrelated at the individual level, suggesting that the presence of punishment may not be strongly linked to prosociality. However, it does not inform the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., Puerto, S., & Velez, M. A. (2021). Regrouping to Reduce Overfishing: Evidence from a Series of Lab-in-the-Field Experiments in Mexico. *Marine Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a framed CPR lab-in-the-field experiment with real fishermen, regrouping participants by their past extraction levels (publicly ranking and then reassigning groups) leads to a significant reduction in individual and group extraction, both in the observation period (after announcement) and after regrouping. The effect is strongest for the most sustainable group but is present across all groups. Compliance with the quota increases, especially in the most sustainable group. Other interventions (social disapproval, venting, moral priming) do not reduce extraction. The control group shows no reduction in extraction. The results suggest that symbolic recognition and group reassignment can reduce overexploitation and improve compliance, indirectly improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that regrouping (a form of symbolic reward and group reassignment based on past behavior) can reduce overexploitation in CPR games, even in field-relevant settings. However, it does not test costly punishment or direct sanctioning mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of non-costly, symbolic, or reputational interventions rather than monetary punishment. The game design is close to a public goods game but is a CPR extraction game. Efficiency is not directly measured, but reductions in extraction and increased compliance imply improved group welfare. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that interventions leveraging social recognition and group identity can have positive effects on efficiency, especially when punishment is weak or absent.

- source: Stoddard, B. V., Cox, C. A., & Walker, J. M. (2021). Incentivizing provision of collective goods: Allocation rules. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: In a lab experiment with repeated collective good provision games, the introduction of a third-party allocator who can flexibly assign shares of the group fund (based on observed contributions) leads to higher group provision than a baseline with equal sharing. The two most flexible allocator mechanisms (PA-Flex and PA-Rules) both result in significantly higher provision than the baseline (PE-PE), and also outperform a less flexible allocator mechanism (PA-SWW). The highest levels of provision are observed in the most flexible mechanism (PA-Flex), though a small number of groups with ineffective allocators show low provision. The study does not directly report efficiency as a normalized payoff ratio, but group provision is closely related and higher provision implies higher group payoffs. The findings indicate that flexible allocation rules that allow for contribution-contingent rewards and punishments can strongly increase collective good provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where a third-party allocator can flexibly assign shares of the collective good based on contributions, group provision (and thus efficiency) increases substantially compared to equal sharing. The effect is robust across two flexible allocator mechanisms and is strongest when the allocator has maximal flexibility. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of introducing a flexible, contribution-contingent allocation mechanism (a form of endogenous reward/punishment), one should expect a large positive effect on group efficiency, provided the allocator uses the mechanism effectively. However, the effect may be heterogeneous if some allocators do not use the mechanism to incentivize contributions. The study does not use classic peer punishment, but the allocation mechanism serves a similar incentive function. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where a third party can allocate payoffs after observing contributions, rather than direct peer-to-peer punishment.

- source: Angelovski, A., Neugebauer, T., & Servátka, M. (2019). RANK-ORDER COMPETITION IN THE VOLUNTARY PROVISION OF IMPURE PUBLIC GOODS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Rank-Order-VCM, which rewards higher contributors with a larger share of the public good (via a higher personal multiplier), leads to significantly higher and more sustained contributions than a control treatment with random assignment of multipliers. In Rank-Order-VCM, median contributions are at the maximum in almost all rounds, while in the control, contributions decline to near zero. The mechanism avoids the efficiency loss of fixed-prize contests by not subtracting a prize from the pool. The results suggest that introducing rank-based competition for access to an impure public good can mitigate free-riding and move outcomes closer to the social optimum, increasing group welfare and implied efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based competition mechanism (where higher contributors receive a larger share of the public good) can dramatically increase contributions and implied efficiency compared to a control with random assignment of benefits. While the main reported outcomes are contributions, the payoff structure ensures that higher contributions translate into higher group payoffs and efficiency, especially since no costly prize is subtracted from the pool. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which create endogenous, contribution-dependent rewards (even without explicit punishment or costly rewards) can outperform standard VCMs and fixed-prize contests in terms of efficiency. However, the results are most directly applicable to impure public goods with preferential access, not to standard PGGs with explicit punishment or peer-sanctioning. The control efficiency (with random assignment) is much lower than in the treatment, so the predicted treatment efficiency should be substantially higher under similar design parameters.

- source: Buso, I. M., Di Cagno, D., Gueth, W., & Spadoni, L. (2025). Voluntary Partnerships for Equally Sharing Contribution Costs. *B E Journal of Theoretical Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Voluntary cost-sharing partnerships in a PGG-like environment significantly increase average contributions and prevent the typical decay of cooperation over repeated rounds. Partners contribute more than outsiders, and the presence of the partnership mechanism (whether endogenous or exogenous) leads to higher group contributions and payoffs than the standard PGG. The effect is strongest for larger partnerships. The mechanism does not involve punishment or reward, but rather a self-commitment to cost-sharing. Efficiency (in terms of group payoff relative to the social optimum) is not directly reported, but the data show that average contributions are about 50% of the endowment with partnerships, compared to about 32% in the standard PGG. The voluntary nature of partnership formation has a modest additional effect, especially for small partnerships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional mechanisms allowing voluntary cost-sharing partnerships can substantially increase group contributions and payoffs in PGG-like settings, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. For prediction tasks, the presence of such a partnership mechanism should be expected to yield higher efficiency than standard PGGs with similar MPCR and group size. The effect is robust to whether partnership formation is endogenous or exogenous, except for very small partnerships. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where cost-sharing or coalition formation is possible, but do not directly inform about punishment effects. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, other institutional mechanisms can also enhance efficiency, and that the design dimensions (player count, MPCR, endogenous/exogenous group formation) interact with the mechanism's effectiveness.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study shows that when competition between cooperators and cheats is repressed by randomizing reproductive success (RC), cooperation is strongly favored and often goes to fixation, whereas in the control treatment (competition allowed), cooperation is maintained at lower frequencies or lost. The RC treatment removes the cost of cooperation, leading to higher group productivity and a higher proportion of cooperators. Relatedness among individuals does not differ between treatments, confirming that the effect is due to RC and not kin selection. Simulations using fitness parameters from this and previous studies confirm that RC robustly promotes cooperation and group productivity across a range of parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that mechanisms which repress within-group competition (analogous to strong, perfectly effective punishment or enforced randomization of payoffs) can dramatically increase cooperation and group efficiency, even when relatedness is low. The experimental design is not a standard public goods game, but the structure is closely analogous: individuals can contribute to a costly public good (siderophores), and the group benefits from higher productivity. The RC treatment is not punishment per se, but functionally similar to a perfect sanction that eliminates the advantage of free-riding. Thus, the findings suggest that in PGG-like environments, highly effective punishment or mechanisms that eliminate the payoff advantage of defectors can lead to large increases in efficiency, especially when the control (no punishment) condition has low or intermediate efficiency. The results are most applicable to cases where punishment is highly effective and costless or nearly so.

- source: Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Marotzke, J. (2006). Stabilizing the Earth's climate is not a losing game: Supporting evidence from public goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that cooperation in a climate public goods game can be substantially increased by making contributions public (enabling reputation effects) and by providing expert information about climate change. Social rewards (support in the indirect reciprocity game) reinforce cooperative behavior, and non-cooperators are socially 'punished' by being less likely to receive support. However, the game design does not include direct monetary punishment, and the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total earnings but rather the behavioral willingness to contribute to a public good with real-world impact. The findings suggest that reputation and information can be powerful motivators for cooperation in public goods dilemmas, even when the benefits are not directly redistributed to the contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that making contributions public (enabling reputation/reward) and providing expert information can increase cooperation rates in a public goods context, even when direct monetary punishment is absent and the public good is not redistributed. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total earnings, and because the 'punishment' is social (withholding support in a separate game) rather than direct, the findings are only adjacent to the core prediction task. The results suggest that enabling reputation or reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in standard PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Chen, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Grimshaw, S. B. (2021). When a nudge is (not) enough: Experiments on social information and incentives. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In the large-N binary prisoners' dilemma, both financial incentives (reward or fine) and positive social information (majority invest) increase cooperation rates among unmotivated types. Negative social information (majority keep) reduces cooperation among motivated types. The combination of incentives and positive social information does not produce additional gains for unmotivated types, and may even crowd out intrinsic motivation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results imply that punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates, especially among those not intrinsically motivated to cooperate. The effect of social information is context-dependent: it is more effective when the externality of cooperation is salient and financially quantifiable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of punishment/reward mechanisms and social information on cooperation rates in a large-N binary prisoners' dilemma with MPCR = 0.05. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the increase in cooperation rates from punishment/reward treatments among unmotivated types suggests that enabling punishment/reward would likely increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the externality is salient. The design dimensions (large group, binary choice, no chat, no explicit payoff feedback beyond own outcome, known group size, no identification of punishers) are all specified. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment/reward increases cooperation and likely efficiency, especially among defectors.
