- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2015). Institutional Sources of Legitimate Authority: An Experimental Investigation. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that citizens are more willing to help salaried (fixed-compensation) authorities than those who personally benefit from collected penalties, but only when transparency is high. When transparency is low, support for salaried authorities collapses, while support for appropriator authorities is low regardless of transparency. Contribution rates and average earnings are reported, but do not differ significantly between treatments under full information; under limited information, contribution rates are lower for salaried authorities. The main outcome is a behavioral measure of legitimacy (citizen intervention), not efficiency or group payoff. The deterrence index (successful punishment of noncontributors minus punishment of contributors) is associated with higher contributions, but the causal effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly estimated.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how institutional features (transparency, authority incentives) affect citizen support for enforcement and, through deterrence, contribution rates. However, it does not directly estimate the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The design is a binary (all-or-nothing) public goods game with centralized, probabilistic punishment and a unique intervention stage. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of punishment (as measured by citizen support) is sensitive to transparency and authority incentives, which may in turn affect deterrence and contributions, but the direct impact on efficiency is not quantified. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative about mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct treatment effect estimates for efficiency.

- source: Harrell, A., & Traeger, M. L. (2025). Reputation-based reciprocity in human-bot and human-human networks. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The presence of bots in indirect reciprocity networks reduces human generosity, even when bots are programmed to be strong reciprocators. This reduction is driven by humans' reluctance to help bots, due to weaker norms about bots deserving help, not by a lack of reputational concern. Both 'reputational giving' (giving when observed by a third party) and 'rewarding reputations' (helping those with a reputation for helping) are less effective when bots are present. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the reduction in giving implies lower group welfare in hybrid networks compared to human-only networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of non-human agents (bots) in indirect reciprocity games reduces generosity and, by implication, group welfare. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that social context (e.g., presence of bots) can moderate the effectiveness of reciprocity-based mechanisms, but does not provide direct evidence about punishment or efficiency. The design and findings may be informative for understanding how social norms and recipient identity affect cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Stoddard, B. V., & Walker, J. M. (2019). The market for talent: Competition for resources and self-governance in teams. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Competition for a common-member's contributions creates 'winners' and 'losers': groups that start with higher contributions retain the common-member's loyalty and sustain higher contributions, while low-contribution groups decline further. Providing dedicated-members with more information about the common-member's contributions increases the common-member's total contributions and reduces discrimination between groups, but does not significantly raise dedicated-members' contributions in low-performing groups. Allowing ostracism (costless exclusion by majority vote) leads to higher and more equal contributions across groups, as both common- and dedicated-members increase contributions and the threat of exclusion disciplines low contributors. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors and rarely used against high contributors. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) and information on contribution behavior in public goods games with divided loyalties. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that ostracism (a form of endogenous, costless group-level punishment) can increase and equalize contributions across groups, especially in settings where initial disparities would otherwise persist. The results also show that information about others' contributions can increase contributions by pivotal members. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling ostracism or increasing transparency may improve group outcomes in public goods games, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Becchetti, L., Pelligra, V., & Salustri, F. (2018). The impact of redistribution mechanisms in the vote with the wallet game: experimental results. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an ex post redistribution mechanism (taxing defectors and subsidizing cooperators) in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game significantly increases the share of cooperative choices compared to the baseline. In the baseline, cooperation decays over time, but with redistribution, cooperation is stabilized at a higher level. Framing the responsible product as socially beneficial also increases cooperation, but the effect of redistribution is robust across frames. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency cannot be directly determined, but the increase in cooperation rates suggests likely higher group payoffs under redistribution.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a centralized, ex post redistribution mechanism (akin to a tax/subsidy rather than peer punishment) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game with 10 players and no communication. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the structure of the payoffs and the increase in cooperation rates imply that group efficiency would also increase under the redistribution mechanism. The findings are most directly relevant for predicting the effect of centralized, automatic sanction/reward mechanisms (not peer punishment) in all-or-nothing, no-communication, 10-player repeated games with known rounds and MPCR of 0.3. The results are less directly applicable to standard peer punishment settings, but suggest that institutionalized redistribution can be highly effective at sustaining cooperation.

- source: Cacault, M. P., Goette, L., Lalive, R., & Thoenig, M. (2015). Do we harm others even if we don't need to?. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when participants are given the option to harm an out-group (even at a cost or with no additional benefit to their own group), a significant minority choose to do so, and this behavior is often motivated by a desire to benefit the in-group at the expense of the out-group. The presence of anti-social options (aggression) increases non-selfish contributions and stabilizes in-group cooperation over repeated rounds, compared to a standard public good game where cooperation declines over time. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the increased cooperation offsets the welfare losses from aggression. The findings are relevant for understanding the behavioral complementarity between in-group cooperation and out-group aggression, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the availability of anti-social options (aggression) can increase in-group cooperation and non-selfish contributions in a public good game variant, especially in later rounds. However, because the anti-social options reduce the payoffs of out-groups and the paper does not report group efficiency or total earnings, it is not possible to determine whether the net effect on efficiency is positive or negative. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that behavioral responses to punishment-like options can increase cooperation, but may also introduce welfare-reducing behaviors. The design is adjacent to standard punishment mechanisms (since aggression is not used to enforce norms but to harm others), so caution is needed in transferring these findings to standard PGG punishment settings.

- source: McIntosh, C., Sadoulet, E., Buck, S., & Rosada, T. (2013). Reputation in a public goods game: Taking the design of credit bureaus to the lab. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms (used for reassigning ejected players) increase average contributions in a repeated public goods game with ejection, compared to a random reassignment baseline. The effect size is about a 7.5% increase in average contribution (from ~75% to ~82% of the endowment). There is no significant difference in average contribution between group and individual information treatments. Ejection is more sensitive to individual contribution in the group information treatment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the contribution data can be mapped to efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly reduction of others' payoffs), but rather exclusion from play. The findings suggest that group-based reputation is at least as effective as individual-based reputation in sustaining cooperation, and that transitioning microfinance credit bureaus from group to individual reporting may not yield substantial gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with ejection (exclusion) as the sanction mechanism, both group-level and individual-level reputation mechanisms increase average contributions compared to random reassignment. The effect is similar for both information types. While the study does not report efficiency directly, the increase in average contribution implies a corresponding increase in efficiency. The ejection mechanism is not a standard punishment technology (no direct costly punishment), so the results are most relevant for environments where exclusion or ostracism is the main sanction. The findings suggest that, for games with endogenous group selection and ejection, introducing a reputation-based reassignment mechanism (either group or individual) can improve cooperation and implied efficiency, but the marginal benefit of individual-level over group-level information is minimal. The results are less directly informative for standard PGGs with costly punishment, but may be useful for predicting the effect of exclusionary sanctions.

- source: Secchi, S., & Banerjee, S. (2019). A dynamic semester-long social dilemma game for economic and interdisciplinary courses. *Journal of Economic Education*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that students, when given the opportunity, may devise and implement their own institutional solutions to the common-pool resource dilemma, including costly peer punishment. The game is effective for teaching about the dynamics of cooperation, cheating, and institutional innovation in resource management. However, the paper does not provide quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically compare outcomes with and without punishment. The main findings are qualitative and pedagogical, emphasizing the diversity of student-devised solutions and the context-dependence of group behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it confirms that peer punishment can emerge and is sometimes used by participants to address over-extraction, it does not provide quantitative or systematic evidence on its impact on efficiency or group payoff. The game design dimensions are described in detail, but the effect of punishment is only discussed anecdotally. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that allowing for endogenous punishment mechanisms can lead to creative solutions and potentially improved group outcomes, but does not provide data to calibrate the size or direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: D'Exelle, B., & van den Berg, M. (2014). AID DISTRIBUTION AND COOPERATION IN UNEQUAL COMMUNITIES. *Review of Income and Wealth*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a representative who allocates aid (selected as the highest contributor) increases group contributions compared to equal sharing, due to both competition for the representative role and anticipation of punishment (i.e., receiving less aid for low contributions). However, representatives keep a large share of the aid for themselves, so the increased total aid does not translate into higher payoffs for the rest of the group. Representatives do consider fairness, giving more to low-endowment and higher-contributing members, but still capture more than their proportional share. Endowment inequality increases contributions under equal sharing but not under representative sharing. The study does not report group efficiency or total group payoff as a ratio to the social optimum, so the effect of the representative mechanism on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, representative allocation with the ability to reward or punish via aid shares) on group contributions and the distribution of payoffs. The representative mechanism increases contributions (a proxy for cooperation), but the efficiency gain for the group as a whole is ambiguous because the representative captures a disproportionate share of the aid. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency (total group payoff relative to the maximum), this study suggests that mechanisms allowing targeted allocation can increase contributions, but may not improve average group payoffs for all members unless the allocation is constrained. The absence of direct efficiency analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shirado, H., Fu, F., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Quality versus quantity of social ties in experimental cooperative networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing dynamic partner selection in a networked cooperation game leads to the highest levels of cooperation at intermediate rates of network rewiring. At low rewiring rates, players accumulate many ties, including to defectors, which undermines cooperation. At very high rewiring rates, defectors can re-attach to cooperators faster than cooperators can detach, also undermining cooperation. The ability to break ties with defectors (interpreted as a form of social punishment) helps maintain cooperation, but only when the rate of change is not too high or too low. The main outcome is the cooperation rate; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of dynamic partner selection (ostracism-like social punishment) on cooperation in networked public goods games. It suggests that enabling players to break ties with defectors can sustain cooperation, but only at intermediate rates of network change. However, because the study does not include explicit costly punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social exclusion or partner choice is the main sanctioning mechanism, rather than costly punishment. The results suggest that the design dimension of network dynamism (rewiring rate) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation, which may translate to efficiency if payoff outcomes were measured.

- source: Gomez-Ruiz, L., & Sánchez-Expósito, M. J. (2020). The Impact of Team Identity and Gender on Free-Riding Responses to Fear and Cooperation Sustainability. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that strong team identity significantly reduces free-riding responses to fear and increases cooperation rates among men, but not among women. The effect of team identity on cooperation sustainability (maintenance of cooperation over time) is marginally significant for men and not significant for women. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. No formal punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; the study focuses on informal control (team identity) and gender differences. The findings suggest that team identity can be an effective informal mechanism to sustain cooperation among men in repeated social dilemmas with binary contribution choices and feedback, but not among women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It studies a repeated binary-choice social dilemma (close to a public goods game) without any punishment or reward mechanism. The main manipulations are team identity and gender. The outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does provide contextual evidence about the role of informal mechanisms (team identity) and gender in sustaining cooperation. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in binary-choice, feedback-rich, no-punishment settings, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., & Christakis, N. A. (2011). Dynamic social networks promote cooperation in experiments with humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in large groups playing a repeated networked cooperation game, cooperation decays over time in static, random, or slowly updating networks, but is stably maintained when players can frequently update their social ties. Players preferentially break links with defectors and form links with cooperators, which disciplines defectors and incentivizes cooperation. This dynamic network updating acts as a decentralized ostracism mechanism, supporting cooperation without explicit costly punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are most directly about behavioral cooperation, not payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that dynamic, fluid social networks can sustain high cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, even in large groups. However, it does not directly address the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (costly punishment) on efficiency or payoff. Instead, it studies a form of endogenous, non-costly ostracism (link breaking), which is adjacent to but not the same as standard punishment in public goods games. For prediction tasks about the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-costly, decentralized exclusion can promote cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or about the effect of costly punishment. The game design dimensions are partially informative (e.g., player count, network updating rules), but the absence of explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes and standard punishment limits direct applicability.

- source: Tan, J. H. W., & Bolle, F. (2023). Intragroup punishment and intergroup conflict aversion weaken intragroup cooperation in finitely repeated games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that intragroup cooperation is sensitive to the presence of intergroup rivalry and the payoff structure. Cooperation is lower when it inflicts losses on rivals, even when socially efficient, and higher when synergy within the group is increased. Participants use dynamic strategies, such as punishing partner defection with unyielding defection, but do not respond similarly to rival actions. Outgroup altruism (conflict aversion) further reduces cooperation that would harm rivals. Cooperation rates decline over time due to these mechanisms. The study does not include a costly punishment stage or analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' discussed is behavioral (trigger strategies, unyielding defection) rather than a costly punishment mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how dynamic strategies and group-specific preferences affect cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to the introduction of a punishment option. The game structure is close to a PGG, but the absence of a costly punishment treatment and lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: De Luca, G., Sekeris, P. G., & Spengler, D. E. (2018). Can violence harm cooperation? Experimental evidence. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments: a 'conflict' treatment where violent appropriation (chance) is a viable option when resources are low, and a 'control' treatment where the cost of appropriation is so high that it is never optimal. In the conflict treatment, participants are significantly less likely to choose the cooperative extraction rate ('low') and more likely to choose the non-cooperative rate ('high') in early rounds, anticipating future conflict. The expectation of future conflict thus undermines cooperation even before conflict occurs. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral shift from cooperation to non-cooperation implies lower efficiency in the conflict treatment. Additional analyses show that experiencing conflict in previous games further reduces cooperation in subsequent games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a credible, destructive appropriation/conflict option in a dynamic CPR game reduces cooperative behavior and likely reduces efficiency, even before conflict is actually chosen. For prediction tasks, the presence of a credible destructive sanction (even if not a standard punishment mechanism) should be expected to reduce efficiency relative to a control with no such option. However, the paper does not study standard punishment (costly peer punishment for norm violations) but rather a destructive appropriation/conflict option, so the results are most relevant for settings where punishment is destructive and ends the game, not for standard public goods games with ongoing punishment. The findings suggest that the threat of destructive sanctions can unravel cooperation and efficiency, especially when the sanction is credible and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Torul, O., & Vostroknutov, A. (2020). Norm compliance, enforcement, and the survival of redistributive institutions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in the absence of enforcement, contributions to redistribution decay rapidly due to free riding, even among individuals with a high propensity to follow rules. Exclusion (random checks with exclusion for non-compliance) slows the decay but does not prevent it. Only full enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) sustains high contributions and reduces post-tax income inequality. Rule-following individuals initially try to follow the rules but eventually reduce contributions in response to free riding. Rule-followers prefer institutions with strong rules when enforcement is present, but switch to unregulated institutions when enforcement is absent and free riding is prevalent. The paper reports post-tax income and Gini coefficients, but does not directly analyze efficiency as group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum. The findings suggest that some form of enforcement is necessary to sustain redistribution and prevent the collapse of cooperative institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enforcement (a form of exogenous punishment) on group outcomes in public-goods-like environments. While it does not implement a classic costly punishment mechanism, it shows that enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) is necessary to sustain high contributions and reduce inequality; exclusion as a sanction slows but does not prevent decay. In the absence of enforcement, contributions and redistribution collapse. The results suggest that, in similar designs, enabling effective enforcement or punishment is likely to increase group welfare and reduce inequality, but the paper does not report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark. The findings are most informative for settings where enforcement is exogenous and not costly to the enforcer. The evidence is less directly applicable to classic peer punishment with explicit costs. Control efficiency (without enforcement) is low due to free riding; treatment efficiency (with enforcement) is likely much higher, but the exact ratio is not reported.

- source: Mitterbacher, K., Palan, S., & Fleiss, J. (2024). Intergroup cooperation in the lab: asymmetric power relations and redistributive policies. *Empirica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in an asymmetric intergroup setting, the threat or implementation of discriminatory redistribution (a form of institutionalized sanction) reduces cooperation by the disadvantaged group (migrants). When citizens choose equal sharing, migrants are more likely to contribute, and when migrants contribute, citizens are less likely to vote for discrimination. This reciprocity can lead to mutually beneficial cooperation, but discrimination or lack of cooperation can trigger downward spirals. The mere possibility of discrimination reduces cooperation compared to a baseline where only equal sharing is possible. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution and voting rates), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, discriminatory redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like games with asymmetric power. The findings suggest that institutionalized sanctions (discriminatory redistribution) can reduce cooperation and thus would likely reduce efficiency, especially when the disadvantaged group perceives the system as unfair. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The design and behavioral results are informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions may backfire in asymmetric settings, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Yoeli, E., Hoffman, M., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Powering up with indirect reciprocity in a large-scale field experiment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that making participation in a voluntary demand response program observable to neighbors nearly triples participation rates compared to an anonymous condition. The effect of observability is much larger than that of a $25 monetary incentive. The effect is strongest in contexts where reputational concerns are likely to matter more (e.g., apartment buildings, homeowners vs. renters, and when the program is framed as a public good). The results support the theory that indirect reciprocity and reputational incentives can drive large-scale cooperation in real-world public goods dilemmas. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing observability (reputational incentives) can dramatically increase participation in a real-world public goods context, especially when social ties and public good framing are salient. However, because the outcome is participation rate (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. It does, however, suggest that non-monetary, reputational incentives can be more effective than small monetary incentives in increasing cooperation.

- source: De Cremer, D., & van Dijk, E. (2009). Paying for sanctions in social dilemmas: The effects of endowment asymmetry and accountability. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in a step-level public good dilemma, high-endowment members contribute proportionally more to the public good than low-endowment members, regardless of accountability. However, when it comes to paying for a sanctioning system (which is framed as a second-order, continuous public good dilemma without a threshold), high-endowment members do not contribute more than low-endowment members unless they are made accountable and expect to be morally evaluated by the group. Accountability and evaluation expectancy increase contributions to the sanctioning system among high-endowment members, but these effects are not observed for contributions to the public good itself. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so the effect of these manipulations on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the psychological and social moderators of willingness to pay for punishment systems in public good dilemmas, especially under endowment asymmetry. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that accountability and evaluation expectancy can increase willingness to pay for sanctions among high-endowment members, but the translation of this effect to efficiency or group payoff is not established. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about the behavioral mechanisms that might affect the implementation of punishment systems.

- source: Lokhorst, A. M., van Dijk, E., & Staats, H. (2009). Public commitment making as a structural solution in social dilemmas. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that both dispositional trust and situational expectations interact to predict willingness to invest in a public commitment system in a public good dilemma. High trusters are more willing to install a commitment system when situational expectations are low, while low trusters are more willing when situational expectations are high. The motive to maximize collective outcomes partially mediates this effect. However, the study does not measure actual contributions, group payoff, or efficiency, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of commitment systems (or punishment) on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding when participants are likely to support or invest in structural solutions (like public commitment systems), but does not report on actual efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be useful for understanding the adoption of commitment or sanctioning systems, but not for predicting their efficiency effects.

- source: Mulder, L. B., & Nelissen, R. M. A. (2010). When Rules Really Make a Difference: The Effect of Cooperation Rules and Self-Sacrificing Leadership on Moral Norms in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that installing a cooperation rule increases personal moral norms and cooperative behavior (less resource extraction, shorter breaks, fewer lottery tickets taken) only when the rule is installed by a self-sacrificing leader. If the leader is self-benefiting, rules only increase cooperation when behavior is monitored (i.e., when there is supervision). The effect of rules on cooperation is mediated by the instigation of moral norms, but only for self-sacrificing leaders. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are limited to behavioral and attitudinal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of rules (and by extension, possibly punishment) in increasing cooperation depends on the perceived legitimacy and self-sacrifice of the authority figure, and on the presence of monitoring. However, because the studies do not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results suggest that rules (and possibly punishment) may be more effective in increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) when installed by a self-sacrificing leader or when monitoring is present, but this is not tested in terms of payoff or efficiency. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of rules, monitoring (supervision), and leader characteristics, but not punishment per se.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Third-party punishment and social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that third-party punishment is common and increases with the severity of norm violations in both dictator games (distribution norm) and prisoner's dilemma games (cooperation norm). However, the magnitude of third-party punishment is lower than that of second-party punishment. In dictator games, third-party punishment reduces but does not eliminate the profitability of unfair transfers, while second-party punishment makes unfair transfers unprofitable. In prisoner's dilemma games, third-party punishment reduces the gains from defection but does not make cooperation strictly dominant, whereas second-party punishment does. Thus, punishment mechanisms—especially when strong or when multiple punishers are present—can increase efficiency by reducing the incentive to violate norms, but the effect is stronger for second-party punishment. The paper does not directly report group efficiency as a percentage of the maximum, but provides enough data to infer that punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes by reducing the profitability of norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (especially second-party, but also third-party) increases the cost of norm violations and can shift incentives toward more efficient, norm-compliant outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with sufficient strength or multiple punishers) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to control conditions without punishment, but the effect size depends on the punishment's strength and whether it is second- or third-party. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the punishment cost/impact ratio, the number of punishers, and whether punishment is second- or third-party. The results are most directly applicable to dictator and prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to but not exact public goods games. The paper does not provide direct evidence for standard PGGs, but the mechanisms and patterns are likely to generalize to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2003). Detrimental effects of sanctions on human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a one-shot sequential trust game, allowing the investor to impose a fine on the trustee for insufficient back-transfer leads to lower back-transfers from trustees and lower payoffs for investors, compared to both a baseline with no sanctions and to cases where the investor refrains from using the fine. The negative effect of sanctions is especially pronounced when the investor's desired back-transfer is high (i.e., when the sanction is used to enforce an unfair distribution). The paper interprets this as evidence that sanctions perceived as self-interested or unfair undermine altruistic cooperation, and thus can reduce overall efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a sequential trust game (not a standard PGG), enabling punishment (sanctions) can reduce efficiency-related outcomes (investor payoff, group surplus) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is perceived as self-interested or unfair. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: in trust games with one-shot interactions and potential for perceived unfairness, punishment may reduce efficiency. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGGs, but highlight the importance of the perceived legitimacy and purpose of punishment as a moderator.

- source: Bohnet, I., & Baytelman, Y. (2007). Institutions and trust - Implications for preferences, beliefs and behavior. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional constraints, including a post-play punishment option, increase trustors' willingness to send money and trustees' willingness to return money in a trust game scenario, compared to an anonymous one-shot game. This suggests that efficiency (total group payoff) is higher with punishment. However, the presence of punishment and other institutional constraints reduces the role of intrinsic motivation (social orientation) in driving trust, supporting the 'crowding out' hypothesis. For trustworthiness, the effect of punishment is indirect: trustees return more because they expect to receive more, not because their intrinsic motivation changes. The study uses a within-subject design with senior executives and hypothetical payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that adding a punishment option to a trust game increases the amounts sent and returned, which is a proxy for increased efficiency. While the design is not a public goods game, the trust game with punishment is adjacent and the direction of effect is likely to generalize to PGG-like settings: punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes. However, the paper also highlights that the mechanism is not an increase in intrinsic motivation, but rather a shift in beliefs and expectations. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in a similar game design will increase efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low. The effect is robust across several institutional manipulations, but the crowding out of intrinsic motivation may have long-term implications not captured by short-run efficiency.

- source: Friebel, G., Kosfeld, M., & Thielmann, G. (2019). Trust the Police? Self-Selection of Motivated Agents into the German Police Force. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Police applicants in Germany are more trustworthy (return more in the trust game) and invest more in costly third-party punishment and reward than comparable high school students. The difference in trust is explained by risk preferences, but the difference in trustworthiness and norm enforcement remains significant after controlling for covariates. The willingness to punish and reward is measured as the average amount invested in these actions as a third party in a trust-game-based scenario. The study provides evidence for positive self-selection of intrinsically motivated, norm-enforcing individuals into the police force. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individuals who self-select into enforcement roles (police applicants) are more willing to engage in costly punishment and reward to enforce cooperation norms. However, the experimental design is not a public goods game or a repeated social dilemma, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the motivation to punish and enforce norms, but do not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect evidence about the types of individuals who may be more likely to use punishment, rather than as evidence about the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Zhao, K., Ferguson, E., & Smillie, L. D. (2017). Politeness and Compassion Differentially Predict Adherence to Fairness Norms and Interventions to Norm Violations in Economic Games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that politeness and honesty-humility predict fair allocations in the dictator game, while compassion predicts third-party recompensation (helping the victim) and, to a lesser extent, third-party punishment (punishing the offender). Most third-party punishers are 'prosocial' (they also behave fairly as dictators), and both punishment and recompensation increase with the degree of unfairness observed. However, the study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the game structure is not a public goods game but a dictator/third-party punishment/recompensation design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it offers insight into the psychological predictors of punishment and recompensation behaviors in third-party games, it does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze public goods game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or rounds. Its findings are only indirectly relevant for understanding the motivations behind punishment and helping behaviors, not their impact on group efficiency.

- source: Fiedler, M., & Haruvy, E. (2017). The effect of third party intervention in the trust game. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a trust game, third party monitoring increases investment and return rates compared to no third party. Adding punishment or reward capabilities to the third party does not significantly increase aggregate cooperation or efficiency beyond monitoring alone, especially in random partner settings. In fixed partner settings, punishment may have a somewhat greater effect, but the difference is not large. Individual players are responsive to punishment and reward, but the overall aggregate effect is muted because the actual use of punishment/reward is infrequent and the absence of punishment/reward can itself act as reinforcement. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but the design allows for inference about total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in trust game environments (which are adjacent to public goods games), third party punishment does not substantially increase efficiency or aggregate payoff beyond what is achieved by third party monitoring alone, especially when partners are randomly matched. The effect of punishment is more pronounced in fixed partner settings, but still not dramatically higher than monitoring. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar trust/investment games, enabling punishment may not yield large efficiency gains over monitoring, particularly with random matching. However, the results may not transfer directly to public goods games, and efficiency is not directly measured.

- source: Pérez, R. L., & Kisst, H. J. (2012). Do People Accurately Anticipate Sanctions?. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that subjects are generally able to anticipate the sign and strength of sanctions (punishment/reward) in two-player allocation games, with moderate errors. Punishment is better anticipated than reward, and nonmonetary sanctions are anticipated with smaller errors than monetary ones. Systematic underestimation occurs mainly where rewards are prevalent. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the games are not public goods games or close variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The games studied are two-player allocation games with a sanctioning stage, not multi-player public goods games. The main outcomes are about belief accuracy, not efficiency or payoff. While the findings suggest that punishment is more salient and better anticipated than reward, and that errors in expectation are moderate, these insights are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The paper may inform mechanism design regarding the salience and predictability of sanctions, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). IS PUNISHMENT EFFECTIVE - COERCIVE STRATEGIES IN SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is effective in increasing compliance (frequency of rewarding the punisher) when it is used frequently, contingently, and with high magnitude. The combination of high probability and high magnitude of punishment produces the highest compliance. Retaliation by the target does not increase with more frequent or stronger punishment, and negative affect toward the punisher is actually lower when punishment is strong and consistent, due to the resulting mutually rewarding exchanges. The effects are robust to the target's ability to retaliate and to variations in the probability and contingency of punishment. However, the main outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, but the frequency of compliance in a dyadic exchange. The results challenge classical exchange and bargaining theories that predict punishment provokes retaliation and reduces cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dyadic reciprocal exchange settings with both reward and punishment options, frequent and contingent punishment increases compliance (rewarding behavior) by the target, without increasing retaliation or negative affect. However, because the primary outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, and the setting is not a public goods game or direct variant, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like environments with caution. The results suggest that, under conditions of high punishment frequency, contingency, and magnitude, punishment can be effective at increasing the target's prosocial behavior, but the implications for group efficiency in multi-person public goods games remain indirect. The paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment rather than direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Rauhut, H. (2009). HIGHER PUNISHMENT, LESS CONTROL? EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE ON THE INSPECTION GAME. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the strength of punishment reduces both the rate of inspection (control) and the rate of theft (crime), contrary to the strict game-theoretic prediction that only inspection should decrease. The effect on inspection is strong and robust, while the effect on theft is also significant but not as strong as predicted by standard theory. The behavioral adaptation to changes in punishment is slower than predicted by rational learning models. The main outcomes are theft and inspection rates; efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured, but the design ensures that theft is inefficient (gamma = 0.5). Inspectees lose money over time, while inspectors' earnings are roughly stable. The findings suggest that punishment has mixed effects on strategic behavior in inspection settings, and that human adaptation is slower and less complete than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the behavioral effects of punishment in an inspection game with inefficient theft, which is structurally adjacent to public goods games. The main outcomes are theft and inspection rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, since theft is inefficient (gamma < 1), reductions in theft due to higher punishment would increase group efficiency, while reductions in inspection (which is costly) could also increase efficiency if inspection is not needed. The paper shows that higher punishment reduces both theft and inspection, suggesting that the net effect on efficiency would depend on the relative magnitudes of these changes and the cost structure. The design and findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is formal, inspection is costly, and theft is inefficient. The lack of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions, but the qualitative direction of effects (higher punishment reduces both crime and control) is informative for adjacent settings.

- source: SCHMITT, D. R., & MARWELL, G. (1970). REWARD AND PUNISHMENT AS INFLUENCE TECHNIQUES FOR ACHIEVEMENT OF COOPERATION UNDER INEQUITY. *Human Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dyadic task with unequal cooperative payoffs, the ability to reward (transfer money) is much more effective than the ability to punish (subtract money) in inducing and sustaining high rates of cooperation. Groups where the reward strategy was used spent more than twice as much time cooperating compared to those using punishment or no strategy. Communication between subjects further increased the use of rewards and the likelihood of achieving steady-state cooperation, particularly among males. Punishment, when used alone, was generally ineffective in producing cooperation, and its use declined over time. The study does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings, but the behavioral outcomes suggest that reward-based influence is more conducive to sustained cooperation than punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment and reward on cooperation in a setting adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment alone is generally ineffective at inducing sustained cooperation, while reward (and especially the combination of reward and communication) is much more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is dyadic, with asymmetric payoffs and the ability for only one player to reward or punish, which differs from standard PGGs. Thus, the main value for prediction is as a caution that punishment may not always increase cooperation or efficiency, especially when reward is also available and more salient.

- source: Batistoni, T., Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. J. (2022). Third-party punishers do not compete to be chosen as partners in an experimental game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and helping are both used as signals of cooperative intent, but helping is a stronger and less ambiguous signal than punishment. Observability increases investments in both, but the possibility of partner choice does not further escalate investments. Higher investments in punishment or helping increase the likelihood of being chosen as a partner and being trusted, but this effect is much stronger for helping. Investments in both are positively associated with actual trustworthiness in a Trust Game. The study does not address group efficiency or total payoff in a public goods game, but provides evidence on the reputational and signaling functions of punishment and helping.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a Dictator Game with third-party punishment/helping and a subsequent Trust Game. The main outcomes are individual-level signaling, partner choice, and trust/trustworthiness, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment is a weaker and more ambiguous signal of cooperative intent than helping, and that reputational incentives may increase costly punishment/helping, but do not escalate under partner choice. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the social and reputational motivations for punishment, not about its effect on group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., Kurzban, R., & McCullough, M. E. (2013). Do humans really punish altruistically? A closer look. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a lab setting where third-party punishment is possible but demand characteristics and audience effects are minimized, witnesses of unfairness toward others rarely engage in costly punishment. When they do, it is not motivated by moralistic anger but by envy. Victims of unfairness (those personally harmed) do punish at nontrivial rates. In hypothetical scenarios, people forecast much more punishment and anger than is observed in real behavior. The results challenge the robustness of altruistic third-party punishment in humans and suggest that previous findings may be due to experimental artefacts. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dictator/third-party punishment games where the punisher is not personally harmed, actual costly punishment is rare and mild, and not motivated by norm-enforcing anger. This suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff, since punishment is rarely used and not directed at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, and the findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is only available to third parties who are not personally affected. The results caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in such designs.

- source: Konishi, N., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2015). Does dishonesty really invite third-party punishment? Results of a more stringent test. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, even after removing five key methodological confounds (including adding a reward option and ensuring anonymity), a significant minority of third-party observers are willing to incur a personal cost to punish a dishonest trustee, even when the trustee's allocation is fair. The rate of punishment is lower than in previous studies (27% vs. 53%), but still significantly higher in the dishonesty condition than in the honesty condition. Participants were more likely to punish than reward in response to dishonesty. The main outcomes are the frequency and cost of punishment/reward, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that third-party punishment of norm violations (specifically dishonesty) can occur even when methodological confounds are controlled. However, the design is a modified trust game with third-party punishment and reward, not a public goods game or direct group efficiency context. The outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates and costs), not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the paper supports the robustness of third-party punishment as a behavioral phenomenon, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment can persist even when alternative explanations are minimized, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on group efficiency.

- source: Li, L. Y., Zhao, X. B., Xie, D., & Xiao, X. (2024). On difference between direct-response method and strategy method in decision-making: behavioural and neural evidence in a reward-punishment game. *Journal of the Operational Research Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that the elicitation method (direct-response vs. strategy) significantly affects the rates of reward and punishment in a repeated two-player reward-punishment game. Specifically, the strategy method leads to higher rates of both reward and punishment. Behavioral modeling and neural evidence suggest that the ownership effect (overweighting of cost) suppresses reward in the direct-response method, while counterfactual thinking (comparison of possible outcomes) increases punishment in the strategy method. Strategic thinking is present in both methods but does not explain the difference between them. The study does not report efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the game structure is not a public goods game or a direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the method of eliciting punishment and reward decisions (direct-response vs. strategy) can substantially affect the frequency of punishment and reward, with the strategy method increasing both. However, it does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The game is a two-player repeated reward-punishment game, not a public goods game or close variant, and the main outcomes are behavioral rates, not payoffs. The findings may be relevant for understanding how elicitation methods could moderate punishment effects, but the lack of efficiency data and the different game structure limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Whitson, J. A., Wang, C. S., See, Y. H. M., Baker, W. E., & Murnighan, J. K. (2015). How, when, and why recipients and observers reward good deeds and punish bad deeds. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party observers are more likely to punish bad deeds (e.g., deception) than direct recipients, and that direct recipients are more likely to reward good deeds (e.g., honesty) than observers. These effects are mediated by feelings of obligation: observers feel a stronger obligation to punish, while recipients feel a stronger obligation to reward. The studies use one-shot, controlled scenarios with real monetary stakes, but do not involve repeated interaction, group provision, or public goods. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward assigned), not group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in one-shot, dyadic or triadic settings, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that the presence of observers may increase the likelihood and intensity of punishment for norm violations, but do not inform how this affects group payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about who is likely to punish and under what circumstances, not about the payoff consequences of punishment.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., & López-Pérez, R. (2014). Different carrots and different sticks: do we reward and punish differently than we approve and disapprove?. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that monetary punishment is used more often and more intensely than non-monetary disapproval, but monetary reward and non-monetary approval are used at similar rates. The determinants of monetary punishment/reward are primarily payoff-based (inequity aversion), while non-monetary approval/disapproval is more strongly driven by reciprocity and norm-based considerations. Strict payoff equality reduces disapproval and increases approval. The experiment is not a repeated public goods game but a set of one-shot, two-player allocation games, so efficiency or group payoff outcomes are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and motivational underpinnings of punishment and reward, distinguishing between monetary and non-monetary forms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes in a repeated public goods context, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Its main value is in clarifying that the impact of punishment/reward on behavior may depend on whether the mechanism is monetary or non-monetary, and that motivations differ. For direct prediction of efficiency effects in PGGs, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Power Asymmetries and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with Variable Cooperative Investment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with variable cooperative investment and a punishment option, punishment did not increase the likelihood that targets would increase their cooperation, regardless of whether the punisher was strong or weak, or whether the game was symmetric or asymmetric. Both weak and strong players retaliated against punishment, and the threat of punishment from strong players may have led to higher baseline cooperation from weak players, but actual punishment had no effect on subsequent cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player games with variable investment and a punishment option, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency, even when punishment is more effective for some players (power asymmetry). However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar two-player designs, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may provoke retaliation. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs (iterated PD with variable investment) and may be less transferable to standard public goods games or multiplayer settings.

- source: Martin, J. W., & Cushman, F. (2015). To Punish or to Leave: Distinct Cognitive Processes Underlie Partner Control and Partner Choice Behaviors. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dyadic allocation game with stochastic outcomes, punishment (partner control) is influenced by both the allocator's intent and the actual outcome, while partner choice (switching partners) is influenced almost exclusively by intent. The design allows for the separation of intent and outcome by having allocators choose between dice with different probabilities of fair/selfish outcomes. Responders in the punishment condition could add or subtract money (costlessly, with low probability of effect), while in the partner choice condition they could choose to stay or switch partners. The main dependent variables are the behavioral responses and their sensitivity to intent/outcome. No group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological determinants of punishment and partner choice behaviors, showing that punishment is sensitive to both intent and outcome, while partner choice is intent-driven. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may inform understanding of when punishment is likely to be used and what cues drive it.

- source: Andreoni, J., Harbaugh, W., & Vesterlund, L. (2003). The carrot or the stick: Rewards, punishments, and cooperation. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a two-person proposer-responder game, the ability to punish (reduce the proposer's payoff at a cost) increases the amount proposers share with responders, compared to a baseline with no punishment or reward. The ability to reward (increase the proposer's payoff at a cost) alone has little effect on the modal offer, though it increases variance and leads a minority to be very generous. When both punishment and reward are available, proposers make the most generous offers, and the distribution of offers first-order stochastically dominates the other treatments.
