Punishment and reward act as complements: punishment moves people away from selfishness, and reward encourages further cooperation. The study does not use a standard public goods game, but the findings are relevant for understanding how punishment and reward options affect cooperative behavior and, by implication, group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-person proposer-responder environment, enabling punishment increases cooperative behavior (higher offers), and that combining punishment and reward is even more effective. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings suggest that, for similar environments, adding punishment (and especially both punishment and reward) will increase efficiency relative to a baseline with neither. The effect of reward alone is weaker. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, one-shot or randomly rematched games with costly punishment and reward options. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that punishment (especially when combined with reward) will increase efficiency or group payoff, even if the effect of reward alone is limited.

- source: Gneezy, A., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2012). Conflict, sticks and carrots: war increases prosocial punishments and rewards. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of violent intergroup conflict, individuals are more willing to incur costs to punish non-cooperative group members (as measured by higher rejection rates of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game) and to reward cooperative group members (as measured by higher returns in the Trust Game when the first mover is generous). These effects are observed by comparing behavior before, during, and after the 2006 Israel-Hezbollah war among Israeli senior citizens. The findings support the hypothesis that intergroup conflict elevates sanctioning behaviors that promote within-group cooperation. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on the behavioral propensity to punish and reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that real-world intergroup conflict increases individuals' willingness to punish non-cooperators and reward cooperators in economic games structurally related to public goods dilemmas. However, because the games used (Ultimatum and Trust Games) are not public goods games and the outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results suggest that contextual factors (such as intergroup conflict) can increase sanctioning behaviors, which, in public goods games, are often associated with higher cooperation and sometimes higher efficiency, but this link is not directly tested here. The study does not manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task (e.g., MPCR, player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.), so its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes is limited.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2024). Time pressure and deliberation affect moral punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main findings are that (1) time pressure reduces the probability of moral punishment, (2) deliberation increases the probability of moral punishment, and (3) the content of deliberation matters: deliberating fairness increases, while deliberating self-interest decreases, moral punishment. These effects are specific to moral punishment and do not consistently affect other types of punishment or cooperation. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct impact of these manipulations on group efficiency is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how cognitive constraints and deliberation affect the likelihood of moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that manipulations increasing deliberation (e.g., removing time pressure, encouraging fairness-based reflection) are likely to increase the use of moral punishment, which in other literature is often associated with higher cooperation and potentially higher efficiency. However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's findings should be used as indirect evidence about the behavioral mechanisms (punishment rates) rather than direct evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2024). People punish defection, not failures to conform to the majority. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that people are more likely to cooperate and to punish defectors (moral punishment) when the majority of partners are cooperative. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is much less common and does not increase when defection is the majority behavior, contradicting a pure conformity account. The presence of partner punishment (Experiment 1) increases cooperation rates above the base rate, while its absence (Experiment 2) leads to cooperation rates more closely matching the environment. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but the final account balances suggest that punishment and cooperation patterns affect individual earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how punishment is used in dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma games, showing that punishment is primarily directed at defectors and is sensitive to the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not directly report or analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a repeated, one-shot, dyadic PD with a costly punishment option, not a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is well specified (costly, with a fixed cost-to-impact ratio). The findings suggest that enabling punishment increases cooperation, especially when the environment is cooperative, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction, this paper supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and likely efficiency in similar dyadic games, but caution is needed in extrapolating to multi-player PGGs.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Cognitive load decreases cooperation and moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that cognitive load reduces both cooperation and moral punishment in a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment. Hypocritical and antisocial punishment are not significantly affected by cognitive load, but a general punishment bias (punishing regardless of partner behavior) increases under cognitive load. The amount spent on punishment, when punishment occurs, does not differ between conditions. The results suggest that both cooperation and moral punishment require cognitive resources and are not purely intuitive. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a two-player one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, the presence of punishment (and its purposeful use) depends on cognitive resources. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding how cognitive load moderates the behavioral use of punishment and cooperation, not their impact on group efficiency. The game is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, so transfer to PGG settings should be cautious.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Sokol-Hessner, P., Van Bavel, J. J., & Phelps, E. A. (2014). Fairness violations elicit greater punishment on behalf of another than for oneself. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that, contrary to the assumption that punishment is the default response to fairness violations, individuals strongly prefer compensation over punishment when both options are available. This preference holds even when punishment is costless. However, when participants make decisions on behalf of others (third-party perspective), they are more likely to choose punitive options that both compensate the victim and punish the transgressor. The findings suggest that the context and available options significantly shape justice-restoration preferences. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its findings are not directly usable for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments where participants can choose between punishment and non-punitive justice-restoration options, punishment is not the preferred response to unfairness. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game. Therefore, while the findings suggest that the presence of alternative options may reduce the use of punishment, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in public goods or similar games. Its main value is in highlighting that behavioral responses to punishment options are context-dependent and that efficiency predictions should consider the available choice set.

- source: Gallo, E., Barak, D., & Langtry, A. (2023). Social distancing in networks: A web-based interactive experiment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Fines (punishment) for not practicing social distancing significantly increase the probability that participants choose to distance, compared to a baseline with no fine. Nudges (informational videos) have a smaller and less robust effect. The effect of fines is immediate and persistent, while the nudge effect is weaker and context-dependent. Network structure (star vs. complete) and position (superspreader vs. peripheral) influence distancing: superspreaders distance more. High disease contagiousness increases distancing. Political ideology, risk preferences, and social preferences also moderate distancing behavior. However, the experiment does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing fines (punishment) increases compliance with a costly pro-social action (distancing) in a networked environment structurally similar to a public goods game. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (distancing) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, binary action, fixed cost/benefit, punishment cost and impact, no reward, no feedback) are well specified, so the paper is informative about the likely direction of punishment effects on cooperation, but not directly on efficiency. Use this paper as supportive evidence that punishment increases pro-social behavior in similar environments, but do not use it for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Guo, Y. C., Zhao, X. R., Liu, Y. B., & Ma, J. H. (2024). The effect of third-party intervention on normative behavior of bystanders: An explanation of the social norm cueing effect. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both third-party punishment and third-party compensation, when observed by bystanders, increase the bystanders' fairness in subsequent dictator game allocations. This effect is mediated by changes in descriptive norm perceptions. The effect is present when the intervention is presented as group summary information (Study 1) or as an individual action perceived as representative of group behavior (Study 2). The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a dictator game, not a public goods game or its direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that observing third-party punishment or compensation can increase norm-compliant behavior (fairness in allocation) among bystanders, mediated by changes in perceived descriptive norms. However, because the study uses a dictator game (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. The findings may be useful for understanding norm transmission and the psychological mechanisms by which punishment or compensation can affect behavior, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs.

- source: El-Bialy, N., Aranda, E. F., Nicklisch, A., Saleh, L., & Voigt, S. (2022). To cooperate or not to cooperate? An analysis of cooperation and peer punishment among Syrian refugees, Germans, and Jordanians. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that Syrian refugees in both Germany and Jordan are more likely to cooperate with other refugees than with host country nationals, indicating in-group favoritism in cooperation. However, punishment behavior (the likelihood of punishing a defector) does not differ significantly based on whether the target is an in-group or out-group member. Host country participants (Germans and Jordanians) do not show significant differences in cooperation or punishment based on the partner's group. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the behavioral effects of punishment opportunities in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with a punishment stage, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does show that the presence of punishment does not lead to group-differentiated sanctioning, and that cooperation rates are high overall, especially within in-groups. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not provide the necessary payoff-based outcome data.

- source: Charness, G., Cobo-Reyes, R., & Jiménez, N. (2008). An investment game with third-party intervention. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The possibility of third-party punishment increases the amount sent by first movers by over 60% compared to the baseline with no punishment. Responders also return more when third-party punishment is possible. When both punishment and reward are possible, first movers send similar amounts as with punishment only, but responders return slightly less. Third parties spend more on total intervention (punishment plus reward) when both are available, but spend more on punishment when it is the only option. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a trust/investment game increases trusting and reciprocal behavior (amounts sent and returned). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that punishment increases cooperation-like behaviors, but the lack of efficiency data limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Nese, A., O'Higgins, N., Sbriglia, P., & Scudiero, M. (2018). Cooperation, punishment and organized crime: a lab-in-the-field experiment in southern Italy. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Camorra inmates are highly cooperative and strongly punish defectors, consistent with their internal codes of conduct. However, when external punishment (third-party punishment) is introduced, their cooperation decreases, indicating a rejection of external authority. Ordinary criminals, in contrast, increase cooperation when external punishment is possible, suggesting opportunism. Students' cooperation also decreases with TPP. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings highlight the importance of group-specific social norms and attitudes in shaping responses to punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how the introduction of punishment affects cooperation rates in a Prisoner's Dilemma setting, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player PD and a three-player PD with third-party punishment, rather than a standard multi-player PGG. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly on efficiency) is highly dependent on group norms and attitudes: punishment can reduce cooperation in some groups (students, Camorra) and increase it in others (ordinary criminals). However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment under different social norms, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that current enforcement (fines for defection) strongly increases the probability of cooperation in the first round of a match. However, exposure to enforcement in previous matches can decrease current cooperation in early games, because enforcement makes it harder to learn about the intrinsic cooperativeness of the group. Observing cooperation in the absence of enforcement is a stronger positive signal for future cooperation than observing cooperation under enforcement. The effect of enforcement on cooperation is dynamic and depends on the stage of learning in the group. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and their determinants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how punishment (formal enforcement via fines) affects cooperation rates in repeated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment increases cooperation in the short run, but may slow down the emergence of high cooperation in the long run by impeding learning about group values. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect evidence: if higher cooperation translates to higher efficiency in the analogous public goods context, then punishment is likely to increase efficiency in the short run, but the long-run effect may be muted or even negative if learning is important. The design dimensions (e.g., random rematching, repeated games, binary choices, fine size) are well specified and can inform parameter mapping, but the lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation.

- source: Liu, J., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2017). Information transparency and equilibrium selection in coordination games: an experimental study. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Revealing information about the existence and structure of centralized punishment or reward mechanisms in a stag hunt game increases the rate of mutual cooperation (the payoff-dominant equilibrium) compared to a control where only final payoffs are shown. The effect is stronger for punishment than for reward. The mechanism works both by increasing beliefs in others' cooperation and, for punishment, by directly increasing cooperative choices. In contrast, when the underlying game remains a prisoner's dilemma (i.e., the punishment/reward is too small to change the equilibrium), revealing the mechanism does not increase cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in mutual cooperation implies higher group welfare in the context of the stag hunt game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in two-player, repeated stag hunt games with centralized punishment or reward, making the institutional mechanism transparent increases cooperation rates, especially when punishment is highlighted. While efficiency is not directly measured, the increase in mutual cooperation (the payoff-dominant outcome) suggests that efficiency would also increase. The results are most relevant for coordination games adjacent to public goods games, rather than standard PGGs. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment and making its mechanism salient is likely to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such information, especially when the punishment is strong enough to change the equilibrium structure.

- source: Davis, D. D., & Holt, C. A. (1999). Equilibrium cooperation in two-stage games: Experimental evidence. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the use of contingent punishment/reward strategies in the second stage of a two-stage game is strongly associated with higher rates of cooperation in the first stage. This effect is robust across different treatments, including those where the punishment is not a Nash equilibrium or is Pareto dominated. The structure of the second-stage equilibria does not reliably predict cooperation rates. Instead, initial cohort propensities and first-stage payoff structure are more important. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability and use of punishment/reward strategies can increase cooperation rates in two-stage games, even when the punishment is not a Nash equilibrium. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The findings suggest that behavioral responses to punishment opportunities are important, but the translation to efficiency outcomes is not addressed. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can support cooperation, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Götte, L., Gügler, R., & Fehr, E. (2012). The mentalizing network orchestrates the impact of parochial altruism on social norm enforcement. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is strongly modulated by group membership: outgroup members who defect are punished much more than ingroup members who commit the same norm violation. This parochial punishment pattern is supported by increased activation and connectivity in a neural network associated with punishment-related decision processes (right orbitofrontal gyrus, right lateral prefrontal cortex, right dorsal caudatus) for outgroup punishment, and increased activation/connectivity in the mentalizing network (dorsomedial prefrontal cortex, bilateral temporo-parietal junction) for lenient ingroup punishment. The study provides evidence for neural mechanisms underlying parochial altruism in norm enforcement, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment option leads to parochial (group-biased) punishment behavior in a third-party punishment context, with outgroup norm violators punished more than ingroup violators. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design is a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with third-party punishment, which is adjacent to but not the same as a repeated public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment can be highly group-biased, and that the behavioral effect of punishment may depend on group context, but the paper does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Dorrough, A. R., Olsson, M. I. T., Froehlich, L., Glöckner, A., & Martiny, S. E. (2021). Does she compensate the victim while he punishes the perpetrator? No gender differences in anonymous economic games across 11 nations. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two large studies (one multinational, one German student sample), there is no evidence for systematic gender differences in third-party punishment or compensation behavior in response to unfairness. Both men and women are more likely to compensate the victim than to punish the perpetrator. Increasing the effectiveness of punishment (punishment impact per unit cost) does not substantially change this pattern. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the game is not a repeated public goods game but a one-shot third-party punishment/compensation scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral use of punishment and compensation options in a third-party context, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves norm enforcement and costly punishment, but is not a repeated or group-based contribution game. The main decision-relevant finding is that, even when punishment is made more effective, participants prefer compensation over punishment, suggesting that punishment may be underutilized in similar designs. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not measured, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding behavioral preferences for punishment versus compensation, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Brosig, J., Weimann, J., & Yang, C. L. (2004). Communication, reputation, and punishment in sequential bargaining experiments. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, without communication, the efficiency of punishment and the opportunity cost for the first mover significantly affect the likelihood of punishment and the first mover's choices, respectively. However, almost all second movers exploit the first mover's cooperative move, and equal splits are rare. When preplay communication is allowed, especially face-to-face, there is a dramatic increase in the number of equal splits (efficient outcomes), and the influence of punishment efficiency and opportunity cost largely disappears. E-mail communication also increases equal splits, but less so than face-to-face. The effect of communication cannot be fully explained by reputation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the behavioral shift toward equal splits with communication suggests higher efficiency in those treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that preplay communication (especially face-to-face) is a powerful moderator that increases the likelihood of efficient, equal-split outcomes in sequential bargaining games with punishment and reward options. In the absence of communication, the efficiency of punishment and the opportunity cost for the first mover are important determinants of behavior, but do not lead to efficient outcomes. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of communication should be considered a key dimension, and that enabling communication can substantially increase efficiency, even when punishment is available. However, the results are from a sequential bargaining game, not a standard public goods game, so direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Apergis-Schoute, A., Herrmann, B., Lieberman, M., Müller, U., Robbins, T. W., & Clark, L. (2013). Serotonin Modulates Striatal Responses to Fairness and Retaliation in Humans. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering serotonin via ATD increases costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the ultimatum game (second-party context), but tends to decrease costly punishment in third-party punishment games. The behavioral effects are accompanied by neural changes: ATD reduces ventral striatal responses to fairness and increases dorsal striatal responses during retaliation. The results suggest that serotonin modulates the drive for retaliation rather than fairness preferences. The games are one-shot, with no repeated interaction, and the main outcomes are behavioral and neural, not payoff-based. There is no direct analysis of efficiency or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and neurobiological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, showing that serotonin depletion increases second-party punishment but decreases third-party punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the games are not public goods games but rather ultimatum and third-party punishment games. The findings may inform predictions about the psychological drivers of punishment in similar environments, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design features (one-shot, no communication, binary accept/reject, known costs of punishment) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Li, S. Y., Ma, S. M., Wang, D. Y., Zhang, H. J., Li, Y. Z., Wang, J. X., Li, J. Y., Zhang, B. Y., Gross, J., De Dreu, C. K. W., Wang, W. X., & Ma, Y. N. (2022). Oxytocin and the Punitive Hub-Dynamic Spread of Cooperation in Human Social Networks. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in repeated networked games (trust game, ultimatum bargaining, and two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment), oxytocin administration to central (hub) players increases their willingness to enforce cooperation norms via costly punishment. This enforcement, rather than mere cooperation or trust, is what enables cooperation to spread throughout the network. Agent-based simulations confirm that high punishment rates by central nodes are more effective than high cooperation rates alone in promoting network-wide cooperation. Manipulating peripheral nodes' punishment or cooperation has much weaker effects. The main outcomes are cooperation and punishment rates, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some payoff equality measures are reported in the ultimatum game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment, especially when wielded by central/influential players, is a key moderator of cooperation in repeated networked social dilemmas. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment, norm enforcement), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that, in networked repeated games with punishment, enabling effective punishment by central players can substantially increase cooperation rates, and by extension, may improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The results are most relevant for predicting behavioral outcomes (cooperation, norm enforcement) rather than efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation. The design dimensions most strongly supported are network structure (heterogeneity, centrality), punishment existence and effectiveness, and repeated interaction. The study does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency change due to punishment, so its use for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect and should be supplemented with studies that report group payoff or efficiency directly.

- source: Arai, S., Tooby, J., & Cosmides, L. (2022). Motivations to reciprocate cooperation and punish defection are calibrated by estimates of how easily others can switch partners. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants who perceive higher relational mobility (i.e., that others can easily switch partners) reciprocate more and punish less in a dyadic trust game with punishment. The opportunity to switch partners (experimental manipulation) did not affect punishment, but did interact with relational mobility to affect reciprocation in American participants. Punishment did not increase subsequent cooperation and made punished partners more likely to leave. The main outcomes are behavioral (reciprocation, punishment, switching), not efficiency or group payoff. Payoff tables are provided, but efficiency is not a dependent variable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in punishment-enabled games: higher perceived partner choice (relational mobility) leads to more reciprocation and less punishment, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured or analyzed. The game is a dyadic trust game with punishment, not a standard public goods game, so findings are adjacent but not directly transferable. The paper is most informative about how punishment and partner choice cues affect individual behavior, not aggregate efficiency.

- source: Pansini, R., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2016). Women Tend to Defect in a Social Dilemma Game in Southwest China. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, women in Southwest China defect more and cooperate less than men, a pattern that contrasts with previous findings from Western and other Asian samples. Both genders earned less when they punished, and there was no significant gender difference in overall monetary earnings. The use of punishment did not increase individual earnings, and the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total welfare. The main findings are about behavioral strategies and gender differences, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a dyadic iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, punishment is used more frequently than in comparable Western samples, but its use is associated with lower individual earnings. The design is not a public goods game but a two-player PD, so direct prediction for PGG efficiency is limited. The main decision support is that punishment, as implemented here, does not increase individual payoffs and may reduce them. The paper does not report group efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). The Effect of Power Asymmetries on Cooperation and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with punishment, punishment did not increase cooperation rates. Instead, cooperation from a partner was more effective at eliciting cooperation than punishment. Punishment, especially from strong players, sometimes provoked retaliation and further defection. Weak players never retaliated against punishment, but both weak and strong players retaliated against strong punishers. Players were more likely to opt out after being punished or when facing a strong partner. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and the authors explicitly state their focus is on behavioral rather than payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma games with punishment, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation and may even have detrimental effects on cooperative behavior, especially when power asymmetries exist. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that, at least in two-player, all-or-nothing, binary-choice games with possible opting out, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may worsen behavioral outcomes. Caution should be used in generalizing to multi-player or continuous-contribution public goods games.

- source: Böckler, A., Tusche, A., & Singer, T. (2016). The Structure of Human Prosociality: Differentiating Altruistically Motivated, Norm Motivated, Strategically Motivated, and Self-Reported Prosocial Behavior. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that prosocial behavior is multidimensional, with four robust factors: altruistically motivated, norm motivated (including punishment and reciprocity), strategically motivated, and self-reported prosociality. Punishment behavior (in second and third person punishment games) loads on the 'norm motivated' factor, indicating its role in norm enforcement. The study replicates standard findings in economic games (e.g., people punish unfairness, give more in dictator than in punishment games, help more when reciprocated), but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The factor structure is stable across two large samples. Altruistic prosociality is associated with lower negative affect and higher cognitive skills. Gender, age, and income show differential associations with the factors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment behavior is a distinct, norm-motivated component of prosociality, separable from altruistic and strategic motivations. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design details of the punishment games (one-shot, anonymous, fixed punishment effectiveness) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task. The study is most useful for understanding the motivational structure underlying punishment and cooperation, not for quantifying efficiency effects.

- source: Ferguson, E. (2021). Inequality averse and compassionate blood donor: implication for interventions. *Vox Sanguinis*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Blood donors are more likely to choose compensation (directly helping the victim) over punishment (sanctioning the perpetrator) in a one-shot 3PPC game, while organ donors are more likely to choose punishment. The compensation and punishment options are both costly to the third party, and the game is designed so that each £1 spent on compensation or punishment has a 2x effect. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but describes how different choices affect the distribution of payoffs. A follow-up experiment finds that an intervention message based on advantageous inequality aversion increases non-donors' willingness to take information about blood donation, with some evidence that guilt-based appeals may have negative effects on current donors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about individual preferences for compensation versus punishment in a third-party context, which is adjacent to public goods games with punishment. The design is a one-shot, three-player game with both punishment and compensation options, and the main outcomes are behavioral choices, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of a compensation option may reduce the use of punishment, and that individuals' philanthropic identities (e.g., blood donor vs. organ donor) influence their preference for direct help versus norm enforcement. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in repeated public goods games, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited and indirect.

- source: Falk, A., Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2005). Driving forces behind informal sanctions. *Econometrica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a three-player one-shot PD with punishment, cooperators predominantly punish defectors, and this pattern is robust across different punishment effectiveness conditions and elicitation methods. Defectors also punish, but their punishment is more likely to be spiteful and disappears when punishment cannot increase payoff differences. The cost-effectiveness of punishment is manipulated, showing that cooperators are willing to spend more to punish defectors even when punishment is less effective. The results challenge fairness theories that focus only on group-level payoffs or inequality aversion, supporting instead a model where retaliation against unfairness is central. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and motivational aspects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on the behavioral patterns and motives of punishment in a three-player PD with punishment, including how punishment is targeted and how its cost-effectiveness affects behavior. However, it does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is frequently used by cooperators against defectors, and that the willingness to punish persists even when it is costly, which may imply potential for efficiency improvements in similar games, but this is not tested or quantified here. The design details (player count, one-shot, punishment cost/effectiveness) are well specified and can inform mechanistic expectations, but not direct efficiency predictions.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that religious primes (subliminally presented) do not generally increase costly punishment of unfair behavior in a two-player punishment game. However, for the subset of participants who have previously donated to a religious organization, religious primes significantly increase the amount of punishment assigned to unfair choices. There is no effect of religious primes on punishment of fair choices, and no main effect of punishment primes or combined religion-punishment primes. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, focusing solely on punishment behavior as the outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and contextual moderators of costly punishment behavior, specifically the interaction between religious priming and prior religious commitment. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a multi-player social dilemma. The design is a two-player allocation and punishment game, which is adjacent to but not the same as a public goods game. The findings may inform predictions about the psychological triggers of punishment, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The absence of efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Chen, M. L. (2012). The Effect of Leader Reward and Punishment Behaviors on Subordinates' Budget Reports. *Engineering Economist*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that hands-off (no reward or punishment) is least effective in reducing budgetary slack. Punishment alone reduces slack more than reward alone, but the combination of reward and punishment (carrot-stick) is most effective. The effect of punishment is stronger when the punishment is large relative to the benefit of slack creation. Rewards alone, regardless of size, have little effect. Requiring a factual assertion (honesty) also reduces slack. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (slack/honesty).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment, especially when combined with reward, reduces dishonest behavior (budgetary slack) in a principal-agent reporting game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it is a one-superior/one-subordinate budget reporting game, not a multi-player public goods game. The findings are most useful for understanding the behavioral impact of punishment and reward on honesty/slack, but less so for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Loustau, T., Glassman, J., Martin, J. W., Young, L., & Mcauliffe, K. (2024). The impact of group membership on punishment versus partner rejection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two preregistered online experiments, participants responded to resource allocation decisions by either punishing (subtracting from the allocator's bonus) or rejecting (choosing not to interact again). In both a minimal group paradigm and a real-world political group context, participants punished outgroup members more than ingroup members, especially when the allocator's intent was selfish and the outcome was unequal. However, group membership did not affect the likelihood of partner rejection. The main outcomes are punishment and rejection rates; no efficiency or group payoff measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games with and without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs but focuses on dyadic allocation games with punishment and partner rejection as responses. The main contribution is to show that group membership increases punishment of outgroup members, but this is not linked to efficiency or welfare outcomes. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, but it does provide evidence that group membership can moderate punishment behavior.

- source: Makovi, K., Sargsyan, A., Li, W. D., Bonnefon, J. F., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Trust within human-machine collectives depends on the perceived consensus about cooperative norms. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a third-party punishment game followed by a trust game, humans earn less trust by sharing with or punishing bots than with humans. Trust-gain is attenuated but not eliminated in mixed human-bot collectives. Perceived consensus about cooperative norms predicts trust-gain, and experimentally making norm consensus salient increases trust-gain in some conditions. The main outcomes are trust and norm perception, not group efficiency or total payoff. The design is adjacent to public goods games, but the outcome focus is on trust and norm-following rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its design is adjacent (third-party punishment game with binary sharing), and the main outcomes are trust-gain and norm perception, not efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can increase trust in norm-followers, especially when norm consensus is salient, but do not quantify effects on group efficiency. The paper may inform mechanism design around norm salience and trust, but is not directly informative for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Bernhard, H., Fischbacher, U., & Fehr, E. (2006). Parochial altruism in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly parochial: punishers spend more to punish norm violations when the victim is from their own group, regardless of the violator's group. Dictators (the potential norm violators) expect more punishment and transfer more to the recipient when the recipient and the third party are from the same group. When the third party is from the dictator's group, dictators expect less punishment and transfer less. The results suggest that the threat of punishment is not impartial but is shaped by group membership, leading to greater protection for ingroup victims. The paper does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are not directly about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness and application of punishment in norm enforcement are highly sensitive to group affiliations, with punishment being much more likely and severe when the victim and punisher are from the same group. However, because the experiment is a one-shot dictator game with third-party punishment (not a repeated public goods game), and because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. It is informative about the social and psychological moderators of punishment, especially parochialism, but not about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Özdemir, Y., & Fehr, E. (2014). The Value of Vengeance and the Demand for Deterrence. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that both retributive and deterrence motives drive costly punishment: participants punish norm violators even when the punishment is hidden and cannot deter future violations, but punishment is stronger when it can be communicated. Second-party punishment (when the punisher is the direct victim) is stronger than third-party punishment (when the punisher is an observer). The findings suggest that retributive motives can lead to punishment that does not increase social welfare and may be inefficient. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be motivated by retribution alone, leading to costly punishment even when it cannot improve future cooperation or group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency or group payoff, especially when punishment is driven by retributive motives rather than deterrence. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games, using a trust game with punishment, so findings may transfer in terms of understanding motives but not in terms of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Barriga, A., Ferguson, N. T. N., Fiala, N., & Leroch, M. A. (2023). Ethnic cooperation and conflict in Kenya. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing participants to switch partners (partner choice) increases cooperation rates compared to when switching is not possible. When switching is not possible, participants are more likely to punish defectors than to withdraw, but when switching is possible, punishment and withdrawal are used equally. Unexpectedly, punishment rates are higher when switching is possible. Importantly, punishment does not increase the likelihood that defectors will cooperate in the next round. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that partner choice is more effective than punishment at sustaining cooperation in this dyadic context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: in two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency as much as enabling partner choice (switching). Punishment does not increase subsequent cooperation, and its use is not associated with higher group welfare. The results suggest that, in dyadic games, punishment may not improve efficiency, and its effect may be limited or even negative if not paired with partner choice. However, because the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games is limited and should be interpreted with caution, especially for multi-player settings.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Rijkhoff, S. A. M., & van Dijk, E. (2013). Equality as a benchmark for third-party punishment and reward: The moderating role of uncertainty in social dilemmas. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishers and rewarders use the equality rule as a benchmark for sanctioning decisions only when the size of the common resource is certain. Under certainty, those who take more than an equal share are punished much more, and those who take an equal or smaller share are rewarded more. Under uncertainty, the amount of punishment or reward is linearly related to the amount harvested, with no sharp benchmark. The emotional basis for punishment is negative affect (anger), while for reward it is positive affect. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or common resource games, as it does not report any payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how the clarity of the cooperation norm (equality rule) and environmental uncertainty affect the application of punishment and reward. In settings where the cooperation norm is clear and known, punishment is more sharply targeted at norm violators; under uncertainty, punishment is more diffuse. This may indirectly inform predictions about the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation or efficiency, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for such effects.

- source: Ibanez, M., & Martinsson, P. (2013). Curbing coca cultivation in Colombia - A framed field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both increased risk of punishment (eradication) and increased legal profits (subsidies) reduce investment in illegal coca cultivation, but the effect is highly heterogeneous. About one third of participants have high moral costs and do not need external incentives, while two fifths are very hard to deter. Only about a quarter are responsive to policy levers. The supply of coca is inelastic to policy changes unless eradication risk is high (>30%). Carrots (subsidies) are more effective in areas with better market integration, while sticks (punishment) are more effective in depressed areas. The study provides cost estimates for both policies and suggests that optimal policy should mix both, tailored to local conditions. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (investment choices), not group efficiency or total welfare as in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence on how exogenous punishment (risk of eradication) and reward (subsidy) policies affect individual investment in a socially harmful activity, with a design adjacent to public goods games. The findings support that punishment can reduce undesirable behavior, but the effect is highly dependent on individual heterogeneity. The results are most useful for predicting how changes in punishment and reward parameters might affect behavioral outcomes (e.g., investment in the public bad), but less directly for predicting group efficiency as defined in PGGs. The cost-effectiveness analysis and elasticity estimates can inform models that link punishment/reward design to behavioral and, indirectly, payoff outcomes, especially in field-like or framed settings with real-world stakes.

- source: Goeschl, T., & Jarke, J. (2016). Second and third party punishment under costly monitoring. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that when monitoring is free, almost all punishers are 'targeted punishers' who punish defectors more than cooperators. As monitoring becomes costly, the share of targeted punishers drops sharply, and more subjects either do not punish at all or punish indiscriminately. The average punishment for defectors falls substantially as monitoring costs rise, and the difference in punishment between defectors and cooperators (the 'incentive to cooperate') is much lower under costly monitoring. These patterns are similar for both second and third party punishment, with some subtle differences in how subjects switch between punisher types across roles. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the authors argue that lower monitoring costs should lead to more efficient outcomes by making punishment more effective at supporting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost of monitoring (i.e., the ability to condition punishment on observed behavior) is a critical moderator of the effectiveness of punishment in social dilemma games. When monitoring is costly, punishment is less targeted and less effective at creating incentives for cooperation. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that in games where monitoring is costly or imperfect, enabling punishment will have a weaker (or possibly negligible) effect on efficiency compared to settings with free or perfect monitoring. For prediction tasks, the presence and cost of monitoring should be considered a key dimension when estimating the efficiency impact of punishment. However, because the study does not report actual efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited.

- source: Guo, Z. Y., Guo, R., Xu, C. Y., & Wu, Z. (2022). Reflexive or reflective? Group bias in third-party punishment in Chinese and Western cultures. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both Chinese and Western participants show ingroup favoritism in third-party punishment under reflexive (fast, high cognitive load) conditions, punishing ingroup norm violators less than outgroup violators. Under reflective (slow, low cognitive load) conditions, Western participants—especially those with high group identity—reduce ingroup favoritism and may even punish ingroup violators more, while Chinese participants continue to show strong ingroup favoritism regardless of decision mode. Group identity amplifies these effects. The main outcome is the amount of punishment assigned, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a third-party punishment mini-dictator game, not a standard public goods game, and does not report on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity, culture, and decision-making mode moderate the behavioral use of costly punishment in a third-party punishment context. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is adjacent to PGGs (mini-dictator/TPP), and the outcome is punishment behavior, not payoff. The findings are useful for understanding when and how punishment is used, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Boonmanunt, S., & Meier, S. (2023). The effect of financial constraints on in-group bias: Evidence from rice farmers in Thailand. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that being more financially constrained (before harvest) does not increase in-group bias in cooperation or norm enforcement. In-group bias in cooperation and punishment is observed only after harvest (less constrained), but these effects are not statistically significant in regression analyses. Sharing behavior is stable across financial conditions. The main outcomes are cooperation rates, punishment assigned, and sharing amounts, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that financial constraints do not inherently increase parochialism in cooperation or punishment among low-income rice farmers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it provides evidence that, in a prisoner's dilemma with third-party punishment (an adjacent but not exact PGG variant), the presence of punishment allows for the study of norm enforcement, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, cooperation rate) rather than efficiency. The design details (player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified, but the lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct value for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Galbiati, R., Schlag, K. H., & van der Weele, J. J. (2013). Sanctions that signal: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenous sanctions increase effort and beliefs about others' effort, with a stronger effect on effort than beliefs. Endogenous sanctions, where a principal chooses to sanction after observing first-round behavior, are less effective, especially for subjects who initially chose high effort. For low initial effort players, both types of sanctions have similar effects. The signaling effect—where the imposition of a sanction is interpreted as a signal that others are not cooperating—can counteract the incentive effect of sanctions for cooperators. The main analysis is on effort and beliefs, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that sanctions can increase effort in a minimum-effort game, but the effect depends on whether sanctions are exogenous or endogenous and on subjects' prior behavior. The signaling effect can reduce the effectiveness of sanctions for cooperators. For prediction, this suggests that the context and perceived informational content of punishment mechanisms may moderate their effect on efficiency, especially in coordination settings. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence.

- source: Bigoni, M., Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2013). Strategies of cooperation and punishment among students and clerical workers. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games for both students and workers. Students show a larger increase in cooperation and are more likely to adopt conditional cooperation strategies (e.g., forgiving or unforgiving trigger strategies) when punishment is available, while workers shift more toward unconditional cooperation. Workers are less likely to use personal punishment than students. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing personal punishment increases cooperation rates in indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games, with the effect being more pronounced among student subjects. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates and strategy adoption, not efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games (pairwise PDs in groups of four with random matching), so caution is needed in generalizing to standard PGGs.

- source: Li, X. H., Molleman, L., & van Dolder, D. (2021). Do descriptive social norms drive peer punishment? Conditional punishment strategies and their impact on cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that individuals' punishment of defectors is conditional on descriptive social norms: people punish more when cooperation or punishment is more common in a reference group. There is substantial heterogeneity: some punish independently, some increase punishment with higher cooperation (norm enforcement), and some with higher punishment (conformist). The dynamic model demonstrates that these conditional punishment strategies can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of high cooperation rates in populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment behavior is conditional on social context, and that such conditional punishment can promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. However, because the experiment is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma with punishment and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games. The dynamic model suggests that conditional punishment can help sustain high cooperation rates, which may imply higher efficiency in analogous settings, but this is an indirect inference.
