The paper is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and heterogeneity in punishment, and for modeling the potential for punishment to support cooperation, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Kurzban, R., DeScioli, P., & O'Brien, E. (2007). Audience effects on moralistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of an audience (even just the experimenter) increases the frequency and amount of third-party punishment of norm violators in both Trust Game and sequential Prisoner's Dilemma Game settings. Under anonymous conditions, punishment is infrequent and small; when observed, punishment is more common and larger. The effect is strongest when the audience includes other participants. The study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and does not use a public goods game, but it demonstrates that reputational concerns strongly increase costly punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers informative evidence that the salience of observation (audience effects) can substantially increase the willingness to engage in costly punishment. If a public goods game design includes features that make punishment decisions observable to others, one might expect higher rates of punishment, which could (in other contexts) affect cooperation and possibly efficiency. The lack of efficiency or payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task, but the findings are relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Deutchman, P., Aellen, M., Bogese, M., Bshary, R., Drayton, L., Gil, D., Martin, J., Pretot, L., Raihani, N., Santos, L. R., & Mcauliffe, K. (2023). Punishment is sensitive to outside options in humans but not in cleaner fish (Labroides dimidiatus). *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that human participants are sensitive to the intent behind cheating when deciding to punish: they punish more when a partner chooses to cheat despite having a cooperative option. Cleaner fish, in contrast, do not show this sensitivity; male fish punish female partners at similar rates regardless of whether the female had a choice to cooperate or not. The main outcome is the likelihood or amount of punishment, not efficiency or group payoff. The human experiment is a novel economic game inspired by cleaner fish mutualism, not a standard public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that human punishment is sensitive to intent (alternative options), while cleaner fish punishment is not. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the psychological basis for punishment in humans includes sensitivity to intent, which may affect when and how punishment is used, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Wang, X. M., Zhu, H. J., Zhao, Q. Y., & Wang, Y. R. (2024). The influence of social comparison on third-party altruistic behavior. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the direction of social comparison strongly influences whether third parties choose to punish violators or compensate victims in dictator-game scenarios. Upward comparison (feeling worse off) increases punishment, mediated by anger, while downward comparison (feeling better off) increases compensation, mediated by empathy. Public contexts increase the amount invested in compensation (especially for downward comparison), but do not change the basic choice pattern. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about psychological and contextual moderators of third-party punishment and compensation in dictator-game settings, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The findings are relevant for predicting when punishment or compensation will be chosen and how much will be invested, depending on social comparison and context. However, because the design is a third-party dictator game (not a public goods game) and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly support prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral and emotional drivers of punishment/compensation, not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Ünal-Kocaslan, O., & Akgün, S. (2024). Altruistic punishment in intergroup context. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants are willing to engage in costly punishment of unfair resource allocations, supporting the concept of altruistic punishment. The amount spent on punishment is higher for unfair than fair allocations. Ingroup favoritism is observed in that participants punish more harshly when the victim is an ingroup member, but there is no effect of the perpetrator's group identity. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and emotional responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is robustly observed in third-party punishment games, especially when unfairness is directed at ingroup members. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it uses a third-party punishment game with a dictator-like allocation, not a standard PGG. The findings are most useful for understanding when and how punishment is deployed, not its effect on group efficiency.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment of selfish behavior is robust to manipulations of third-party endowment (i.e., whether the punisher is relatively poorer or richer than the dictator) and to the use of the strategy method versus 'hot' decisions. Punishment is strongly associated with self-reported anger, not envy. The presence of third-party punishment is anticipated by actors and recipients, and actors who expect more punishment are less likely to behave selfishly. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment in dictator games is motivated by anger at norm violations rather than by envy or artifacts of the strategy method. However, because the design is a one-shot (or single-round) dictator game with a third-party punisher, and does not report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated public goods games or similar environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punishment, and suggest that punishment is robust to certain design manipulations (endowment, strategy method), but do not inform the efficiency consequences of enabling punishment.

- source: Klempt, C., & Pull, K. (2018). The hidden costs of control revisited: Should a sanctioning policy be announced in advance?. *Managerial and Decision Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the way sanctions are implemented and communicated matters: when the principal preannounces a sanctioning policy, agents interpret this as a signal of distrust, which can reduce their voluntary effort (crowding out). When the sanctioning decision is not announced in advance or is made by a computer, agents do not interpret it as a negative signal, and effort is higher. The study provides evidence for both incentive and signaling effects of sanctions, but all results are in terms of individual effort, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct measurement or reporting of efficiency or total surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the importance of how punishment is announced and who imposes it. If the prediction task involves settings where punishment is preannounced and personalized, one should expect possible crowding out of intrinsic motivation, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. If punishment is not preannounced or is impersonal (e.g., random or computer-imposed), the negative signaling effect is absent, and the incentive effect dominates, likely leading to higher effort (and by analogy, higher efficiency). However, since the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are indirect and should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Xie, E. H., Liu, M. D., Liu, J. Q., Gao, X. X., & Li, X. C. (2022). Neural mechanisms of the mood effects on third-party responses to injustice after unfair experiences. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals who experience disadvantageous unfairness (receiving less than others) are more likely to choose third-party punishment in a subsequent justice game, while those who experience advantageous unfairness (receiving more than others) are more likely to choose third-party compensation. These effects are mediated by mood (negative emotion increases punishment, positive emotion increases compensation) and sense of unfairness. Neural data show that increased DLPFC activity is associated with compensation after advantageous unfairness, while decreased DLPFC activity is associated with punishment after disadvantageous unfairness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a standard public goods game but a third-party justice game with costly punishment and compensation options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, focusing on third-party responses to unfairness rather than first-party cooperation or group provision. However, it provides informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie punishment and compensation choices, and how prior experiences and mood can shift the likelihood of punishment versus compensation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address how punishment affects group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Fabbri, M., & Carbonara, E. (2017). Social influence on third-party punishment: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that social information about peer punishment decisions leads third-party punishers to revise their own punishment levels toward the peer average. This effect is robust and stronger when conformity requires increasing punishment, even though this reduces the punisher's own payoff. The addition of a normative social influence channel (emoticon feedback) does not significantly change punishment behavior compared to informational social influence alone. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral conformity in punishment decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social information about peer punishment can substantially alter third-party punishment behavior, increasing conformity to peer norms. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The design is a modified dictator game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, conformity), not efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding how social information moderates punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Spadaro, G., Molho, C., Van Prooijen, J. W., Romano, A., Mosso, C. O., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2023). Corrupt third parties undermine trust and prosocial behaviour between people. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across five experimental studies, participants exposed to a corrupt (dishonest) third-party punisher (TPP) trusted strangers less and cooperated less in subsequent economic games (trust game, prisoner's dilemma, dictator game) with TPP present. The effect is robust to both second-hand and first-hand exposure to corruption, and to whether the TPP's corruption harms only the experiment or also a collective good. The negative effect of perceived TPP corruption on cooperation is mediated by reduced interpersonal trust. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of TPP corruption on efficiency is not measured. The design always includes a TPP; there is no comparison to a no-punishment baseline, so the effect of enabling punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived integrity of a third-party punisher moderates the effectiveness of punishment institutions in promoting trust and cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment (vs. no punishment) on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it report efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that if punishment is administered by corrupt or untrusted third parties, its positive effect on cooperation may be undermined or even reversed, but the paper does not quantify efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of institutional trustworthiness, not about the baseline effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

- source: Windrich, I., Kierspel, S., Neumann, T., Berger, R., & Vogt, B. (2024). Enforcement of Fairness Norms by Punishment: A Comparison of Gains and Losses. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Introducing a third-party punishment mechanism in the dictator game reduces selfishness (i.e., increases fairness) only when the punishment is strong enough, and this effect is more pronounced in the loss domain than in the gains domain. Weak punishment can have no effect or even reduce fairness in the gains domain. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, but provides detailed analysis of behavioral responses (dictator's demand, fair/equal splits, punishment frequency) to punishment strength and domain (gains vs. losses).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how the strength of the punishment mechanism (punishment impact per unit cost) moderates the behavioral effect of punishment in a dictator game context, especially in the loss domain. However, because the main outcome is dictator's demand (a measure of fairness/selfishness) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results suggest that strong punishment is necessary to reduce selfishness, and that punishment may be more effective in loss contexts. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support for the importance of punishment strength, but does not provide direct efficiency estimates.

- source: Wiederhold, E., & Bühren, C. (2025). Letting off Steam! Experimental Evidence on Displaced Aggression. *Review of Behavioral Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment studies how participants in the role of 'Player C' choose to punish others after allocations that may be unfair (intentional or by chance) or fair. Punishment is costly: for each point given up, 5 points are deducted from the target. The main findings (from the main paper, not this appendix) concern when and whom participants punish, and how this varies by the intentionality of the unfairness and by subject pool. Theoretical predictions using the Fehr-Schmidt model suggest punishment should only occur in unfair allocations, and only against those with higher payoffs. The appendix does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on punishment behavior in a non-PGG allocation game, with a strong manipulation of intentionality and target of punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The punishment technology (cost and impact) is clearly specified, which may be useful for mapping to other studies. The design is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant.

- source: Zhang, Y., Yu, H. B., Yin, Y., & Zhou, X. L. (2016). Intention Modulates the Effect of Punishment Threat in Norm Enforcement via the Lateral Orbitofrontal Cortex. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that the intention behind punishment threat critically shapes norm compliance: when a partner intentionally retains the right to punish, participants allocate less to the partner (reduced norm compliance), but when the partner intentionally waives the right, participants allocate more (increased norm compliance and positive affect). When the punishment threat is unintentional (computer-decided), these effects are weaker or absent. The rLOFC is shown to mediate these effects, as brain stimulation modulates the behavioral response to intention. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the social context and perceived intention behind punishment can strongly affect norm compliance (proxied by allocation), but does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment is perceived as self-serving or intentional, punishment may reduce cooperation or norm compliance, while unintentional or third-party punishment may be less detrimental or even neutral. For prediction, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as legitimate or self-interested, but the lack of efficiency data and the use of a dictator game limit direct applicability to public goods game efficiency predictions.

- source: Chen, H. Z., Zeng, Z. J., & Ma, J. H. (2020). The source of punishment matters: Third-party punishment restrains observers from selfish behaviors better than does second-party punishment by shaping norm perceptions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) of unfair behavior in a dictator game leads observers to perceive higher descriptive and injunctive norms for sharing, and to share more themselves, compared to second-party punishment (SPP). Mediation analyses show that the effect on sharing is primarily through changes in descriptive norm perception. The effect is robust across two experiments with different manipulations (verbal blame and monetary punishment). However, the outcome is individual sharing behavior, not group efficiency or payoff, and the context is a dictator game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the source of punishment (third-party vs. second-party) can affect observers' prosocial behavior by shaping norm perceptions, especially descriptive norms. While the findings are robust for sharing behavior in dictator games, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment mechanisms that are perceived as norm-expressive (e.g., third-party punishment) may have stronger effects on prosocial behavior, but the transferability to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is indirect and should be treated with caution.

- source: Bone, J. E., McAuliffe, K., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Exploring the Motivations for Punishment: Framing and Country-Level Effects. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment behavior in a two-player stealing game is strongly influenced by country of origin. US-based participants are more likely to punish in response to both losses and disadvantageous inequality, while India-based participants are more punitive overall and engage in more antisocial punishment, but only increase punishment when experiencing disadvantageous inequality. Methodological factors such as stake size and punishment cost (within the tested range) do not significantly affect punishment rates. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and all outcome measures are behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. However, it provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior varies with country of origin and with the presence of disadvantageous inequality, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is likely to be used. The design is adjacent to PGGs (two-player stealing game with punishment), but not a PGG or threshold public goods game. The findings suggest that punishment rates and motives can vary substantially by context and population, which may moderate any efficiency effects in related games.

- source: D'Orsogna, M. R., Kendall, R., McBride, M., & Short, M. B. (2013). Criminal Defectors Lead to the Emergence of Cooperation in an Experimental, Adversarial Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that the availability of the Informant strategy (criminals who also report/punish other criminals) is necessary for the emergence and stability of high cooperation in an adversarial game. When Informants are allowed, the system reliably transitions to and maintains a high-cooperation state (Utopia), regardless of initial conditions or parameter profile. When Informants are not allowed, the system converges to high defection (Dystopia). The results are robust across different initializations, parameter profiles (costs of reporting), and levels of behavioral inertia. However, the study does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare as outcome variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of a punishment mechanism (specifically, the Informant strategy) is crucial for the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in adversarial games. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The game is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by defectors) can transform a high-defection environment into a high-cooperation one, which would likely increase efficiency if measured. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of this inference.

- source: Goette, L., Huffman, D., Meier, S., & Sutter, M. (2012). Competition Between Organizational Groups: Its Impact on Altruistic and Antisocial Motivations. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in a neutral environment, punishment is used prosocially to enforce cooperation, especially when the victim of defection is an in-group member. When competition between groups is introduced, punishment becomes antisocial: out-group members are punished more, regardless of whether they cooperate or defect. Competition increases in-group cooperation but does not reduce out-group cooperation. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the authors argue that antisocial punishment under competition is wasteful and could reduce overall efficiency, while increased in-group cooperation could enhance it. The net effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of intergroup competition can fundamentally change the function of punishment in social dilemma games: from norm enforcement to antisocial, destructive punishment. While the design is not a standard repeated public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting when punishment will be efficiency-enhancing versus efficiency-reducing. Specifically, in environments with salient group competition, enabling punishment may lead to resource-destroying antisocial punishment, undermining the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data means the findings should be used as qualitative guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Cooper, D. J., & Kühn, K. U. (2014). Communication, Renegotiation, and the Scope for Collusion. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that communication, especially when it allows explicit threats of punishment, is critical for sustaining collusion in repeated games with incentives to cheat. Limited message spaces (even those allowing contingent punishment messages) do not sustain collusion, while open chat does. Renegotiation (chat between periods) further increases collusion, likely due to the role of verbal (non-monetary) punishment. Explicit threats of punishment significantly reduce cheating, and sending promises of trustworthy behavior also reduces cheating, though to a lesser extent. The effect of communication is not simply due to increased trust or appeals to mutual benefit, but specifically to the ability to coordinate on credible punishment strategies. The results are robust to various econometric specifications.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the communication environment. Open-ended chat that allows explicit threats of punishment leads to higher and more stable cooperation (collusion) than limited or structured message spaces. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum group payoff. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible, its effectiveness is greatly enhanced by the ability to communicate explicit threats, and that the structure of communication channels is a key moderator. The results also highlight that behavioral outcomes (collusion, cheating) can diverge from what would be predicted by monetary incentives alone, due to the role of verbal and social punishment.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2016). Looking Under the Hood of Third-Party Punishment Reveals Design for Personal Benefit. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is driven by the punisher's inference that a dictator who mistreats a recipient is also likely to mistreat the punisher. When punishers have information about how they themselves are treated, this information dominates their punishment decisions, and information about third-party treatment is largely ignored. The results support a deterrence-based account of punishment motivation. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological motivations for punishment, especially the conditions under which punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not address whether punishment increases or decreases group efficiency. The design is a modified dictator/third-party-punishment game, not a standard public goods game, and no efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency.

- source: Li, J. B., Pan, J. J., Zhu, C. K., & Wang, Y. W. (2022). Inter-brain synchronization is weakened by the introduction of external punishment. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of external punishment (i.e., making responder rejection payoff-relevant in the rrUG) leads to significantly higher mean offers from proposers compared to the rrDG (no punishment). However, the main focus is on neural outcomes: inter-brain synchronization is significantly lower in the punishment condition (rrUG) than in the no-punishment condition (rrDG). There is no significant effect of punishment on the rejection rate of responders. The study interprets the neural results as evidence that punishment crowds out moral motivation, reducing shared cognitive/emotional processing between players. No direct measures of group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (via responder rejection in a two-player ultimatum game) increases proposer generosity (mean offer size), which could be associated with higher group payoffs if responders accept. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games but is not a PGG or close variant. The main outcome is neural (inter-brain synchronization), not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers only weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bauer, M., Cahlfková, J., Chytilová, J., Roland, G., & Zelinsky, T. (2023). Shifting Punishment onto Minorities: Experimental Evidence of Scapegoating. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when a member of the punisher's own group is harmed by a wrongdoer, majority-group punishers are much more likely to punish an innocent minority (Roma) scapegoat than an innocent majority scapegoat. The amount of punishment assigned to the scapegoat increases with the intensity of harm done by the wrongdoer, and this increase is twice as large when the scapegoat is from the minority group. No ethnic bias is observed when no harm is done. The effect is robust to controls and is not explained by collective responsibility, over-attribution of blame, or unconditional spite. The design does not involve a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides direct evidence on punishment behavior and ethnic bias in scapegoating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma settings, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in such games. However, it provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior can be biased by group identity and context, especially in situations where punishment is displaced onto innocent parties. The punishment mechanism here is highly effective (low cost, high impact), and punishment is purely destructive. The findings may be relevant for understanding the social and psychological moderators of punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard PGG-like environments.

- source: Doering, L., & Ody-Brasier, A. (2021). Time and Punishment: How Individuals Respond to Being Sanctioned in Voluntary Associations. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effect of being sanctioned (fined) for absence in voluntary microsavings groups changes over time. Early in group tenure, sanctioned individuals reduce their prosocial contributions to the collective fund, but as they become more dependent on the group (measured by time in the group or by having received emergency funds), their response to sanctions becomes increasingly prosocial, with higher allocations to the collective fund after being fined. The effect is robust to various controls and alternative specifications. The study does not measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but the findings suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting prosocial behavior is temporally and relationally dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on prosocial behavior (here, contributions to a collective fund) is not static but depends on group tenure and individual dependence on the group. For prediction tasks involving efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments, this suggests that the impact of punishment may be weaker or even negative early in group formation, but can become more positive as group members become more dependent on each other. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather individual prosocial allocation, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The study is most informative about the temporal dynamics and social context moderating punishment effects, rather than about the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Lois, G., & Wessa, M. (2019). Creating sanctioning norms in the lab: the influence of descriptive norms in third-party punishment. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants' third-party punishment behavior in a repeated dictator game was strongly shaped by social feedback about peer punishment. Exposure to high or low average peer punishment led to persistent differences in participants' own punishment levels, even after feedback was removed. The presence of a single free rider (a peer who never punished) in a group with otherwise high punishment reduced average punishment and increased the frequency of free riding, despite the same average peer punishment as the high group. These effects persisted over time, indicating the formation of stable sanctioning norms. The study demonstrates the importance of descriptive norms and the 'bad apple' effect in shaping costly punishment behavior, but does not directly measure or report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that descriptive norms and the presence of free riders can substantially moderate the level of costly punishment in repeated norm enforcement games. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, free riding) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments. The study suggests that the presence of free riders can undermine the effectiveness of punishment as a norm enforcement tool, which could plausibly reduce efficiency gains from punishment, but this is not directly tested. The game is a repeated third-party punishment dictator game, not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency should be made with caution.

- source: Li, J., Xiao, E., Houser, D., & Montague, P. R. (2009). Neural responses to sanction threats in two-party economic exchange. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a two-player repeated trust game, the threat of monetary sanctions (punishment) leads trustees to return significantly less to investors compared to when no sanction is threatened. This effect is robust across fair and unfair investor requests. Neural data show that the presence of a sanction threat reduces activation in brain regions associated with social reward (VMPFC, LOFC, amygdala) and increases activation in regions associated with rational, utility-based decision making (parietal cortex). The findings support the 'perception shift' hypothesis: sanction threats crowd out norm-based motivations and shift behavior toward self-interested, utility-maximizing choices. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, but the reduction in reciprocity implies a likely reduction in efficiency relative to the no-sanction condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player repeated trust game, the introduction of a punishment (sanction) threat reduces cooperative behavior (reciprocity) by trustees. While the design is not a standard public goods game, the mechanism—punishment crowding out intrinsic motivation—may generalize to PGG-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment can reduce efficiency or group payoff in environments where cooperation is norm-driven and where punishment is perceived as a signal of distrust or as crowding out intrinsic motivation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or total earnings, the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the reduction in reciprocity. The findings are most relevant for two-player, repeated, sequential games with optional punishment, and less directly for standard multi-player PGGs.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2023). Communicating emotions, but not expressing them privately, reduces moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, the authors find that giving participants the opportunity to communicate their emotions to their partner after each round of a Prisoner's Dilemma game with costly punishment reduces the rate of moral punishment compared to a control condition. However, this reduction is small, and moral punishment remains common. Privately expressing emotions (without communication) does not reduce moral punishment. Both emotion communication and private expression can reduce cooperation and increase other forms of punishment (hypocritical punishment, punishment bias). The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how emotion communication and private expression affect punishment behavior in a repeated one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as a function of punishment, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment rates (especially moral punishment) can be reduced by allowing emotion communication, but the effect is small. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage. The results are most useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Moral labels increase cooperation and costly punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that moral framing (using labels like 'I cooperate' and 'I cheat') increases the likelihood of both moral and hypocritical punishment compared to neutral framing ('A' and 'B'). In Experiment 1, where only participants could punish, moral framing increased punishment but did not affect cooperation. In Experiment 2, where both participants and partners could punish, moral framing increased both punishment and cooperation. Antisocial punishment and general punishment bias were not affected by framing. The results suggest that the effect of moral framing on cooperation is contingent on the presence of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and framing of punishment options can increase both punishment behavior and, under certain conditions, cooperation in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage, but the findings about behavioral responses to punishment and framing may be informative for predicting when punishment will increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) in similar environments. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to increase cooperation when it is symmetric (i.e., both players can punish), and that moral framing amplifies this effect.

- source: Boschini, A., Muren, A., & Persson, M. (2011). Men among men do not take norm enforcement seriously. *Journal of Socio-economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the inclination to punish norm-breakers (interpreted as norm enforcement) is context-dependent: men among men are less likely to punish than women or men in mixed-gender settings. This result holds both for individuals and for groups. The difference is substantial (about 25% lower punishment rates for men-among-men) and statistically significant. However, the paper does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this study. The findings are relevant for understanding who enforces cooperation norms, but not for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like environments. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the behavioral propensity to punish under different gender and group contexts in a sequential prisoner's dilemma. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence and composition of gender in groups may affect the likelihood of punishment being used, but it does not inform how this translates into efficiency outcomes. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not measured or discussed. Thus, the paper is only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, and should be weighted accordingly.

- source: Cameron, L., Chaudhuri, A., Erkal, N., & Gangadharan, L. (2009). Propensities to engage in and punish corrupt behavior: Experimental evidence from Australia, India, Indonesia and Singapore. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish corrupt behavior varies more across cultures than the propensity to engage in corruption. Indian subjects are most likely to offer and accept bribes and least likely to punish, while Indonesian subjects are least likely to engage in corruption and most likely to punish. The manipulation of the bribe's welfare impact (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) only significantly affects behavior in Australia, where higher harm reduces punishment rates and increases bribery. In other countries, this manipulation has little effect. The main outcomes are behavioral (bribery and punishment rates/amounts), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how punishment opportunities and the cost/harm structure of norm violations affect the willingness to punish and engage in norm-violating behavior in a corruption context. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a three-player, one-shot, sequential-move game with endogenous costly punishment, but not a repeated or group-based public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment rates and their deterrent effect are highly culture-dependent and may not always translate into higher efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and the harm from norm violation is high. The manipulation of the harm from corruption (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) shows that increasing the harm can sometimes reduce punishment and increase norm violation, due to the increased cost to the victim. This is relevant for predicting when punishment will be effective in improving efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Tan, F. F., & Xiao, E. (2018). Third-party punishment: Retribution or deterrence?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that group third parties are more likely to approve punishment for deterrence purposes (when their decisions are revealed before the PD game), while individual third parties' punishment decisions are not sensitive to whether punishment can deter. Groups are less likely than individuals to approve punishment overall, but the magnitude of punishment (when approved) is similar. Despite the presence of third-party punishment, cooperation rates in the PD game remain low across all treatments, and punishment is not sufficient to change the game into one where cooperation is the dominant strategy for most participants. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that the punishment institution as implemented does not substantially increase cooperation or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the behavioral effects of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD game, especially regarding the motives for punishment and the difference between group and individual third parties. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment (even when costless to the third party) is not always used to maximize cooperation or group welfare, and that group decision-making may lead to more deterrence-driven but less frequent punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of third-party punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency or cooperation, especially if punishment is not strong enough to change incentives. The design is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant.

- source: Gummerum, M., López-Pérez, B., Van Dijk, E., & Van Dillen, L. F. (2022). Ire and punishment: Incidental anger and costly punishment in children, adolescents, and adults. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that experimentally induced incidental anger increases costly punishment of unfair offers in the ultimatum game (second-party punishment) for children, adolescents, and adults. In third-party punishment games, incidental anger increases punishment severity for unfair offers in adults and adolescents, but not in children. The effect of anger is specific to unfair (unequal) offers and does not increase punishment of fair (equal) offers. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment rates and severity), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the motivation for costly punishment in response to unfairness is influenced by emotional state and developmental stage, with self-relevance and norm appraisals playing key roles.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or related games. Its main contribution is to show that emotional state (incidental anger) can increase costly punishment of unfairness, especially when the violation is self-relevant (second-party) or, for adults, when it violates fairness norms (third-party). For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment rates and severity can be modulated by emotional and developmental factors, but it does not inform how these changes in punishment behavior translate into changes in efficiency or group payoff. The game designs used (ultimatum and third-party punishment games) are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard public goods games, and no efficiency or welfare outcomes are reported.

- source: Houser, D., Xiao, E., McCabe, K., & Smith, V. (2008). When punishment fails: Research on sanctions, intentions and non-cooperation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that threats of punishment (a $4 fine for not meeting the requested back-transfer) do not increase cooperation and can even reduce it, especially when the requested return is high relative to the sanction. This effect is the same whether the punishment is intentionally imposed by the investor or randomly assigned by nature, indicating that incentives, not intentions, drive the effect. When the sanction is used to enforce a low request, it increases full cooperation; when used to enforce a high request, it increases complete defection. The main outcomes are the percentage of the tripled amount returned and the distribution of cooperation/defection types. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation in trust/investment games depends on the relationship between the sanction size and the requested return. Punishment can reduce cooperation when the sanction is small relative to the demand. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral responses (cooperation/defection) to punishment, especially in one-shot, two-player, trust-like games with continuous contribution and fixed punishment cost/effectiveness. The lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Delton, A. W., & Krasnow, M. M. (2017). The psychology of deterrence explains why group membership matters for third-party punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly influenced by group membership: punishers assign the most punishment when an outgroup dictator treats an ingroup recipient poorly, and the least when an ingroup dictator treats an outgroup recipient poorly. This pattern is best explained by deterrence psychology, where punishers infer that outgroup dictators who mistreat ingroup members are likely to mistreat the punisher as well. The group norm maintenance theory (fairness or parochialism norms) does not fit the observed punishment patterns. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on punishment behavior and inferences about personal valuation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about the psychological mechanisms underlying third-party punishment and how group membership moderates punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most useful for understanding when and why punishment is likely to be assigned, especially in relation to group membership, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

- source: Bone, J. E., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). Human punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge and a desire for equality. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is motivated by both a desire for revenge (punishment occurs even when it cannot restore equality) and a desire for equality (when possible, punishment is tailored to equalize payoffs). Punishment is more likely when stealing creates disadvantageous inequality and when punishment is effective. However, the outcomes measured are behavioral (punishment frequency and amount), not efficiency or group payoff. The experiment is a two-player, one-shot, anonymous game with no communication, and does not use a public goods game or its direct variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is shaped by the effectiveness of punishment and the presence of inequality, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar multi-player settings. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be used and how it will be targeted, especially regarding the importance of punishment effectiveness (fee-to-fine ratio) and inequality, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not report efficiency or welfare, so its value for the prediction task is limited to qualitative insights about punishment motivation and targeting.

- source: Shinada, M., & Yamagishia, T. (2007). Punishing free riders: direct and indirect promotion of cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that third-party punishment is group-based: cooperators in the gift-giving game punish in-group cheaters more severely than out-group cheaters, while noncooperators show the opposite pattern. Most punishment is directed at the least cooperative group members. Emotional responses (anger, unfairness, guilt) are correlated with punishment of in-group cheaters but not out-group cheaters. The design uses a gift-giving game with a public-goods-like structure, but does not directly measure or report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral allocation of punishment in group-structured social exchange games, especially regarding in-group vs. out-group targeting. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design and findings are most useful for understanding the social and motivational determinants of punishment, not its impact on group welfare or efficiency. The punishment mechanism (costly to punisher, 3x impact on target) and the absence of feedback or repeated interaction are relevant for mapping to other designs, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Rommel, J. (2015). What can economic experiments tell us about institutional change in social-ecological systems?. *Environmental Science & Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing third-party punishment of traders (who cause negative externalities) leads to a significant reduction in market entry rates compared to the baseline, especially for sellers. About 86% of observers are willing to punish, spending on average more than half their endowment to do so. The conditional entry treatment also reduces market entry, as about a quarter of participants condition their entry on others' behavior, reflecting norm conformity. The main outcome is the proportion of participants who choose to trade (i.e., cause the externality), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings show that punishment can enforce social norms and reduce harmful market behavior, but the study does not measure efficiency as defined in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of costly punishment reduces the frequency of norm-violating behavior (trading that causes negative externalities) in a large market setting. However, because the outcome is market entry rate (not group efficiency or total payoff), the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games. The punishment mechanism is effective at deterring harmful behavior, especially among sellers, and most observers are willing to punish at a cost. For prediction tasks, the main signal is that enabling punishment reduces the rate of norm violation, but the mapping to efficiency or group payoff in standard PGGs is indirect. The market structure, one-shot design, and externality to a third party differ from standard PGGs, so caution is needed in transferring quantitative effects.

- source: Baum, W. M., Paciotti, B., Richerson, P., Lubell, M., & McElreath, R. (2012). Cooperation due to cultural norms, not individual reputation. *Behavioural Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a trust game with a conditional fine, trustees return more to investors from their own ethnic group when no fine is available. When a fine is available and imposed, trustworthiness increases in cross-ethnic pairs, eliminating the in-group advantage. In same-ethnic pairs, the fine has no effect or may crowd out trustworthiness. The effect is similar when the fine is available but not used. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings as a main outcome, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment is more effective at increasing trustworthiness across ethnic lines than within them.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (measured as trustworthiness) depends on group identity: punishment increases trustworthiness in cross-ethnic pairs but not in same-ethnic pairs. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The design is a modified trust game, not a public goods game, so transferability to PGGs is limited but informative for settings where group identity and punishment interact. The punishment is costless to the punisher and has a fixed impact, which should be considered when mapping to other designs.

- source: Migheli, M., & Saraceno, M. (2023). On the propensity to settle or litigate in laboratory disputes. *Economia Politica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of costly, uncertain punishment (litigation) is a strong motivator for offenders to restore what they took, more so than fairness concerns about the victim's effort. Offenders are more likely to restore when they learn the victim worked for their endowment, but this effect is mainly present among female offenders. Higher litigation fees increase the probability that offenders restore to avoid litigation and decrease the probability that victims initiate litigation, with gender moderating these effects. The study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses to punishment threats and fairness cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral effects of costly, uncertain punishment threats (litigation) in a two-player dispute context, not a public goods game. It shows that punishment threats (here, litigation) increase the likelihood of restoration (settlement), especially when the cost of punishment is high. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it involves second-party costly punishment with the possibility of redress, but lacks the multi-player, repeated, or contribution structure of PGGs. The main relevance is for understanding how punishment cost and fairness cues affect compliance and dispute resolution, not efficiency.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kteily, N. S. (2023). How reputation does (and does not) drive people to punish without looking. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputational incentives can increase the likelihood that individuals engage in punishment (signing punitive petitions), and as a byproduct, some of this punishment occurs without considering opposing perspectives. However, when the process of 'looking' is made observable, individuals are more likely to consider opposing perspectives, and punishment without looking does not increase. Evaluators (the audience) reward punishment in general, but do not reward punishment without looking; in fact, they see it as less fair and competent. The effect is stronger with more ideological audiences. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on reputational and behavioral outcomes in a Dictator Game context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or social dilemmas. Its design is adjacent: it uses a reputational Dictator Game with real-world punitive actions (signing petitions) and manipulates observability of punishment and information-gathering. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment, looking) and reputational (evaluations, money shared as a reward), not group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that making punishment observable increases punishment (and punishment without looking), but making deliberation observable increases deliberation. For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about how reputational incentives can increase punishment rates, but not about their effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Zhang, L., Jin, Y., Xia, L., Xu, B. B., & Abdullah, S. M. S. (2022). The Effects of Social Distance and Asymmetric Reward and Punishment on Individual Cooperative Behavior in Dilemma Situations. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that (1) cooperation rates are higher with friends than with strangers; (2) symmetric rewards and punishments lead to higher cooperation rates than asymmetric ones; (3) introducing a punishment option reduces cooperation rates compared to a baseline with only cooperation/defection; (4) asymmetric punishment especially reduces cooperation among friends. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment, especially when asymmetric, can undermine cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (especially asymmetric punishment) can reduce cooperation rates in iterated prisoner's dilemma games, and that this effect is stronger among socially close participants. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that in similar environments, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and may even reduce it if it reduces cooperation, but this is an inference from behavioral outcomes, not a direct measurement of efficiency.

- source: Güney, S., & Newell, B. R. (2013). Fairness overrides reputation: the importance of fairness considerations in altruistic cooperation. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that rejection of unfair offers (costly punishment) in the Mini Ultimatum Game is primarily driven by fairness considerations, particularly the perceived intentions of the proposer. When intentions are not assessable, outcome fairness still motivates some punishment, but to a lesser degree. The opportunity for reputation building and future interaction (i.e., repeated, non-anonymous play) does not significantly increase costly punishment when fairness considerations are strong, but can increase punishment when intentions are ambiguous. The study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the psychological drivers of costly punishment in ultimatum-like bargaining games are primarily fairness-based, not reputation-based, and that the presence of repeated interaction or reputation opportunities does not necessarily increase punishment or change behavioral outcomes when fairness motives are strong. However, because the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment occurs, not its aggregate payoff consequences.

- source: Cabrales, A., Kendall, R., & Sánchez, A. (2022). The effectiveness of prosocial policies: Gender differences arising from social norms. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that policy interventions (framing, exogenous punishment, endogenous punishment) do not reduce the overall average speed (a proxy for collective safety) compared to control. However, there are strong gender differences: policies reduce risky (Fast) choices and average speed among female participants, but increase them among males. This results in no net improvement in group-level outcomes and increases gender inequality in payoffs. The effect is mediated by differences in social norms and normative expectations. The most effective policy for changing female behavior is endogenous punishment, but male participants respond by increasing risky behavior, offsetting the gains. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, and the main outcomes are behavioral and norm-related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment mechanisms (both exogenous and endogenous) on group-level efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments may be highly heterogeneous and context-dependent, especially along gender lines. In this specific driving game, punishment does not improve group-level efficiency (as proxied by average speed or safety) because positive effects for one subgroup (females) are offset by negative effects for another (males). The design is not a standard PGG, and efficiency is not directly measured, so the findings should be transferred with caution. The main implication for prediction is that punishment may not always increase efficiency, especially if there are strong heterogeneities in norm compliance or behavioral response. The study highlights the importance of considering subgroup responses and social norms when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Cosmides, L., Pedersen, E. J., & Tooby, J. (2012). What Are Punishment and Reputation for?. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
