  findings: The experiments show that (1) subjects use third-party reputation to regulate trust only when first-party (personal) information is unavailable; (2) punishment is not more likely to be directed at partners with a reputation for norm violation; (3) subjects are more likely to cooperate with defectors whom they have punished, consistent with punishment as a bargaining tool for future cooperation; (4) when deciding to cooperate, subjects rely on the partner's most recent behavior toward themselves, not on past reputation. The results support social exchange theory over group norm maintenance theory in the context of dyadic trust games with punishment. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological function of punishment in dyadic trust games, showing that punishment is used to bargain for better treatment rather than to enforce group norms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a multi-player setting. The findings suggest that in small-scale, dyadic games, the presence of punishment may not increase group efficiency, but rather serves to regulate individual relationships. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether the game context is dyadic or group-based, and on the psychological mechanisms engaged. The design dimensions most relevant here are the dyadic structure, the use of reputation, and the role of punishment as a bargaining tool.

- source: Myers, C. D. (2016). Participation and punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is possible, participation (turnout) increases dramatically (from 60% to 88%). Punishment is overwhelmingly directed at abstainers, and abstainers incur a net loss from being punished. Egoist subjects are more responsive to punishment than pro-social subjects, leading to a more egoist composition among participants when punishment is available. However, because participation is costly and does not increase the value of the prize, increased participation does not improve group efficiency or welfare. The study does not report treatment effects on efficiency or group payoff, and the design makes clear that increased participation is not efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases participation in a binary-choice, team-based contest game where participation is individually costly and does not increase group welfare. However, it does not provide evidence that punishment increases efficiency or group payoff; in fact, the design ensures that increased participation is efficiency-reducing. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment depends critically on the structure of the game: in games where participation is not efficiency-enhancing, punishment may increase costly participation without improving (and possibly reducing) efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses (participation/cooperation) to punishment, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard public goods games.

- source: Henrich, J., McElreath, R., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., Marlowe, F. W., Tracer, D., & Ziker, J. (2006). Costly punishment across human societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that costly punishment (both second-party and third-party) is a widespread human behavior, but its prevalence and intensity vary substantially across societies. In all populations, more unequal offers are punished more frequently, but the threshold for punishment and the willingness to punish differ. There is a strong positive correlation between a population's willingness to punish and its average altruistic behavior (as measured by dictator game offers). The findings support theories of gene-culture coevolution in human cooperation. However, the paper does not use public goods games and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is a robust and variable feature of human behavior across societies, and that it is positively associated with altruism. However, because the experiments are ultimatum and third-party punishment games (not public goods games), and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that the effect of punishment on cooperation-related behaviors is likely to be highly variable across populations, and that willingness to punish is linked to local norms of altruism. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in repeated or group-based public goods settings.

- source: Weiss, M., Saulin, A., Iotzov, V., Hewig, J., & Hein, G. (2023). Can monetary incentives overturn fairness-based decisions?. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that offering monetary incentives that conflict with fairness-based punishment or compensation reduces the frequency of fairness-congruent decisions and slows the decision process (lower drift rate in DDM). This effect is similar for both punishment and compensation domains. The effect of conflicting incentives is stronger at higher incentive levels. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on behavioral and cognitive process outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that monetary incentives can undermine fairness-based punishment and compensation behavior, reducing the likelihood and efficiency of such decisions. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, using a modified dictator game with punishment/compensation framing, but lacks repeated interaction, group structure, or explicit efficiency measures. The main predictive value is in showing that the cost and alignment of incentives can strongly moderate the behavioral impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Engel, C., Goerg, S. J., & Yu, G. N. (2016). Symmetric vs. Asymmetric Punishment Regimes for Collusive Bribery. *American Law and Economics Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is asymmetric (bribers punished less than recipients), bribers are much more likely to report to authorities if recipients accept the bribe but do not grant the favor. This threat is anticipated by recipients, who are then more likely to grant the favor, and bribers are more likely to offer bribes. These effects are robust across countries (Germany and China) and to framing (neutral vs. corruption). The main outcomes are behavioral (bribe offers, reporting, granting favors), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that asymmetric punishment, while intended to deter corruption, may actually facilitate collusive bribery by making enforcement of corrupt deals easier.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that lowering the cost of punishment (making it cheaper for one party to punish another) increases the use of punishment and, in this context, increases the frequency of cooperation in corrupt deals. However, because the game is not a public goods game or direct variant, and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators of behavioral outcomes, but the translation to efficiency is not directly tested here.

- source: Cheung, G., & Siegel, D. A. (2025). The Costs of Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Risk, Punishment, and Immunity. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that higher punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success among nonimmune individuals. Immune individuals are more likely to participate, but their participation is not affected by punishment risk or cost. As the number of immune individuals increases, nonimmune individuals participate less, consistent with free riding. Group size has no significant effect on nonimmune participation or group success, but immune individuals participate more in larger groups. The main outcomes are participation and group success, not efficiency or total group payoff. Average payoffs are reported, but not analyzed as a function of the experimental treatments. The findings support the theoretical model's predictions about the effects of punishment risk, cost, immunity, and group size on participation and collective action success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how external punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success in a collective action setting, and how immunity and group size moderate these effects. However, the game is not a standard public goods game: the group benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a random pivotal actor, and only participants can be punished by an external agent. There is no within-group punishment or contribution to a divisible public good. Efficiency as defined in public goods games (total group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed, though average payoffs and group success rates are reported. The results are most relevant for predicting how external punishment risk and cost affect participation and group-level success in threshold-like or all-or-nothing collective action settings, especially when some individuals are immune to punishment. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGG efficiency predictions, but may inform models where external sanctions or risk of punishment for cooperation are present.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2005). Emotion expression in human punishment behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when responders in the ultimatum game are allowed to send a message to proposers expressing their feelings about the offer, the rejection rate of unfair offers (≤20% to responder) drops from 60% (no emotion expression allowed) to 32% (emotion expression allowed). This effect is statistically significant. The ability to express negative emotions through messages appears to reduce the use of costly punishment. The distribution of offers by proposers does not differ between treatments, indicating that the effect is due to responder behavior. The study does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, but the behavioral change would mechanically increase total earnings by reducing the frequency of zero-payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in environments where costly punishment is possible, introducing a non-costly channel for emotion expression can reduce the use of costly punishment. While the study is not a public goods game, it is highly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punishment and the potential for communication or emotion expression to moderate punishment behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling communication or emotion expression may reduce the negative efficiency impact of punishment by reducing its frequency. However, the findings are specific to the ultimatum game and may not transfer directly to public goods games without further evidence.

- source: FeldmanHall, O., Otto, A. R., & Phelps, E. A. (2018). Learning Moral Values: Another's Desire to Punish Enhances One's Own Punitive Behavior. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across four experiments, participants who learned about another's strong preference for punishment increased their own endorsement of punitive options when responding to unfair offers. This effect was observed both when participants actively implemented another's preferences and when they passively observed them. The effect generalized across online and lab settings, and from a justice game (with compensatory and punitive options) to the ultimatum game (where only costly punishment is available). The learning process is best described by a reinforcement learning model sensitive to the magnitude of unfairness. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare; the focus is on individual behavioral change in punitive choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that exposure to others' punitive preferences increases individuals' own willingness to punish in response to unfairness, even when punishment is costly. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is dyadic and focused on justice restoration, not multi-player public goods provision. The findings are most relevant for predicting changes in punitive behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Martin, J. W., Martin, S., & McAuliffe, K. (2021). Third-Party Punishment Promotes Fairness in Children. *Developmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Third-party punishment (costly, by an adult experimenter) increases the rate at which younger children (ages 6-7) choose the fairer allocation in a forced-choice dictator game, compared to both a baseline and a reputation-only (observer) condition. The effect is not present in older children (ages 8-9), who already show high rates of fair choices. The mechanism appears to be direct feedback from punishment, not reputational concern. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design is a dictator game rather than a public goods game or its direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that third-party punishment can increase norm-compliant (fair) behavior in a resource allocation game among children, especially those in a developmental window where fairness norms are emerging. However, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a forced-choice dictator game, not a public goods game or a repeated social dilemma. Thus, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main value is as adjacent evidence that punishment can shape norm compliance in allocation tasks, especially for younger participants, but it does not provide direct evidence for efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that paranoia is associated with reduced generosity in both the Dictator and Ultimatum Games, and with a greater tendency to punish in the Dictator Game. The effect of paranoia on punishment is mediated by harmful intent attributions. There is no evidence that paranoia increases Ultimatum Game offers (contrary to a distrust-based account), and paranoia does not reliably predict rejection of Ultimatum Game offers. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the games are not public goods games but are adjacent in that they involve costly punishment and cooperation decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The games studied are two-player allocation games (Dictator and Ultimatum), not multi-player public goods games. The main outcomes are behavioral (offers, donations, punishment/rejection), not efficiency or group payoff. However, the study does provide evidence that the availability of punishment increases punitive behavior, especially among individuals with higher paranoia, and that punishment is more likely when the cost-to-impact ratio is favorable. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that punishment may be more frequent when individuals attribute harmful intent, but it does not address efficiency or welfare outcomes.

- source: Yin, X. L., Li, J. B., Li, D. H., & Chen, S. Y. (2023). When emotional responses conflict with self-interested impulses: A transcranial direct current stimulation study of cognitive control in cooperative norm compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) is higher when punishment is costless and right DLPFC activity is inhibited (cathodal tDCS), driven by stronger negative emotional responses to norm violations. When punishment is costly, neither tDCS nor emotional response significantly affects TPP. The results suggest that the cognitive control process for norm compliance involves a conflict between emotional impulses to punish and self-interested motives to avoid cost. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological mechanisms of third-party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma variant, especially the role of cost in moderating punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that costly punishment may be less frequent and less sensitive to emotional impulses, which could imply a limited effect on efficiency, but this is not measured. The design and findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is used, not its impact on group welfare.

- source: Li, J., Zhu, L. Q., & Chen, Z. (2018). The association between punishment and cooperation in children with high-functioning autism. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Punishment (administered by a third party, the experimenter) for defection in a repeated PDG increases the rate of cooperation among both HFA and TD children. The effect is robust to IQ and group (HFA vs TD), with no significant interaction. The study provides evidence that children with HFA understand the concept of punishment and respond to it by increasing cooperation. However, the study does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a repeated two-player PDG increases cooperation rates among children, including those with high-functioning autism. However, because the primary outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games (PDG vs PGG), and the punishment is third-party and automatic for defection. The study supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperative behavior, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare effects.

- source: Meier, S., Pierce, L., Vaccaro, A., & La Cara, B. (2016). Trust and in-group favoritism in a culture of crime. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment mechanisms increase cooperation rates in one-shot prisoner's dilemma games in both high- and low-Mafia neighborhoods, but do not close the gap in trust or cooperation between the two. In high-Mafia schools, punishment intensifies in-group favoritism, leading to higher cooperation with in-group members but not with out-group members. In low-Mafia schools, punishment does not increase in-group favoritism and may even reduce it. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. The main outcomes are behavioral: trust, cooperation, and in-group bias.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma increases cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not reported. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (binary PD, not multi-person PGG), so results may partially transfer. The effect of punishment is context-dependent: in environments with strong in-group norms (high-Mafia), punishment increases in-group favoritism and does not resolve overall trust/cooperation deficits. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment may increase cooperation rates but not necessarily efficiency, especially in settings with strong parochial norms. The absence of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Sun, K. T., Liu, Y., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Evolution of trust in a hierarchical population with punishing investors. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenously imposed collective punishment in a binary trust game significantly increases trustworthiness (from 38.5% to 82.3%) and somewhat increases trust (from 28.1% to 39.6%). When the adoption of the punishment mechanism is determined by majority vote, most subjects vote against it, and the endogenous adoption does not further increase trust or trustworthiness compared to exogenous imposition. Cognitive ability predicts voting for punishment. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the main focus is on behavioral measures (trust, trustworthiness, voting).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that collective punishment can increase trustworthiness and trust in a binary trust game, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it is a binary trust game rather than a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is collective and endogenous in some treatments, but the main outcomes are behavioral. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can improve cooperative behavior, but does not allow direct inference about efficiency effects. The absence of efficiency or payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Sun, C. B., & Luo, C. (2020). Co-evolution of influence-based preferential selection and limited resource with multi-games on interdependent networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that female water point leaders (WPLs) in rural Namibia are younger and better educated than male WPLs, but are often related to traditional elites. In a trust game with third-party punishment, female WPLs punish less frequently (not statistically significant) and significantly less intensively than male WPLs. However, these differences in punishment behavior do not translate into differences in villager satisfaction with leaders. The experiment does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on gender differences in third-party punishment in a trust game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It shows that female leaders punish less, but this does not affect satisfaction. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design and findings may be useful for understanding how leader characteristics affect norm enforcement, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both Vietnamese and Chinese participants do not show negative outgroup bias toward the low-status Khmer in games involving unidirectional prosociality (dictator, envy, and third-party punishment games). In the third-party punishment game, participants are as likely to punish unfairness against Khmer as against their own group, indicating a lack of typical ingroup bias in norm enforcement. However, in games requiring mutual cooperation (trust and coalition formation), the Khmer are excluded and earn less, reflecting negative outgroup effects. The main focus is on behavioral outcomes and trait perceptions, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about punishment in public-goods-like settings, specifically via the third-party punishment game. It shows that punishment can be applied without strong ingroup bias, especially toward low-status, warm outgroups. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that social context and group perceptions can moderate punishment behavior, but the lack of efficiency data limits its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods games.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2017). Effects of Gender on Costly Punishment. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that costly punishment is used frequently in a two-player prisoner's dilemma with a punishment option, primarily as moralistic punishment (punishing defection after cooperating). Participants spend more money punishing defecting female partners than male partners, but this is due to higher cooperation rates with female partners, not a direct gender bias in punishment. Female participants are more likely to cooperate with and punish female partners, while male participants do not differentiate by partner gender. The presence of punishment by partners increases cooperation rates, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it compare efficiency with and without punishment in a way that would allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment is used in a two-player prisoner's dilemma, and that the opportunity to punish is strongly influenced by cooperation rates, which in turn are affected by gender dynamics. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it provide a control (no-punishment) condition for direct comparison. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation when the threat of being punished exists, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or their variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral findings may inform models of punishment and cooperation, but not efficiency prediction per se.

- source: Yudkin, D. A., Rothmund, T., Twardawski, M., Thalla, N., & Van Bavel, J. J. (2016). Reflexive Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned to a third-party role punished out-group transgressors more harshly than in-group transgressors, especially under conditions promoting reflexive judgment (fast response or cognitive load). The effect is robust across different group identity manipulations (sports teams, nationality). The punishment is costless to the punisher and only removes money from the transgressor. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The findings are about behavioral bias in punishment assignment, not about the impact of punishment on cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological determinants of punishment assignment (intergroup bias, reflexivity) in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on cooperation. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games but is a one-shot, three-player 'stealing and punishment' scenario with costless punishment. Thus, the findings are not directly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. The main relevance is for understanding when and how punishment is assigned, not its effect on group welfare.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when punishment is administered by a second party (the direct victim of a transgression), it is perceived as more biased and is significantly more likely to provoke counter-punishment than when administered by a third party. Specifically, second-party punishment is 66.3% more likely to trigger counter-punishment, and the loss in earnings due to counter-punishment is 64.6% higher for second-party punishers. The experiment uses a one-shot 'taking game' with punishment and counter-punishment stages, but does not measure or report group efficiency or total welfare. The findings highlight the importance of the source of punishment in determining the social costs of norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the identity of the punisher (second-party vs. third-party) is a critical moderator of the likelihood and severity of counter-punishment, which can increase the costs of norm enforcement. However, because the experiment is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that punishment by affected parties may be less effective (or more costly) due to increased counter-punishment, but the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes for use in prediction tasks.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Pepper, G. V., & Nettle, D. (2014). Local norms of cheating and the cultural evolution of crime and punishment: a study of two urban neighborhoods. *Peerj*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a 3PP game, residents of a high-crime, low-trust neighborhood (B) are more likely to steal and less likely to punish theft than those in a low-crime, high-trust neighborhood (A). Perceived cheating (descriptive norm violation) is higher in B and predicts both more theft and less expectation of punishment. Experimentally manipulating perceived norms in B increases expectation of punishment. The cost of punishment (subjective value of £2) and trust both moderate willingness to punish. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but mean payoffs are similar across neighborhoods. The results support the idea that local norms and trust shape antisocial behavior and punishment, with potential feedback loops.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not use a standard public goods game or directly analyze efficiency, it shows that punishment is less likely to be used in low-trust, high-norm-violation environments, and that expectation of punishment can be increased by manipulating perceived norms. The cost of punishment and trust are important moderators. For prediction, this suggests that punishment may be less effective at increasing efficiency in environments with low trust and high perceived norm violation, and that the subjective cost of punishment matters. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the evidence is indirect and should be used with caution.

- source: Güth, W., Kliemt, H., Levati, M. V., & von Wangenheim, G. (2007). On the coevolution of retribution and trustworthiness:: An (indirect) evolutionary and experimental analysis. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that most participants behave trustworthily (exert effort) and that a small but significant share of punishment (revenge/suing) persists, even among those without monetary inducement. The share of trustworthiness and punishment does not depend on the number of monetarily induced 'moral' players or the order of treatments. There is a weak positive correlation between observed untrustworthiness and subsequent punishment. Monetary inducement increases the likelihood of trustworthiness and punishment among targeted individuals, but not to the level predicted by rational choice. The main outcomes are behavioral; the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated trust game with costly punishment, the availability of punishment leads to persistent (though low) rates of punishment and high rates of trustworthiness, even without material incentives. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The behavioral findings suggest that punishment can support trustworthiness, but the lack of payoff analysis limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Behnk, S., Barreda-Tarrazona, I., & García-Gallego, A. (2018). Punishing liars-How monitoring affects honesty and trust. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a severe, cost-free punishment mechanism for dishonesty in a sender-receiver game leads to a large increase in honest messages and trust by receivers, compared to a baseline with no punishment. This effect persists even when the probability of detecting dishonesty is reduced to 10%, except at a moderate detection probability (25%), where honesty is lower. The deterrence effect of punishment is less pronounced when the sender's potential gain from deception is high. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the behavioral results indicate that punishment can sustain high levels of honesty and trust with minimal monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a principal-agent (sender-receiver) environment with asymmetric information, the availability of severe, cost-free punishment for dishonesty can dramatically increase honest behavior and trust, even with low monitoring (detection) probabilities. However, the game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, and efficiency or group payoff is not measured. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like environments, the results suggest that punishment mechanisms with high severity and low cost may have strong positive effects on pro-social behavior, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGGs is indirect. The findings are most relevant for settings where the main concern is honesty/truth-telling under asymmetric information, rather than contribution to a public good.

- source: de Quervain, D. J. F., Fischbacher, U., Treyer, V., Schelthammer, M., Schnyder, U., Buck, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). The neural basis of altruistic punishment. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when participants are given the opportunity to punish defectors in a trust game, they frequently do so, even at a personal cost. Effective (costly) punishment, as opposed to symbolic (non-impactful) punishment, is associated with increased activation in the dorsal striatum (caudate nucleus), a brain region implicated in reward processing. The amount of money spent on punishment correlates with the level of caudate activation, suggesting that anticipated satisfaction motivates punishment. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment compared to costless punishment, but does not eliminate it. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, but does show that punishment is used and is psychologically rewarding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, individuals are willing to pay to punish defectors, and that this behavior is motivated by anticipated satisfaction. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total welfare, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a trust game with a punishment stage. The findings support the idea that costly punishment will be used when available, but do not provide direct evidence about whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency/intensity, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Vollan, B., Blanco, E., Steimanis, I., Petutschnig, F., & Prediger, S. (2020). Procedural fairness and nepotism among local traditional and democratic leaders in rural Namibia. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both traditional chiefs and democratically elected leaders in rural Namibia are similarly likely to implement democratic decision-making procedures and are similarly unlikely to engage in nepotistic punishment. Chiefs may be slightly more likely to choose democratic procedures and less likely to be lenient toward relatives in punishment, but these differences are not statistically robust. The main outcomes are behavioral (procedural choice, nepotism in punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The punishment task is a trust game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game. The study suggests that, in this context of legal pluralism, traditional leaders are not more autocratic or nepotistic than elected leaders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on punishment behavior (altruistic punishment) and procedural fairness in a trust game with third-party punishment, not in a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does show that, in a real-world setting, leaders' willingness to punish norm violations is not strongly affected by whether they are democratically elected or traditional chiefs, and that nepotism in punishment is low for both. The design and context differ from standard PGGs, so the transfer value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited.

- source: Takahashi, R., & Tanaka, K. (2021). Social punishment for breaching restrictions during the COVID-19 pandemic. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment against stores that breach social norms (COVID-19 restrictions), as evidenced by a significant reduction in donations to such stores at personal cost. The average reduction for norm-breakers was about 35 yen (11% of the average donation). However, when participants were told that norm-breaking stores adhered to safety guidelines or faced bankruptcy risk, the level of costly punishment was reduced to be statistically indistinguishable from the control (complier) group. The results suggest that punishment for norm-breaking is sensitive to the presence of competing or contrasting norms, but the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the willingness to punish at a cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and behavioral drivers of costly punishment in a norm-violation context, but does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as in public goods games. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why individuals engage in costly punishment, and how this can be moderated by information about other norms. However, because the experiment is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The main value is in showing that punishment can be reduced by introducing competing normative considerations.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Gurven, M., & Tracer, D. (2011). The 'spiteful' origins of human cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that: (1) Small-scale societies are less generous in the Dictator Game and less likely to engage in third-party punishment, but are equally likely to engage in second-party punishment (Ultimatum Game) as large societies. (2) In large societies, third-party punishment is more common and even exceeds second-party punishment, suggesting a greater role for norm enforcement. (3) Generosity and punishment behaviors are correlated across games and societies. The findings challenge the view that strong reciprocity (including third-party punishment) is universal, suggesting that 'spiteful' second-party punishment is more fundamental in small-scale societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not use a PGG or report efficiency-related outcomes. However, it provides informative context about the prevalence and type of punishment (second- vs third-party) across societies, which may be relevant for understanding the social and cultural moderators of punishment effects in related game environments. The results suggest that the willingness to punish (especially second-party) is widespread, but the emergence of third-party punishment and higher generosity is more characteristic of large, complex societies. For prediction tasks, this implies that the social context (e.g., group size, societal complexity) may moderate the type and frequency of punishment, but the direct impact on efficiency in PGG-like games is not addressed.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barr, A., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Ensminger, J., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, J., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., McElreath, R., & Tracer, D. (2008). More 'altruistic' punishment in larger societies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the level of third-party punishment (as measured by MAO) is significantly higher in larger and more complex societies. Both local group size and ethnic group size are strong predictors of the willingness to punish unfair offers in a third-party punishment game. The results suggest that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, possibly as a response to increased anonymity and collective action problems. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the relationship between group size and the prevalence of third-party punishment in dictator-like games, which are adjacent to public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that the willingness to punish norm violations increases with group size and social complexity, which may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment is more likely to be used or effective. For the prediction task, this paper is mainly informative about the social and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Schildberg-Hörisch, H., & Strassmair, C. (2012). An Experimental Test of the Deterrence Hypothesis. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing small or intermediate punishment incentives can increase the amount stolen compared to no punishment, contrary to the deterrence hypothesis. Only very strong punishment incentives reduce stealing. The effect is driven by heterogeneity: 'selfish' subjects respond to incentives as predicted, but 'fair-minded' subjects increase stealing when incentives are introduced, possibly due to crowding out of fairness. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, as the design is not a public goods game but a dyadic stealing game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on norm-violating behavior (stealing) is non-monotonic: small and intermediate punishments can increase violations, while only strong punishments deter. However, because the design is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main lesson is that punishment can backfire under some conditions, especially when intrinsic motivation is present, but the mapping to efficiency outcomes in multi-player PGGs is indirect.

- source: Rostovtseva, V. V., Puurtinen, M., Salinas, E. M., Cox, R. F. A., Groothuis, A. G. G., Butovskaya, M. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2023). Unravelling the many facets of human cooperation in an experimental study. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that individuals are consistent in their prosocial and punishment-related decisions across a variety of economic games, but that punishment is not correlated with other prosocial tendencies. The appearance of interaction partners (as judged by facial attributes) systematically affects prosocial behavior in most games, but not in the punishment game. There is no evidence that facial appearance predicts actual prosocial behavior. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a suite of economic games including a punishment game, the tendency to punish is not associated with other prosocial behaviors and is not influenced by partner appearance. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment behavior is psychologically and behaviorally distinct from other forms of cooperation, and that its drivers may differ from those of prosociality. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always align with higher prosociality, but provides no direct evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Tang, Z. X., Qu, C., Hu, Y., Benistant, J., Moisan, F., Derrington, E., & Dreher, J. C. (2023). Strengths of social ties modulate brain computations for third-party punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment increases as the social distance between the punisher and the norm violator increases. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment assigned, but the effect of social distance remains robust. Computational modeling shows that participants integrate social distance, inequity aversion, and cost to punish when making punishment decisions. fMRI results identify brain regions involved in these computations. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the willingness to punish norm violators depends on the social distance between the punisher and the violator, and that costliness of punishment reduces punishment levels. However, because the experiment is a third-party punishment variant of the Dictator Game (not a public goods game), and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of social distance and cost, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that impulsive choice and altruistic punishment are positively correlated: individuals who are more impulsive (prefer immediate rewards) are more likely to reject unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game, incurring personal cost to punish norm violations. Lowering serotonin via tryptophan depletion increases both impulsive choice and altruistic punishment, and the magnitude of these increases is correlated across individuals. The results suggest that altruistic punishment is more impulsive/emotion-driven than self-controlled. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it uses the Ultimatum Game and focuses on individual punishment behavior rather than group outcomes. However, it provides informative context about the psychological drivers of costly punishment, suggesting that impulsivity and emotional responses (modulated by serotonin) can increase the frequency of costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this implies that environments or manipulations that increase impulsivity or emotional arousal may increase punishment rates, but the effect on efficiency cannot be inferred from this study.

- source: Im, C., & Lee, J. (2022). On the fragility of third-party punishment: The context effect of a dominated risky investment option. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when a third party in a dictator game is offered a dominated risky investment option in addition to the punishment and safe options, both the frequency and amount of punishment decrease significantly compared to when only punishment and safe options are available. The demand for investment increases when all options are available, indicating a substitution effect. The results are robust to risk attitudes and to making the investment option strictly dominated (negative expected return). The findings support the hypothesis that context effects (specifically, the compromise effect) can undermine third-party punishment, even when the alternative is unattractive in both material and psychological terms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of additional, even irrelevant, choice options can reduce the use of third-party punishment in dictator-game-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design of the choice set (e.g., presence of unrelated alternatives) can moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation or norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the fragility of punishment as an enforcement tool in the presence of context effects.

- source: Wang, C. S., Sivanathan, N., Narayanan, J., Ganegoda, D. B., Bauer, M., Bodenhausen, G. V., & Murnighan, K. (2011). Retribution and emotional regulation: The effects of time delay in angry economic interactions. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across five lab experiments, the authors show that introducing a time delay before making punishment decisions (e.g., rejecting unfair offers or punishing norm violators) generally reduces costly punishment, but only if the delay is accompanied by distraction or cognitive reappraisal. If participants ruminate during the delay, punishment increases. The effect is mediated by negative emotions (anger). The experiments use one-shot or single-decision ultimatum games and third-party punishment trust games, with punishment measured as rejection rates or points spent. No direct group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, but the reduction in costly punishment implies less wasteful destruction of resources. The findings highlight the importance of emotional regulation in moderating punitive impulses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the context and psychological state of punishers (e.g., time delay, distraction, rumination) can strongly moderate the frequency and magnitude of costly punishment. While the games are not public goods games, the results suggest that interventions reducing negative emotion or promoting reappraisal/distraction could reduce inefficient punishment and thus improve group payoffs. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, and the games are not repeated or multi-player PGGs, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence for prediction tasks.

- source: Lo Gerfo, E., Gallucci, A., Morese, R., Vergallito, A., Ottone, S., Ponzano, F., Locatelli, G., Bosco, F., & Lauro, L. J. R. (2019). The role of ventromedial prefrontal cortex and temporo-parietal junction in third-party punishment behavior. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that modulating neural activity in the VMPFC increases the tendency to punish unfair behavior (altruistic punishment), while modulating the TPJ increases the tendency to punish fair behavior (antisocial punishment) in a third-party punishment game. The behavioral outcomes are measured as the decision to punish and the amount of punishment, not as group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report on the effect of punishment on group welfare, efficiency, or aggregate earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are limited to individual punishment behavior in a third-party context, with no analysis of how punishment affects group outcomes or efficiency. The game is a modified Dictator Game, not a standard PGG or repeated PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as context for the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., & Zettler, I. (2009). Pillars of cooperation: Honesty-Humility, social value orientations, and economic behavior. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that the possibility of punishment (ultimatum game vs dictator game) leads to more fair allocations, especially among individuals low in Honesty-Humility. High Honesty-Humility individuals are fair regardless of punishment possibility. Social value orientation partially mediates these effects. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all games are one-shot, hypothetical, and not incentivized with real payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the introduction of punishment (recipient's power to reject) increases fairness in allocation, particularly among those predisposed to selfishness. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, using dictator and ultimatum games, and the outcome is individual allocation, not group welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can shift behavior toward fairness, but the lack of payoff-based outcomes and the hypothetical, one-shot nature of the games limit its direct predictive value for efficiency in repeated, incentivized PGG-like environments.

- source: Bartos, V., & Levely, I. (2021). Sanctioning and trustworthiness across ethnic groups: Experimental evidence from Afghanistan. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of scarcity (lean season), participants are significantly less willing to engage in costly third-party punishment to enforce sharing norms, as measured by a lower minimum acceptable offer (MAO) in the TPPG. However, the amount shared by dictators (Player A) in both the DG and TPPG remains stable across seasons, indicating that sharing behavior is robust to scarcity even as enforcement weakens. The results are robust to various controls and alternative explanations. No direct analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish norm violations (i.e., enforce sharing) is sensitive to resource scarcity, but actual sharing behavior is not. However, because the design is a one-shot dictator/TPPG (not a repeated public goods game), and because no group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment may become less frequent or intense under scarcity, but this does not necessarily reduce sharing in the short run. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be context-dependent and potentially weaker under scarcity, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Marlowe, F. W. (2009). Hadza Cooperation - Second-Party Punishment, Yes; Third-Party Punishment, No. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The Hadza, unlike participants from larger or more complex societies, rarely engage in costly third-party punishment in experimental games. Offers in all games are low, and Hadza proposers tend to maximize their own income. Second-party punishment (rejection of low offers in the UG) is frequent, but third-party punishment (punishing low offers to others in the TPPG) is infrequent. The paper finds that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, suggesting that the scale and complexity of society may moderate the use of third-party punishment. Group earnings are low due to frequent rejections and low offers, but efficiency or group payoff is not the main focus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not use a public goods game or directly report efficiency, it shows that in a closely related allocation game (TPPG), the availability of third-party punishment does not increase offers or group earnings among the Hadza, because third-party punishment is rarely used. In small-scale societies with low third-party punishment, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on societal context and the willingness to use punishment, which may be low in small, egalitarian groups. The results are less informative for standard PGGs but suggest caution in assuming punishment will increase efficiency in all contexts.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: MDD patients exhibit a negative emotional bias and are more likely to reject moderately unfair offers in the ultimatum game compared to healthy controls. This over-sanctioning is not explained by differences in fairness perception or cognitive deficits. The tendency persists after 6 weeks of treatment and is associated with depression severity, impulsivity, and anxiety. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral pattern suggests increased personal costs due to more frequent punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that negative emotional states (major depression) can increase the frequency of costly punishment in a bargaining game (ultimatum game). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate game design dimensions relevant to public goods games. The findings suggest that individual differences in emotional state can moderate punishment behavior, but do not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled in public goods or similar games. The study is most useful for understanding psychological moderators of punishment, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency from game design parameters.

- source: Ouss, A., & Peysakhovich, A. (2015). When Punishment Doesn't Pay: Cold Glow and Decisions to Punish. *Journal of Law & Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across a series of lab experiments, the authors show that individual punishment decisions are driven by 'cold glow' (private satisfaction from punishing) rather than by optimizing social efficiency. When punishment costs are shared by the group, individuals assign higher punishments than when they pay the cost themselves, resulting in over-punishment and excessive social cost. When the probability of apprehension is low, punishers do not increase punishment to compensate, so deterrence is insufficient and social efficiency is not achieved. When multiple punishers can assign punishment, their decisions are not crowded out by others' punishments, leading to excessive total punishment. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, taking behavior), but the implications for efficiency are discussed in terms of deviation from optimal deterrence and social cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on institutional details: (1) If punishment costs are externalized (group pays), enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to over-punishment and excessive social cost. (2) If the probability of apprehension is low, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency because punishers do not adjust their behavior, leading to under-deterrence. (3) When multiple punishers are present, lack of crowding out can lead to excessive total punishment and lower efficiency. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effect is moderated by cost structure, probability of apprehension, and the number of punishers. For prediction, the presence of group-funded punishment, low apprehension probability, or multiple punishers should be expected to reduce or even reverse the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Pan, X. F., & Houser, D. (2019). Why trust out-groups? The role of punishment under uncertainty. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds an asymmetric effect of punishment on trust: punishment opportunities increase non-social group investors' trust towards out-group trustees, but have no effect for social group investors or for in-group trust. Social group members are less likely to punish, and their trust in out-group members is higher even without punishment. The effect of punishment on trust is mediated by beliefs about trustee reciprocity. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare are reported, though the discussion suggests that social group formation may reduce the need for costly punishment and thus could improve efficiency in principle.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how group identity formation and punishment opportunities interact to affect trust and punishment behavior in a trust game with hidden action. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms (trust, punishment, beliefs) rather than payoff-based outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, but not a direct PGG or linear public goods game. Use this paper for qualitative insight into how punishment and group identity interact, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Li, Z., Hu, G. D., & Li, Q. Q. (2023). Impact of Social Identity Complexity in Unfair Events on Intergroup Bias in Third-Party Fairness Maintenance. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when third parties are presented with multiple social identities (vs. a single group identity) for the parties involved in unfair dictator game allocations, they show less punitive behavior toward outgroup violators and more compensatory behavior toward outgroup victims. However, the treatment does not affect behavior toward ingroup members. The reduction in intergroup bias is thus achieved by reducing outgroup hate, not by reducing ingroup love. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes related to punishment and compensation choices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social identity complexity can moderate the behavioral expression of punishment and compensation in third-party fairness maintenance, specifically by reducing outgroup hate. However, because the study does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in suggesting that features which increase perceived social identity complexity may reduce punitive behavior toward outgroup members, which could, in principle, affect efficiency if such behaviors are costly, but this is not tested or quantified in terms of group payoff.

- source: Li, M. P., Shangguan, C. Y., Shi, H. Q., & Lu, J. M. (2021). Watching Eyes Triggers Third-Party Punishment: The Role of Emotion Within the Eyes. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that displaying images of eyes (vs. control images) increases the frequency of third-party punishment in a modified dictator game, and that negative (angry) eyes further increase punishment compared to positive (happy) eyes. The effect is robust across varying degrees of unfairness in the dictator's offer. The outcome measured is the rate of punishment decisions, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that social cues of observation and negative evaluation can increase norm enforcement via punishment, but do not address the impact on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that social cues (watching eyes) and their emotional valence can increase the frequency of third-party punishment in a dictator game context. However, it does not provide data on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a third-party dictator game with costly punishment, but the outcome is behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based. The findings may be useful for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gambetta, D., & Przepiorka, W. (2019). Sharing Compromising Information as a Cooperative Strategy. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
