  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when a punishment mechanism is available (i.e., when norm-enforcement is possible), cooperation among less cooperative types ('hawks') increases, especially if both parties can mutually reveal their compromising information and thus make themselves vulnerable to punishment. In conditions without punishment, hawks rarely cooperate with each other. The effect is strongest when both the revelation of compromising information and the ability to punish are present and mutually chosen. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, defection, information sharing), not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment opportunities can enable cooperation among otherwise uncooperative individuals, but the paper does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a trust-game-like environment can increase cooperation among less cooperative individuals, especially when mutual vulnerability is possible. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as indirect support for the prediction task. The game is a one-shot trust game variant, not a standard repeated public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured. The punishment mechanism is costly and only available in certain conditions. The results suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains (if any) is not reported.

- source: Alam, R., & Rai, T. S. (2025). Profitable third- party punishment destabilizes cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that profitable third-party punishment (i.e., paying punishers to punish) destabilizes cooperation: dictators are less likely to share when punishers are paid, both before and after any punishment is experienced. This effect is robust across multiple experimental designs, including repeated games and conditions optimized for reinforcement learning. Even when receivers (who benefit from cooperation) can choose the punishment regime, they systematically select profitable punishment, reducing their own expected earnings by about 20%. The negative effect of profitable punishment on cooperation is only eliminated when the game structure makes antisocial punishment impossible and this is clearly signaled. The main outcome is cooperation rate, but expected receiver earnings are also reported, showing a negative welfare effect. The study highlights the importance of the communicative function of punishment and the dangers of introducing profit motives into punishment systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing profit motives for punishment (i.e., making punishment profitable for the punisher) can reduce cooperation and lower welfare, even when punishment is otherwise available. While the experiments use dictator game variants rather than standard public goods games, the mechanisms are closely related: the presence and structure of punishment, and especially the motives signaled by punishment, are critical moderators of its effect on cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment is not always beneficial for efficiency—if punishment is profitable for the punisher, it may reduce efficiency compared to both unpaid punishment and no punishment. The effect is robust to repeated play and persists even when learning is optimized. The results also show that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether antisocial punishment is possible) is a key moderator. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than group efficiency, and the game is a dictator game variant, direct quantitative predictions for public goods games should be made with caution, but the qualitative direction is clear: profitable punishment can backfire.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Espín, A. M., Exadaktylos, F., & Herrmann, B. (2014). Fair and unfair punishers coexist in the Ultimatum Game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both prosocial and antisocial individuals engage in costly punishment in the Ultimatum Game, as measured by rejection of unequal offers. Prosocial punishers are those who also behave fairly in the Dictator Game, while antisocial punishers are unfair as dictators but still reject unequal offers in the Ultimatum Game. The coexistence of these types is robust across two large-scale experiments (one with a representative city sample, one with university students). The paper highlights that antisocial punishment may undermine the efficiency and stability of social systems, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it focuses on the motivations behind punishment in the Ultimatum Game. However, it offers important contextual evidence that not all punishment is prosocial or norm-enforcing; antisocial punishment can exist and may have negative implications for efficiency in broader social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency, especially if antisocial motives are prevalent. The findings are most relevant for understanding heterogeneity in punishment motives, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Koenig, B. L., & Riley, C. M. (2017). To what reference point do people calibrate cost-free, third-party punishment?. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three experiments, participants assigned preferred punishments (fines) to a perpetrator in a theft scenario, with victim loss and perpetrator gain manipulated independently. Both victim loss and perpetrator gain influenced punishment preferences, with participants tending to select the larger of the two as a reference point. On average, preferred punishments exceeded the sum of victim loss and perpetrator gain. A substantial minority of participants preferred punishments much larger than this outcome differential, while a small minority preferred no punishment. When asked about compensation for the victim (rather than punishment for the perpetrator), participants' preferred amounts were lower and rarely exceeded the outcome differential. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment preferences are not simply proportional to harm or gain, but often exceed both, and that there is considerable heterogeneity in punitive preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it is informative about the psychological calibration of punishment preferences in third-party contexts, showing that people often prefer punishments that more than offset both victim loss and perpetrator gain. This suggests that, in environments where third-party punishment is possible and costless, punishment levels may be set high, potentially deterring transgressions more strongly than would be predicted by models based solely on harm or gain. The findings are most relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Lönnqvist, J. E., Rilke, R. M., & Walkowitz, G. (2015). On why hypocrisy thrives: Reasonable doubt created by moral posturing can deter punishment. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in ultimatum games, proposers can reduce the likelihood of being punished for unfair offers by claiming that a random process (coin flip) determined the outcome. This effect holds even when responders do not believe the claim and is observed for both direct victims (responders) and third-party observers (judges). The reduction in punishment is not explained by responders' self-interest or gratitude. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total earnings, but the findings suggest that moral posturing can deter punishment and thus may allow unfair behavior to persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of plausible deniability (moral posturing) can reduce the frequency of punishment in bargaining games, even when punishment is available and even if the claim is not believed. However, the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of uncertainty or doubt about intent, rather than the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Chen, M. L., Zhu, X. L., Zhang, J., Ma, G. P., & Wu, Y. H. (2021). Neural correlates of proposers' fairness perception in punishment and non-punishment economic games. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Proposers overwhelmingly choose absolutely unfair offers in the dictator game (non-punishment), but shift to more relatively unfair or fair offers in the ultimatum game (punishment). The presence of punishment leads to more fair behavior by proposers, consistent with the idea that punishment deters unfairness. Neural data (MFN amplitude) and self-reported guilt indicate that proposers experience cognitive conflict and guilt when making unfair offers, even when punishment is not possible. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (ultimatum game) leads to more fair offers by proposers compared to the absence of punishment (dictator game). However, because the study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. It is informative for predicting behavioral changes (offer distributions) under punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task. The design is adjacent to public goods games, but not a direct variant.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2018). Heterogeneous groups overcome the diffusion of responsibility problem in social norm enforcement. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that diffusion of responsibility (lower individual sanctioning rates as group size increases) is present in symmetric groups but is largely overcome in heterogeneous groups where one member has a lower cost of sanctioning. Over repeated rounds, groups tacitly coordinate on the strong member to sanction, leading to higher and more efficient norm enforcement. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (sanctioning rates), not payoff-based efficiency or group welfare. The findings suggest that group heterogeneity can mitigate the bystander effect in norm enforcement, but do not provide direct evidence on how this affects group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how group size and heterogeneity in punishment cost affect the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement in a volunteer's dilemma context. However, it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings suggest that introducing heterogeneity in punishment cost can increase the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement, especially in larger groups, but the translation to efficiency or welfare outcomes in standard PGGs is indirect.

- source: Roberts, S. C., Vakirtzis, A., Kristjánsdóttir, L., & Havlícek, J. (2013). Who Punishes? Personality Traits Predict Individual Variation in Punitive Sentiment. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individual differences in willingness to punish a transgressor in a public-goods-like scenario are predicted by personality traits, especially lower Agreeableness. Other traits (Conscientiousness, Extraversion) have weaker or more limited associations. The study uses hypothetical scenarios where participants indicate their anger, desire to punish, and willingness to pay to punish a free-rider, but no actual economic game is played and no payoff or efficiency data are collected. The findings are relevant for understanding who is likely to engage in costly punishment, but do not inform the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or variants. It does not manipulate or measure any game design dimensions relevant to efficiency, nor does it report control or treatment efficiency. Its findings are about individual differences in punitive sentiment, not about the consequences of punishment for group outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to predict efficiency effects of punishment based on game design dimensions.

- source: Guo, R., Ding, J. S., & Wu, Z. (2020). How intergroup relation moderates group bias in Third-Party Punishment. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that third-party observers punish selfish allocators more than fair ones, and punish outgroup members more harshly than ingroup members, demonstrating group bias in norm enforcement. Competition between groups increases punishment toward outgroup selfish allocators when the cost of punishment is low, but this effect is eliminated when the cost is high. The results support the 'mere-preference hypothesis' of group bias and show that cost-benefit analysis moderates the effect of intergroup relations on punishment behavior. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about how punishment behavior is moderated by intergroup relations and punishment cost in a third-party punishment context, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the findings suggest that punishment behavior (and thus potential effects on efficiency) may be more sensitive to intergroup competition and cost-benefit ratios, especially in settings where group identity and outgroup status are salient. However, since efficiency is not measured, the paper's value is mainly in informing behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A. (2008). The price you pay: cost-dependent reputation effects of altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who invest more in costly third-party punishment are perceived as more fair and trustworthy, and are subsequently entrusted with more money in a trust game. This effect is contingent on the cost being a reliable signal (i.e., known relative to endowment). The effect is mediated by perceptions of fairness, not by general likability or generosity. The study does not measure group efficiency or public goods provision, but rather the reputational and trust consequences for punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide indirect evidence that costly punishment can enhance the punisher's reputation and increase trust from others, but only when the cost is interpretable as a reliable signal. For prediction tasks focused on group efficiency in PGGs, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that punishment may have reputational benefits for the punisher, which could, in theory, affect cooperation and efficiency in repeated or reputation-sensitive environments, but it does not test this directly. The design and outcomes are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Perugini, M., Gallucci, M., Presaghi, F., & Ercolani, A. P. (2003). The personal norm of reciprocity. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that individual differences in positive and negative reciprocity (as measured by the PNR questionnaire) predict how people allocate money to others in response to fair or unfair prior actions, both in hypothetical and incentivized settings. Positive reciprocity predicts greater rewarding of positive behavior, while negative reciprocity predicts greater punishment of negative behavior. These effects are robust across cultures (Italy and UK) and are distinct from general prosocial orientation or Big Five personality traits. However, the studies do not directly examine group efficiency, total welfare, or the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes in repeated public goods or social dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in the propensity to punish or reward (reciprocity norms) influence how people respond to others' actions in allocation games, including costly punishment and reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings are most useful for understanding heterogeneity in punishment/reward behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. The allocation tasks are adjacent to PGGs but lack repeated group interaction, explicit MPCR, or group efficiency measures.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2005). Norm enforcement among the Ju/'hoansi Bushmen - A case of strong reciprocity?. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is a frequent and central mechanism for enforcing social norms among the Ju/'hoansi, with most norm enforcement occurring through criticism, mockery, or coalition-based verbal sanctions rather than material or violent means. The costs of punishment are generally low due to cultural mechanisms that distribute costs, allow restraint, and minimize escalation, but are not negligible. Punishment is more often used than reward, and is primarily aimed at maintaining group harmony and curbing disruptive behavior rather than strictly deterring free-riding. The study finds some support for the strong reciprocity hypothesis, as individuals sometimes incur costs to punish norm violators even when not directly benefiting. However, the outcomes of punishment are mixed, with only a minority of cases resulting in visible reform. The study does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or material payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar experimental settings. Its relevance is primarily in providing ethnographic context and qualitative insight into how punishment operates in a real-world cooperative society, including the social costs, coalition dynamics, and norm enforcement mechanisms. It does not report or allow estimation of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not manipulate or systematically vary game design dimensions. Therefore, it should not be used as a quantitative datapoint for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games, but may inform qualitative understanding of punishment's social functions and costs.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Female cleaner fish cooperate more with unfamiliar males. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that female cleaner fish behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) when paired with unfamiliar males, who are more aggressive in punishing cheating than familiar males. Preventing male punishment reduces female cooperation. The effect of punishment on female cheating is short-lived, with no significant long-term effect. Male cooperative behavior is not affected by partner familiarity, likely because females do not punish males. The results suggest that in asymmetric interactions, harsher punishment from unfamiliar partners can induce greater cooperation from subordinates, even when familiarity would otherwise be expected to promote cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral proxies for cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and severity of punishment can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric dyadic interactions, even when the punisher is unfamiliar. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment increases cooperation, especially in settings with asymmetric power and punishment ability. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that enabling punishment (especially effective, low-cost punishment by dominants) can increase cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the impact on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Ruff, C. C., Ugazio, G., & Fehr, E. (2013). Changing Social Norm Compliance with Noninvasive Brain Stimulation. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the threat of punishment increases norm compliance (higher transfers) in a modified dictator game, and that noninvasive brain stimulation of the rLPFC can causally modulate this effect. Anodal tDCS (increasing excitability) increases sanction-induced compliance (larger transfer difference between punishment and baseline rounds), while cathodal tDCS (decreasing excitability) reduces it. The effect is stronger in social (human) punishment contexts than in non-social (computer) contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral compliance rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment opportunities increases norm compliance in a dictator-like game, and that this effect is modulated by neural mechanisms. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The behavioral results (increased compliance under punishment) are consistent with the broader literature, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Pinto, A. I., Grutter, A. S., Wismer, S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Male cleaner wrasses adjust punishment of female partners according to the stakes. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that male cleaner wrasses adjust the severity of punishment towards female partners based on the stakes of the cheating event (client value) and the relative size of the female. More severe punishment is delivered when cheating is more costly to the male (high-value client) and when the female is similar in size to the male. In turn, females respond to more severe punishment by behaving more cooperatively (cheating less in subsequent opportunities). The results support the idea that punishment can be flexibly adjusted to circumstances and that such flexibility can influence cooperative behavior. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be flexibly and contextually applied in cooperative dilemmas, and that increased punishment can reduce cheating and increase cooperative behavior. However, because the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot directly inform quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and moderators (e.g., cost of cheating, partner asymmetry) that may influence the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2011). Punishment sustains large-scale cooperation in prestate warfare. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The Turkana sustain costly, large-scale cooperation in warfare through informal punishment of free riders. Raiding parties are large and composed of unrelated individuals from multiple social units. Free riding (desertion, cowardice, unfair loot appropriation) is common, but is often sanctioned through verbal reprimands, corporal punishment, and fines. The system relies on third-party consensus and community enforcement, not formal institutions. Norms governing warfare are group-beneficial at the ethnolinguistic scale, not just for smaller social units. While the paper provides rich evidence on the mechanisms and scale of cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct measures of efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the real-world operation of punishment and its role in sustaining cooperation at large scales, especially in the absence of formal institutions. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a controlled experimental design. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, the paper offers strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation and reduce free riding, even in large, anonymous groups. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the mechanisms and social context of punishment, rather than for parameterizing efficiency outcomes in laboratory PGGs. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective at large group sizes and in the absence of repeated interaction or kinship, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency changes due to punishment in a controlled game setting.

- source: Mulder, L. B., Verboon, P., & De Cremer, D. (2009). Sanctions and moral judgments: The moderating effect of sanction severity and trust in authorities. *European Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that severe sanctions (relative to mild or no sanctions) increase the extent to which people judge rule-breaking as morally wrong, and this effect is moderated by trust in authorities: it is present only when trust is high. In a behavioral allocation task, severe sanctions also increase social disapproval of rule-breakers (willingness to forgo personal gain to disadvantage a rule-breaker), again only when trust in authorities is high. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct economic outcome, and does not use a public goods game or a close variant. The results are relevant for understanding how punishment can shape moral norms and social enforcement, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms (moral judgment, social disapproval) that may underlie compliance and norm enforcement, especially the moderating role of trust in authorities and sanction severity. However, because the outcomes are not payoff-based and the game structure is not a PGG or close variant, the paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for contextual or mechanistic discussion, especially regarding how punishment can affect perceived legitimacy and social norms.

- source: Yang, S. S., Chen, S. J., & Liu, Y. F. (2025). Perceptions of Economic Fairness Positively Affect Altruistic Punishment. *European Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Across five studies (including lab experiments with TPP games), the paper finds that individuals with higher perceptions of economic fairness are more likely to engage in costly punishment of norm violators. This effect is causal and robust to different manipulations of fairness perceptions. However, the effect is moderated by outcome efficacy: the positive effect of fairness perceptions on punishment only appears when punishment is expected to be effective at changing behavior or upholding norms. The main dependent variable is the amount of punishment assigned (tokens spent or likelihood of sending a disapproval message), not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about psychological moderators of punishment behavior (specifically, perceptions of economic fairness and outcome efficacy), but does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes in public-goods-like games. The experimental paradigm is adjacent to PGGs (third-party punishment of unfair dictator allocations), but the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The results may inform models of when punishment is more likely to be used, but not its effect on group efficiency. Game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost are not systematically manipulated or reported, and efficiency is not measured.

- source: Brandts, J., & Charness, G. (2011). The strategy versus the direct-response method: a first survey of experimental comparisons. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that, across a wide range of experimental games (including some with punishment and cooperation), the method of elicitation (strategy vs. direct response) usually does not significantly affect behavior. However, in games involving punishment, direct response tends to result in higher punishment rates than the strategy method. The paper notes that this difference is robust across several studies, but the effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The survey also finds that differences between methods are more likely when there are fewer contingent choices or in one-shot games, but these effects are not strong. Overall, the strategy method is generally a valid tool for studying behavior, but may understate punishment rates compared to direct response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment. It suggests that the elicitation method can affect observed punishment rates, with direct response yielding higher punishment. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed, the paper does not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that studies using the strategy method may understate the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, on efficiency. When synthesizing evidence, one should be cautious about comparing results across studies using different elicitation methods, especially for punishment-related outcomes.

- source: Balafoutas, L., & Nikiforakis, N. (2012). Norm enforcement in the city: A natural field experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that some individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment to enforce social norms in one-shot, anonymous field interactions, but this is rare (11.7% overall, 4% for non-littering, 19.3% for escalator norm). Men are more likely to punish than women; the violator's height and gender do not significantly affect enforcement rates. The main reason for not punishing is fear of retaliation. The better-known (universal) norm (non-littering) is enforced less often than the environment-specific norm (escalator), despite being more widely valued. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the willingness to engage in costly punishment in real-world, one-shot norm violation settings, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings suggest that, in natural field settings, the rate of costly punishment is much lower than in laboratory public goods games, possibly due to higher perceived costs (e.g., risk of retaliation) and lack of anonymity. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the mere availability of punishment may not translate into high rates of enforcement or efficiency gains in field-like conditions. However, because no group payoff or efficiency data are reported, the paper cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the behavioral limits of punishment in naturalistic settings and the importance of context (e.g., fear of counter-punishment, norm universality) as moderators.

- source: Qirko, H. (2020). Altruistic punishment in modern intentional communities. *Interaction Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that, in modern intentional communities, free-riding is often acknowledged as a problem and most communities have rules or norms to address it. However, only about half of the communities report actually enforcing these rules through third-party punishment, and even then, enforcement is context-dependent and often flexible. The data do not support the existence of a strong, inherited predisposition to engage in costly third-party punishment. Instead, punishment behavior appears to be shaped by contextual, self-maximizing considerations. No direct measures of group efficiency, welfare, or payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It suggests that, in real-world cooperative groups with explicit rules and opportunities for punishment, actual use of costly third-party punishment is infrequent and context-dependent. This implies that the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee their use or an increase in efficiency, especially outside of laboratory or game-like settings. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding the limits of external validity for lab-based punishment effects and for highlighting the importance of contextual moderators.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2010). Self-Serving Punishment of a Common Enemy Creates a Public Good in Reef Fishes. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that (1) punishment by victim fish (chasing the blenny) reduces the likelihood of being attacked again, (2) in shoaling species, this punishment increases the probability that the blenny switches to another species, creating a public good for the group, and (3) blennies can learn to discriminate between punishers and nonpunishers, which selects against free riding. The findings are based on both field observations and controlled laboratory experiments using artificial plates to simulate fish. The outcomes are behavioral and do not include direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, but the results are relevant to understanding how punishment can create public goods in repeated natural interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, naturalistic interactions, self-serving punishment can create public good benefits for a group, and that the ability of the 'cheater' (blenny) to identify and target nonpunishers can sustain punishment even in the absence of kin structure. However, the study does not use a formal public goods game, does not manipulate standard game design dimensions (e.g., MPCR, player count, explicit payoff structure), and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms are informative for understanding the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation and public goods, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency in laboratory public goods games with or without punishment. The main value is in highlighting the importance of repeated interactions, individual identification, and self-serving motives in the emergence of public goods via punishment.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2014). The cost of cowardice: punitive sentiments towards free riders in Turkana raids. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that Turkana participants are significantly more likely to express punitive sentiments (both direct and indirect) toward hypothetical warriors who free ride (display cowardice) in combat, compared to those who fail to contribute due to inability (lack of skill). Direct sanctions include criticism and corporal punishment, while indirect sanctions include withholding social support, lending, and marriage opportunities. The findings support the idea that both direct punishment and indirect reciprocity are important mechanisms for sustaining large-scale cooperation in acephalous societies. However, the study does not measure actual payoffs, group efficiency, or the effect of punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punitive motivations toward free riders are present in a real-world, high-stakes collective action context, and that both direct and indirect sanctions are socially endorsed. However, because the study does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a controlled game, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The findings are most useful for understanding the psychological and social underpinnings of punishment in collective action, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2005). Punishment and partner switching cause cooperative behaviour in a cleaning mutualism. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that cleaner fish, when exposed to simulated punishment (chasing) or partner switching (fleeing) after eating their preferred food, learn to avoid the preferred food and instead eat more of the less preferred food. This behavioral shift is interpreted as evidence that punishment and partner switching can promote cooperative behavior in mutualistic interactions. The control group, which experienced no negative consequences, increased their preference for the preferred food. The findings support the idea that both punishment and the threat of partner switching can be effective partner control mechanisms, but the outcomes are measured in terms of behavioral change rather than direct payoffs or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and partner switching can alter behavior in a way that is consistent with increased cooperation, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, focusing on dyadic mutualism rather than group contributions and payoffs. For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper suggests that punishment and partner switching can change behavior toward more cooperative outcomes, but does not quantify the effect on efficiency or provide data on group-level payoffs. Its main value is in supporting the behavioral mechanism by which punishment can promote cooperation.

- source: Fitouchi, L., & Singh, M. (2023). Punitive justice serves to restore reciprocal cooperation in three small-scale societies. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three small-scale societies, punitive justice is primarily restorative and dyadic, not group-norm-enforcing. Third-party punishment is rare, and most interventions are aimed at mediating disputes and restoring relationships. Compensation to victims is common and often proportional to harm, especially among the Mentawai and Nuer. Kinship and interdependence reduce the likelihood or magnitude of punishment/compensation. When costs are not imposed on offenders, cooperation often breaks down and feuding may result. The paper does not use public goods games or measure efficiency, but provides evidence that punishment and compensation are more about restoring dyadic cooperation than increasing group-level efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the function of punishment in environments that are adjacent to public goods games: real-world small-scale societies with reciprocal obligations and norm violations. It suggests that, in such contexts, punishment is not primarily about increasing group efficiency or enforcing group-level cooperation, but about restoring dyadic relationships and preventing escalation. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment may depend on whether the context is more about dyadic reciprocity or group-level cooperation. However, because the paper does not use experimental PGGs or report efficiency or payoff outcomes, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGGs is limited.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Skarlicki, D. P., Hoegg, J., & Daniels, M. A. (2022). Consequentialist Motives for Punishment Signal Trustworthiness. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who express or are perceived to have consequentialist motives for punishing norm violators are rated as more trustworthy, are entrusted with more money in Trust Game scenarios, and are chosen more often as cooperation partners compared to those with deontological motives. This effect is mediated by lower perceived psychopathy. The findings are robust across different phrasings of motives, experimental vignettes, and real-world recall. However, the studies do not examine actual group cooperation rates, efficiency, or payoff-based outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding how the rationale for punishment affects the punisher's reputation and trustworthiness, which may indirectly influence cooperation, but it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding psychological mechanisms or for modeling how punishment motives affect trust and partner selection.

- source: Lopez, A. C. (2017). The Evolutionary Psychology of War: Offense and Defense in the Adapted Mind. *Evolutionary Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that (1) willingness to participate in collective aggression is predicted by expected group benefit in defense (public good) and by expected personal benefit in offense (private/club good); (2) punitive sentiment toward nonparticipants is more strongly triggered by willingness to participate in defense than in offense, supporting the idea that punishment is used to enforce participation in public goods contexts; (3) reward sentiment is also modulated by conflict type, with willingness to participate predicting reward sentiment in offense, and expected benefit predicting it in defense; (4) sex differences exist, with males generally more willing to participate, but labor sufficiency cues can increase female participation in defense; (5) labor sufficiency/insufficiency manipulations affect both willingness to participate and punitive sentiment, especially in defense. However, all outcomes are self-reported attitudes or intentions in hypothetical scenarios, not actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment is more likely to be used and to have a motivational effect in public goods (defense) contexts than in private/club goods (offense) contexts, and that the effectiveness of punishment as a recruitment/enforcement tool depends on the structure of the collective action problem and cues about labor sufficiency. However, it does not provide any direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, group payoff, or realized cooperation in public goods games or similar environments. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment will be more or less effective as a cooperation-enforcing mechanism, especially in relation to the publicness of the good and the perceived need for labor, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes. The study is best used for mechanism and moderator hypotheses, not for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Price, M. E., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2002). Punitive sentiment as an anti-free rider psychological device. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals' willingness to participate in a collective action (e.g., military draft) is a strong predictor of their desire to punish nonparticipants (free riders), but not of their desire to reward participants. Perceived self-interest in the group goal predicts pro-reward sentiment but not punitive sentiment, once willingness to participate is controlled for. The results suggest that punitive sentiment is a specialized psychological adaptation for eliminating the fitness advantage of free riders, rather than for recruiting labor or maximizing group efficiency. The study does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in a public goods game or similar environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar experimental settings. Its findings are based on survey responses to hypothetical scenarios, not on observed behavior or payoffs. However, it does provide indirect support for the idea that punitive sentiment is psychologically linked to participation in collective action, and that punishment may function to deter free riding. For the prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or measure the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games.

- source: Lumeau, M., Masclet, D., & Penard, T. (2015). Reputation and social (dis)approval in feedback mechanisms: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both private and public feedback mechanisms increase trust (amount sent) and trustworthiness (return rate) in a repeated trust game compared to a baseline with no feedback. Public feedback (where ratings are observable to future partners) is more effective than private feedback (where ratings are only seen by the recipient) in increasing these behaviors. The difference is attributed to both a higher frequency of ratings and a stronger behavioral response to public ratings. When a cost is introduced for assigning ratings, the effects persist but are weaker, especially for private feedback. The study concludes that reputation mechanisms (public feedback) are essential for sustaining high levels of cooperation and implied efficiency in environments with imperfect information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a feedback mechanism (a form of non-monetary sanction or reward) increases cooperation and implied efficiency in trust-game-like environments. Publicly observable feedback (reputation) is more effective than private feedback (social (dis)approval only). The results are robust to the introduction of a small cost for assigning feedback. For prediction tasks, the presence and observability of feedback mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency, with public/reputational mechanisms having a larger effect than private ones. However, the results are from a trust game, not a PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative direction is likely similar.

- source: Romaniuc, R., Dubois, D., Dimant, E., Lupusor, A., & Prohnitchi, V. (2022). Understanding cross-cultural differences in peer reporting practices: evidence from tax evasion games in Moldova and France. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling costly, anonymous peer reporting (whistleblowing) in a repeated tax evasion game leads to significantly higher tax compliance (i.e., a higher percentage of income declared) compared to a control condition where only social observation is possible. This effect is observed in both Moldova and France, even though Moldovan participants view whistleblowing as less socially acceptable and engage in it less frequently than French participants. The increase in compliance is attributed to the deterrent effect of the possibility of being reported, not just the actual frequency of reporting. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff directly, but the increase in compliance is a strong proxy for improved group outcomes in a public-goods-like context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costly, anonymous peer reporting mechanism in a repeated tax evasion game (a close variant of a public goods game) increases compliance rates, which is closely related to group efficiency. The effect is robust to large cross-cultural differences in the acceptability and frequency of reporting. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (here, in the form of peer reporting to a central authority) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to a control with no such mechanism, even if actual punishment is rare. The effect is not strongly moderated by cultural differences in the willingness to punish, at least in the settings studied. However, the outcome measured is tax compliance, not direct group payoff or efficiency, so the mapping is indirect.

- source: Johnson, D. B. (2016). (Please Don't) Say It to My Face! The Interaction of Feedback and Distance: Experiments with Vulgar Language. *Kyklos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The possibility of non-monetary punishment via unrestricted feedback increases proposer offers in the lab (where social distance is low), but not in online settings (where social distance is high). In the lab, known possible feedback leads to significantly higher offers, suggesting that the threat of negative feedback (punishment) can increase pro-social behavior and potentially improve monetary outcomes. However, in online/anonymous settings, the same feedback mechanism does not affect offers. The effect of punishment via feedback is thus moderated by social distance/proximity. The paper does not directly measure group efficiency as in public goods games, but the findings are relevant for understanding when non-monetary punishment can improve monetary outcomes in bargaining environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (here, non-monetary feedback) in increasing efficiency or pro-social monetary outcomes depends strongly on social distance and context. In environments with low social distance (e.g., lab, face-to-face), the threat of punishment can increase offers and thus improve monetary outcomes. In high social distance or anonymous environments (e.g., online), punishment has little or no effect on offers. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive only when social distance is low and the punishment is salient to the decision-maker. The findings are adjacent to public goods games but provide informative indirect evidence about the context-dependence of punishment effects.

- source: López-Pérez, R., & Vorsatz, M. (2010). On approval and disapproval: Theory and experiments. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: (1) control (standard PD, no feedback), (2) expectations (subjects state what they expect their co-player will think of their choice), and (3) feedback (after the PD, subjects can pay to send an approval/disapproval message to their co-player). The cooperation rate is highest in the feedback treatment (38.3%), intermediate in the expectations treatment (29.0%), and lowest in the control (22.4%), but only the difference between feedback and control is statistically significant. The study finds that the possibility of non-material sanctions (approval/disapproval) increases cooperation, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff. The effect is interpreted as arising from increased awareness of others' opinions and aversion to receiving negative feedback. The feedback mechanism is costly (10 ECU per message), but the messages have no material effect on payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the opportunity for non-material, costly approval/disapproval feedback can increase cooperation in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are adjacent to public goods games with punishment, as the game is a two-player PD and the 'punishment' is non-material and costly, with no direct effect on payoffs. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that enabling even non-material, costly feedback can increase cooperation rates, but the effect on efficiency is not measured. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence of a feedback/punishment stage, the cost of feedback, and the fact that feedback is non-material. The results suggest that both the psychological salience of being evaluated and the aversion to negative feedback are important for increasing cooperation, but the transferability to efficiency in PGGs is limited by the absence of group payoff data.

- source: Jung, Y. B., Hall, J., Hong, R. Y., Goh, T., Ong, N., & Tan, N. (2014). Payback: Effects of relationship and cultural norms on reciprocity. *Asian Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that reciprocity norms (reward and punishment) are shaped by relationship and culture. Collectivists are more generous to friends after positive treatment and less likely to punish after negative treatment (even to strangers), sometimes offering an 'olive branch' (positive or neutral response to negative treatment). Individualists are more likely to punish norm violators (destroy money at a cost to themselves) regardless of relationship. The main dependent variable is the amount given or destroyed, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not use a public goods game or a repeated multi-player setting, but rather a dyadic hybrid trust-dictator game with a single round (though participants believed there would be five rounds).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in dyadic trust/dictator game settings, especially the influence of culture and relationship on willingness to punish at a cost. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or multi-player repeated setting. The findings may inform expectations about the likelihood and intensity of costly punishment or reward in similar dyadic games, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the psychological and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Ansink, E., Tesfaye, A., Bouma, J., & Brouwer, R. (2017). Cooperation in watershed management: A field experiment on location, trust, and enforcement. *Resource and Energy Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The enforcement (punishment) treatment, which penalizes trustees for not returning at least the amount sent by trustors, increases the amount returned but does not increase trust (investment by trustors). There is no significant effect of enforcement on average payoffs. Framing the game in terms of soil conservation reduces trust. Trust (as measured in the game) is a strong predictor of actual soil conservation investments in the field. The study finds no evidence that enforcement increases cooperation or efficiency in this context, and suggests that enforcement may crowd out trust. The trust game is not a public goods game but is related as a social dilemma with externalities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment/enforcement mechanism in a trust game does not increase trust or average payoffs, and may crowd out intrinsic motivation for cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in trust-game-like environments with high baseline trust, adding punishment may not improve efficiency and could even reduce voluntary cooperation. However, the game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency is not directly measured as a share of maximum possible group payoff. The enforcement mechanism is probabilistic and only applies to the trustee role. The findings are most relevant for settings where trust and reciprocity are central, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Liu, X. S., Bao, H. L., Su, Y., & He, M. F. (2018). Evolution of cooperation through adaptive interaction in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing and increasing the probability of disconnection (β) after defection leads to a significant increase in the fraction of cooperators in both simulation and human lab experiments. The effect is nonlinear: at low β, the effect is modest, but at higher β, cooperation increases sharply. The mechanism is interpreted as a punishment for defection, as defectors are excluded from interactions and thus lose opportunities for payoff. The system can transition between states of all-defector, all-cooperator, or coexistence depending on the parameters (temptation to defect, cost, and β). While payoffs are discussed and collected, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a punishment-like mechanism (disconnection after defection) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma environment. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect. The findings suggest that enabling a punishment mechanism that reduces defectors' ability to interact (and thus earn payoff) can increase cooperation, which is likely to improve efficiency in similar environments, especially when the punishment is strong (high β). The design is adjacent to public goods games, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious. The main predictive signal is that punishment mechanisms that exclude defectors can be effective, especially when the exclusion probability is high.

- source: Mendoza, S. A., Lane, S. P., & Amodio, D. M. (2014). For Members Only: Ingroup Punishment of Fairness Norm Violations in the Ultimatum Game. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that in the ultimatum game, participants are more likely to reject marginally unfair offers from in-group members than from out-group members, even though rejection is costly to both parties. This pattern is interpreted as stricter enforcement of fairness norms within groups. The effect is most pronounced for offers that are only slightly unfair (e.g., $8/$20), and is stronger among participants with higher in-group identification. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, and the outcome is measured in terms of behavioral responses (accept/reject) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and social moderators of costly punishment in a bargaining context (ultimatum game), specifically the role of group membership and social identity. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to public goods games. The findings may inform predictions about when punishment is more likely to be used (e.g., in-group contexts, marginal norm violations), but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Truskanov, N., Emery, Y., & Bshary, R. (2020). Juvenile cleaner fish can socially learn the consequences of cheating. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Juvenile cleaner fish, after observing adults interacting with model clients (plates) that respond negatively to cheating (either by fleeing or punishing), subsequently behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) and prefer partners that are tolerant to cheating. Social learning about the consequences of cheating shapes cooperative strategies, but juveniles do not simply copy arbitrary preferences of adults. The study provides evidence that social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation in a non-human animal, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observation of punishment or negative partner responses can increase cooperative behavior in a dyadic animal model. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that the presence of observable sanctions can bias individuals toward more cooperative choices, which in analogous human or multi-player games could translate to higher efficiency if similar mechanisms operate. However, the lack of direct payoff or efficiency measurement, and the dyadic (not group) structure, limit direct transfer to the prediction of efficiency in public goods games with punishment. The main value is mechanistic: social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation.

- source: Song, X. Y., Lin, L. A., Wang, Z. C., Li, Y., & Liu, L. (2024). Preemptive strikes or not: the role of emotion on intergroup defensive aggression. *Current Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a group preemptive strike game, introducing punishment for non-contributors or assigning leadership increases the likelihood that group members will engage in preemptive attack (press the red button). This effect is mediated by increased fear (which increases attack) and hope (which decreases attack). However, the main outcome is behavioral (attack frequency), not efficiency or group payoff. The game rules indicate that preemptive attacks are costly for both groups, suggesting that increased attack rates likely reduce group efficiency, but this is not directly measured or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that introducing punishment increases costly preemptive attack behavior in a group conflict game, which by the game rules reduces group welfare. However, the study does not report efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the game structure. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment increases costly defensive aggression, efficiency may decrease. The game is not a standard public goods game, but the mechanism (punishment increasing costly group-protective action) is adjacent. Use this paper as indirect evidence that punishment can increase costly, inefficient group-protective behaviors in some intergroup settings.

- source: Molina, M., Nee, V., & Holm, H. (2023). Cooperation with Strangers: Spillover of Community Norms. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that among Chinese CEOs, stronger belief that norm violations are met with retaliatory sanctions in their business community is associated with a higher probability of cooperating with a stranger in a one-shot PD. This association is especially strong in some cities (Nanjing, Wenzhou). An online experiment with managers and nonmanagers shows that priming reciprocity norms increases cooperation with strangers in some subgroups and cities. However, the outcome is always the individual choice to cooperate, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct evidence on how punishment or norm enforcement affects efficiency or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is showing that belief in norm enforcement (especially retaliatory sanctions) is associated with higher cooperation rates in a one-shot PD. While this suggests that punishment norms can promote cooperation, the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes, and the use of a PD rather than a PGG, limits its direct applicability to the efficiency prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of punishment norms in promoting cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: DAVIS, D. D., & HOLT, C. A. (1994). EQUILIBRIUM COOPERATION IN 3-PERSON, CHOICE-OF-PARTNER GAMES. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the ability to punish by switching partners does not increase cooperation in two-stage games compared to single-stage controls, but does lead to much higher cooperation rates in ten-stage games. The increase in cooperation in longer games is attributed to both the opportunity for reputation building and the higher cost of being punished (switched away from) early in a long sequence. The punishment mechanism is salient and does not require coordination. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that salient, unilateral punishment mechanisms (switching partners) can increase cooperation rates in repeated games, but only when the game is sufficiently long to allow for reputation effects and to make the cost of being punished significant. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the number of rounds (num_rounds) is a critical moderator of punishment effectiveness on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results are most informative for predicting behavioral (cooperation) outcomes rather than efficiency, and the game is not a standard public goods game but an adjacent partner-selection game with similar strategic features.

- source: Julle-Danière, E., Whitehouse, J., Vrij, A., Gustafsson, E., & Waller, B. M. (2020). The social function of the feeling and expression of guilt. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individuals who feel more guilt after being blamed for a cooperative failure allocate more of a shared reward to their partner, regardless of friendship strength. When the victim of a transgression (Player 2) perceives their partner as guilty, they are more likely to 'punish' (i.e., take more for themselves) if the partner is a close friend, though this effect is sensitive to outliers. The expression of guilt thus has a complex social function, sometimes leading to harsher punishment from close friends.
