The main behavioral outcome is the reward split in a dictator game, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not directly address the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a one-shot dictator game after a cooperative task with induced guilt, but does not involve repeated contribution decisions, group efficiency, or explicit punishment mechanisms as in standard PGGs. The main outcome is the allocation of a fixed reward, interpreted as relationship repair or punishment, but not as efficiency. The findings suggest that social context (friendship, perceived guilt) can modulate punitive or reparative behavior, but do not provide quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games. Thus, the paper is more informative about the social and emotional moderators of punishment-like behavior than about efficiency outcomes relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Chambers, C. R. (2024). Nonmonetary Reward Systems, Counterproductive Behavior, and Responses to Sanctions in Open Collaboration Environments. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that proximity to nonmonetary reward thresholds is associated with a higher likelihood of counterproductive behavior (suspension), suggesting that reward systems can create performance pressures leading to negative behaviors. However, individuals who are suspended tend to increase their productive contributions just before suspension, and their contribution rates drop after suspension. The paper does not measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that formal sanctions may have unintended negative consequences for sustaining contributions in open collaboration environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers indirect evidence that formal sanctions (suspensions) may reduce subsequent contribution rates, which could imply a negative effect on group output or efficiency if mapped to a PGG context. The findings suggest that while sanctions may deter counterproductive behavior, they may also discourage future productive contributions, highlighting a potential tradeoff. For prediction tasks, this paper signals caution in assuming that punishment will always improve group outcomes, especially in voluntary, open collaboration settings.

- source: Gingins, S., Werminghausen, J., Johnstone, R. A., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2013). Power and temptation cause shifts between exploitation and cooperation in a cleaner wrasse mutualism. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: Cleaner wrasse strategically adjust their foraging behavior in response to the threat of early termination (sanction) and temptation to cheat. When the threat of early termination is high and the temptation to cheat is low, cleaners behave more cooperatively (eat more less-preferred items, which is analogous to providing better service). When the temptation to cheat is high and the threat is low, cleaners exploit the situation (eat more preferred items). The non-cleaner control species does not adjust its behavior in response to these manipulations. The study provides evidence that simple partner control mechanisms (sanctions) can maintain cooperation in asymmetric interactions, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the presence of a sanction mechanism (threat of early termination) can shift behavior from exploitation to cooperation, especially when the temptation to cheat is low. However, the outcomes are behavioral and not directly about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, enabling a sanction mechanism may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and whether the sanction is costly or not. The study is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which sanctions can promote cooperation, but less directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2012). Individual Differences in Foraging Strategies of Parasitic Sabre-Tooth Blennies. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individual blennies differ markedly in their foraging strategies, including how they respond to punishment by victim fish. Some blennies are more likely to switch victim species after being punished, while others are not. Laboratory experiments show that punishment can reduce the likelihood of attack or cause switching, but these effects are highly individual-specific. The paper discusses how only certain patterns of blenny response to punishment (specifically, switching to another species) would generate a public good for the victim group, while other patterns would not. However, the study does not measure group efficiency or payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment might or might not generate public goods in a natural system, depending on the behavioral rules of the punished party. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that the effect of punishment on public good provision (and thus efficiency) may depend on the behavioral heterogeneity of the punished agents and the structure of the interaction (e.g., whether punishment causes switching to other species or not).

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2010). Interactions Between Sabre-Tooth Blennies and Their Reef Fish Victims: Effects Of Enforced Repeated Game Structure and Local Abundance on Victim Aggression. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that resident reef fish species, which cannot avoid repeated interactions with parasitic blennies, are significantly more likely to show aggressive responses (interpreted as punishment) than visitor species, even after controlling for size and territoriality. Aggression is negatively correlated with local abundance (group size), both across species and within the most abundant species, suggesting that the benefits of punishment are diluted in larger groups. The authors interpret these patterns as consistent with theoretical predictions from repeated game and public goods models, but do not directly measure efficiency, group payoff, or the downstream effects of punishment on blenny behavior or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, ecological evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like settings. It supports the idea that repeated interactions (enforced by residency) increase the likelihood of punishment, and that larger group size dilutes the incentive to punish. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its main value is in supporting the importance of repeated interactions and group size as moderators of punishment behavior, which may inform priors or mechanistic expectations in related experimental PGG studies.

- source: Cisarovsky, G., Bshary, A., Bouzelboudjen, M., & Bshary, R. (2012). Spatial Group Structure as Potential Mechanism to Maintain Cooperation in Fish Shoals of Unrelated Individuals. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that anthias in natural shoals have partially non-overlapping home ranges, and blennies (parasites) show site fidelity, attacking more frequently in certain areas. Individuals more exposed to the blenny may benefit more from punishing (chasing) the blenny, making punishment potentially self-serving rather than purely altruistic. This spatial structuring could help maintain the public good of punishment even in large, unrelated groups. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or direct fitness outcomes; it infers potential payoff implications from spatial and behavioral patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that spatial structure and local exposure can moderate the effectiveness and self-serving nature of punishment in public-goods-like dilemmas in natural systems. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate game design dimensions in a controlled way. For prediction tasks, the main value is in highlighting that spatial structure and heterogeneity in exposure can affect the distribution and potential benefits of punishment, but the paper does not provide direct input for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in standard PGG designs.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schneider, F. (2010). Eyes are on us, but nobody cares: are eye cues relevant for strong reciprocity?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that subtle, implicit reputation cues (eyespots) do not increase trustees' altruistic behavior in a trust game, nor do they affect trustors' willingness to invest. In contrast, explicit pecuniary reputation incentives—where trustees' past back-transfers are revealed to future trustors—lead to a large and significant increase in both trustees' back-transfers and trustors' investments. This effect is particularly pronounced among individuals with high Machiavellianism scores. The results suggest that explicit, material reputation incentives are powerful drivers of prosocial behavior, while implicit cues without real consequences are ineffective in this context. The findings cast doubt on the hypothesis that most prosocial behavior in anonymous experiments is due to uncontrolled implicit reputation cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that explicit, material reputation incentives (a form of positive sanctioning or reward) can substantially increase prosocial behavior and, by implication, group efficiency in trust-based economic games. However, it also shows that implicit cues (such as eyespots) have no such effect. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of explicit reputation or reward mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency, while subtle cues without real payoff consequences are not. The results are adjacent to public goods games: the trust game is a two-player, sequential, one-shot game repeated with random rematching, not a standard PGG, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or reward is at stake, and less so for punishment-based designs. The paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, but supports the importance of explicit incentive structures for efficiency.

- source: Bernard, M., Fanning, J., & Yuksel, S. (2018). Finding cooperators: Sorting through repeated interaction. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that in the Baseline (replacement allowed), cooperation (measured by responder return) increases over time, surpassing the Fixed Partner treatment (no replacement) in later rounds, despite similar initial levels. The Contract Restrictions treatment (no replacement, no punishment) starts with lower cooperation but converges to the Fixed Partner level in later rounds. The ability to punish by reducing transfers does not significantly increase cooperation compared to the inability to punish. Instead, the ability to sort (replace uncooperative partners) is the key driver of higher long-run cooperation. Surplus sharing becomes more equal in long-term relationships, but overall efficiency is not maximized in any treatment. The findings suggest that sorting, not punishment, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated gift-exchange games (adjacent to PGGs), the ability to punish (reduce transfers) does not significantly increase efficiency or cooperation compared to when punishment is not possible. Instead, the ability to sort (replace partners) is more important for sustaining cooperation and improving surplus. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment alone (without sorting/replacement) may not increase efficiency, and that the structure of partner matching is a critical moderator. The findings are most relevant for environments where partner selection or replacement is possible, and less so for standard PGGs with fixed groups and explicit costly punishment. The paper's results should be used to caution against overestimating the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games without sorting opportunities.

- source: Müller, J., Schwieren, C., & Spitzer, F. (2022). How to prevent destruction - On the malleability of anti-social behavior. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper demonstrates that anti-social destruction of group resources in a laboratory setting is highly sensitive to contextual factors. When the destruction opportunity is presented in a transactional, market-like framing and is the only available activity, subjects destroy a large share of the group's resources, sharply reducing group payoff. However, if the destruction is framed as harming jointly owned property, or if subjects have a parallel, meaningful activity (real-effort task) to occupy them, destruction is almost eliminated and group payoffs are preserved. The effect of these interventions is large and robust. The study does not use a public goods game or punishment of free riders, but the findings are relevant for understanding how institutional design and framing can prevent anti-social, payoff-reducing behaviors in group settings.
  prediction_guidance: While this study does not directly test public goods games or punishment, it provides strong evidence that institutional context (such as framing and the presence of alternative activities) can dramatically affect the prevalence of anti-social, payoff-reducing behaviors. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in group games, the results suggest that interventions making the negative consequences of anti-social actions salient to all, or providing alternative meaningful activities, can greatly improve group efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where group resources can be destroyed at a cost, but the general principle—that context and framing can strongly moderate destructive or anti-social behavior—may inform predictions about punishment and efficiency in related environments.

- source: Büyükboyaci, M., Karagözoglu, E., & Küçüksenel, S. (2025). Productive and Unproductive Effort Choices in Groups and Sharing: An Experimental Study. *Managerial and Decision Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the opportunity cost of unproductive investment (i.e., making productive investment more attractive) does not significantly increase productive investment rates. Subjects' own allocation choices do not significantly affect their giving in the distribution stage, but they give less to human partners who chose unproductive investment, indicating a punishment mechanism. This punishment is not applied when the unproductive choice is made by a computer. The size of the surplus (group payoff) is determined by the sum of productive investments, but the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or compare group payoffs across treatments. The main behavioral outcome is the use of distributive punishment in response to unproductive choices by humans.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment. The design is adjacent to a PGG: resource allocation between productive and unproductive uses, with endogenous surplus and a contest for control. There is no explicit punishment stage, but distributive punishment (giving less in the dictator game) serves a similar function. The main implication is that increasing the marginal return to productive investment (analogous to MPCR) does not necessarily increase productive investment or efficiency. Punishment (via lower giving) is selectively applied to human partners who make unproductive choices, but this does not feed back to increase productive investment rates. Thus, the presence of a punishment-like mechanism does not guarantee higher efficiency in this context. For prediction, this suggests that punishment may not always improve efficiency, especially if it is not structured as a direct, costly sanction and if it does not change initial contribution behavior.

- source: Kas, J., Corten, R., & van de Rijt, A. (2020). Reputations in mixed-role markets: A theory and an experimental test. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated dyadic trust/lending game, providing information about a participant's past behavior in both roles (mixed-role reputation) increases the rate at which trust is placed and honored in the Lending Game (free exchange, where lending is costly and not compensated), but not in the Trust Game (paid exchange, where lending is compensated). Specifically, in the Lending Game, the trust rate and the 'success rate' (trust placed and honored) are significantly higher under mixed-role reputation than under single-role reputation. In the Trust Game, there is no significant difference between reputation conditions. The paper interprets these results as evidence that mixed-role reputation systems facilitate reciprocal lending and increase overall cooperation and welfare in free exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving public-goods-like environments where the main intervention is a change in the information structure (e.g., adding mixed-role reputation), this paper provides evidence that such interventions can increase efficiency-related outcomes (success rate) in free exchange settings, but not in paid exchange settings. The results are most relevant for environments where cooperation is costly and not directly compensated, and where reputation can be built across multiple roles. The findings suggest that enabling richer reputation signals (beyond single-role) can improve group outcomes in these contexts. However, the paper does not directly study classic public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, so the transfer to PGGs with punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Nakashima, N. A., Halali, E., & Halevy, N. (2017). Third parties promote cooperative norms in repeated interactions. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party intervention in repeated adversarial interactions (modeled as a Peacemaker Game) increases cooperation rates among adversaries. Early intervention is more effective at establishing persistent cooperative norms than late intervention. Third parties are more likely to intervene when adversaries have a history of cooperation, and adversaries are more likely to cooperate following third party intervention. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and intervention rates), not efficiency or group payoff. The study provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which combine reward and punishment can transform competitive repeated interactions into more cooperative ones, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling third-party interventions (which combine reward and punishment) can increase cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation and intervention rates, not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as suggestive rather than directly predictive for efficiency outcomes. The game structure is adjacent to public goods games, and the intervention mechanism is not a standard peer punishment or reward system. The results suggest that early, consistent intervention is more effective at establishing cooperation than late intervention, and that the presence of a third party who can both reward and punish can transform group behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that enabling such mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency is not directly reported.

- source: Gillenkirch, R. M., & Kreienbaum, H. (2017). What guides subjective performance evaluation: Incentive alignment or norm enforcement?. *Review of Managerial Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that superiors are primarily motivated by norm enforcement: they pay more for information that allows them to reward high and punish low effort, and their allocation decisions reflect a willingness to punish norm violations. Subordinates increase their effort when they know their effort can be observed and evaluated. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total surplus, but higher effort levels in conditions with more informative evaluation suggest that group output and subordinate payoffs increase when norm enforcement is possible. However, the focus is on behavioral mechanisms (effort, information acquisition, allocation) rather than on efficiency as a formal outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling punishment (via subjective evaluation and information acquisition) increases effort and likely increases group output in a team production setting with a free rider problem. However, it does not report efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The design is adjacent to PGGs: two subordinates choose effort (costly), output is the sum of efforts plus noise, and a superior (not a group member) allocates the output. The 'punishment' is implemented via allocation decisions, not explicit costly punishment. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that mechanisms enabling norm enforcement (punishment) can increase effort and likely improve group outcomes, but the lack of direct efficiency data and the non-standard game structure limit its direct applicability to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game. However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Watson, R., Morgan, T. J. H., Kendal, R. L., van de Vyver, J., & Kendal, J. (2024). Investigating the effects of social information on spite in an online game. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants overwhelmingly chose altruism over spite when given a costly choice to increase or decrease an anonymous partner's payoff. Exposure to social information indicating others had behaved spitefully (vs. altruistically) reduced the degree of altruism, but did not substantially increase spiteful acts. The source of social information (majority vs. most successful) had no effect. The study does not report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the design is a one-shot, two-player, non-iterated game with a continuous choice from costly spite to costly altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated group games. The design is a one-shot, two-player, continuous-choice game where participants can pay to harm or help an anonymous partner, and the main outcome is a behavioral slider, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no repeated interaction, no group context, and no explicit public goods structure. The findings suggest that when both costly spite and costly altruism are available, spite is rare and social information can reduce altruism, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Salmon, T. C., & Serra, D. (2017). Corruption, social judgment and culture: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing social observability and allowing for social judgment (informal sanctions in the form of approval/disapproval messages) reduces the likelihood of corrupt behavior (offering or accepting bribes) among participants who identify with low-corruption cultures, but not among those who identify with high-corruption cultures. The effect is driven by differences in the likelihood of social disapproval: participants from high-corruption cultures are less likely to disapprove of corrupt acts. The Victim Knows treatment (where the victim is informed but cannot judge) has little effect. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the design is a one-shot, role-based bribery game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal social sanctions (social judgment) on norm-violating behavior in a one-shot, negative-externality game. While not a public goods game, it is relevant for understanding the limits of informal punishment/sanction mechanisms, especially their dependence on cultural context. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of informal sanctions in reducing norm violations (and thus potentially increasing efficiency) is highly contingent on prevailing social norms and expectations of disapproval. However, because the game is not repeated, does not feature voluntary contributions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for public goods game efficiency is limited. It is most informative for settings where informal social sanctions are the main enforcement mechanism and where cultural heterogeneity is a concern.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Mui, V. L. (2014). Coordinating Resistance Through Communication and Repeated Interaction. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that repetition alone only modestly reduces leader DAC (transgression) rates, as leaders can punish beneficiaries who resist, deterring future resistance. Communication between responders, even when non-binding and private, substantially increases the rate of coordinated resistance and reduces leader DAC. The combination of repetition and communication is most effective at reducing DAC. The study also shows that hiding responder identities from the leader (so the leader cannot target challenging beneficiaries) increases resistance. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total surplus, or group payoff as a main outcome, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The results are most relevant for understanding how communication and repeated interaction affect coordination in social dilemmas with endogenous roles and targeted punishment, rather than standard public goods games with peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the importance of communication and repeated interaction for coordinated resistance in social dilemmas with endogenous roles. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that communication between potential punishers (or resisters) can be as important as, or more important than, repetition for deterring exploitative behavior. The results highlight that the ability to target punishers (e.g., identifying who punished) can undermine the effectiveness of resistance. For prediction tasks in public goods games, the main lesson is that communication channels and anonymity of punishment/reward can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes, but it does suggest that communication is a powerful moderator.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kuwabara, K. (2015). Do Reputation Systems Undermine Trust? Divergent Effects of Enforcement Type on Generalized Trust and Trustworthiness. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Reputation systems, as a form of diffuse, non-material enforcement, reinforce generalized trust and trustworthiness more than contractual (material punishment) or relational (repeated partner) enforcement. After exposure to reputational enforcement, participants are more trusting and trustworthy in subsequent unenforced exchanges than after exposure to contracts or repeated partners. Contractual enforcement, in contrast, can undermine intrinsic motivation to trust or be trustworthy once enforcement is removed. These effects are robust across multiple studies and are supported by both behavioral and self-report measures. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the experimental paradigm is the investment game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like enforcement (contracts) and reputation systems on subsequent cooperation, but not on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that material punishment (contracts) can undermine intrinsic motivation for cooperation, while reputation systems can reinforce it. However, because the outcomes are trust/trustworthiness (not efficiency) and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The evidence is more relevant for understanding how enforcement mechanisms affect willingness to cooperate after enforcement is removed, rather than direct effects on group efficiency during enforcement.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Abbink, K., & Sadrieh, A. (2009). The pleasure of being nasty. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of subjects are willing to destroy the other player's endowment when their actions are hidden, but not when they are observable. The presence of anonymity increases anti-social behavior. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods or punishment game. The findings are relevant for understanding anti-social punishment and spite, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The game studied is a two-player, anti-social destruction game with no cooperation or group payoff structure. The main insight is that anonymity increases anti-social behavior, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The findings may be contextually relevant for understanding anti-social punishment or spiteful behavior, but do not inform the prediction task in a quantitative or directly comparable way.

- source: Geschwind, S., & Lambsdorff, J. G. (2025). Does scarcity induce hostility? An experimental investigation of common-pool resources. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when scarcity is perceived as human-induced (overexploitation), participants are more likely to destroy each other's resources in the subsequent stage, indicating increased hostility. When scarcity is exogenously imposed (low reproduction), destruction is lower, suggesting that environmental scarcity can foster restraint and cooperation. The destruction mechanism is not a standard punishment stage: destruction is costless, partially hidden (due to random 'bear' destruction), and not directly strategic. The study finds that reciprocity (retaliation for overexploitation) is a key driver of destruction, while inequality aversion plays a limited role. Overharvesting is substantial in the CPR game, leading to welfare losses, but the main focus is on behavioral responses (hostility) rather than efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency outcomes for a standard PGG with or without punishment, it shows that the presence of a destruction (punishment-like) stage increases hostile behavior when scarcity is human-induced, and decreases it when scarcity is exogenous. The destruction mechanism differs from standard costly punishment: it is costless, partially hidden, and not designed to change future behavior. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency may depend on the perceived cause of scarcity and the design of the punishment stage. The paper is most informative for understanding how the framing and cause of scarcity moderate the impact of punishment-like options on group behavior, but does not provide direct data for predicting efficiency changes from enabling punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Leppänen, I., & Hämäläinen, R. P. (2017). Emotions in a Repeated Cournot Duopoly Game: A Psychophysiological Experiment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that emotional responses are closely tied to reciprocal adjustments in a repeated Cournot duopoly game. When a player observes that the other did not cooperate (i.e., the other increased production), the player is more likely to make an upward adjustment (interpreted as punishment), and the size of this adjustment is positively correlated with emotional arousal. Disgust is associated with advantageous inequity (own one-sided gain), and positive affect is associated with mutual gains. The study provides evidence that emotions play a role in motivating reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not directly measure or report the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The Cournot duopoly is adjacent to public goods games, and punishment is implemented as endogenous behavioral adjustment rather than as a discrete, explicit punishment option.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the emotional and motivational underpinnings of reciprocal (including punitive) behavior in repeated social dilemmas with continuous strategy spaces. It suggests that endogenous punishment-like responses (upward adjustments after defection) are emotionally motivated and can be costly for both players, potentially reducing efficiency. However, because the study does not manipulate explicit punishment mechanisms or report efficiency outcomes as a function of punishment, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the psychological processes underlying endogenous punishment in adjacent game structures, but should not be used as direct quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, X. L., Han, J. H., Li, F. H., & Cao, B. H. (2018). Both Rewards and Moral Praise Can Increase the Prosocial Decisions: Revealed in a Modified Ultimatum Game Task. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants are less likely to reject unfair/self-benefiting offers (i.e., more likely to act selfishly) when doing so would result in a material loss (risk condition). When economic or moral rewards are provided for norm compliance, participants are more likely to reject unfair offers and act prosocially. The effect is stronger for economic rewards than for moral rewards, and is most pronounced for extremely unfair divisions. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral compliance with fairness norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not use a public goods game or analyze efficiency/payoff outcomes, it shows that the threat of material loss for norm compliance reduces prosocial behavior, and that rewards (especially economic) can restore norm compliance. The findings suggest that in environments where norm compliance is costly, punishment alone may not sustain cooperation, but rewards can help. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited.

- source: Cuesta, J. A., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Ferrer, A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2015). Reputation drives cooperative behaviour and network formation in human groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that providing information about others' past actions (reputation/memory) leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates in a dynamic network Prisoner's Dilemma. When no memory is available, cooperation decays and the network becomes highly connected (as players have no reason to avoid defectors). With memory, players break links with defectors, leading to lower connectivity but higher cooperation. The effect is stronger as the memory window increases. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The 'punishment' mechanism is the breaking of links, not a costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reputation-based partner selection (link breaking) on cooperation in dynamic network games. While the setting is an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (not a PGG), and the 'punishment' is not costly, the results suggest that allowing players to sanction defectors by breaking ties (when reputation is available) can sustain higher cooperation. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not measured, and the game is not a PGG, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that information about others' behavior (reputation) and the ability to avoid defectors can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Karagonlar, G., & Kuhlman, D. M. (2013). The role of Social Value Orientation in response to an unfair offer in the Ultimatum Game. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two lab experiments, the paper finds that Prosocials (as measured by SVO) are less likely to reject an unfair offer (20% of the pie) in the Ultimatum Game than Proselfs. This difference is not due to differences in fairness perceptions or expectations, but is mediated by more positive emotions and more positive attributions toward the proposer among Prosocials. The findings support the view that emotion regulation and cognitive reappraisal, rather than altruistic punishment, drive acceptance of unfair offers among Prosocials. The study does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The Ultimatum Game is structurally different from public goods games, and the outcome of interest is the accept/reject decision rather than group payoff or efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding individual differences in responses to norm violations and costly punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does, however, suggest that motivations for punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) may differ by individual orientation, which could be contextually relevant for interpreting punishment effects in other games.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2009). Avoiding the sharp tongue: Anticipated written messages promote fair economic exchange. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The opportunity for receivers to send ex post written messages (expressing disapproval or emotion) in a one-shot dictator game leads to a statistically significant reduction in the frequency of maximally selfish (10%) offers by dictators, compared to the standard dictator game. However, the frequency of fair (50/50) offers is not significantly increased. The effect of non-monetary sanctions (messages) is smaller than that of monetary punishment (rejection) in ultimatum games, which more strongly reduce selfish offers but at the cost of efficiency due to destroyed surplus. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, as the dictator game does not involve group production or a public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal, non-monetary sanctions (written messages) on selfishness in a simple allocation game, but not in a public goods or group production context. It shows that non-monetary sanctions can reduce selfish behavior, but less so than monetary punishment. However, because the game is a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated or multi-player public goods game), and efficiency is not measured, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that in environments where only non-monetary sanctions are possible, some reduction in selfishness may occur, but the effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be directly inferred from this study.

- source: Wu, J. H., Balliet, D., Kou, Y., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). Gossip in the Dictator and Ultimatum Games: Its Immediate and Downstream Consequences for Cooperation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the possibility of gossip (reputation transmission) increases generosity in both dictator and ultimatum games. However, strategic motivation (fear of rejection in the ultimatum game) does not significantly increase generosity compared to the dictator game. In subsequent trust games, those who previously played the ultimatum game are less likely to reciprocate trust, especially when gossip is present. The effects are measured in terms of individual behavioral outcomes (generosity, trust, reciprocity), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that reputation systems (gossip) can promote cooperation, but strategic motivations may undermine future cooperation, particularly when reputation is at stake.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers adjacent evidence that reputation mechanisms (gossip) can increase cooperative behavior in dyadic allocation games, and that strategic motivations (as in the ultimatum game) may not robustly increase cooperation and can even reduce reciprocity in subsequent interactions. Since the design lacks group-level interaction, explicit punishment, and efficiency outcomes, its direct predictive value for the target task is limited. It may inform the prediction task by analogy: reputation-based incentives can substitute for punishment in promoting cooperation, but strategic extrinsic incentives may have negative downstream effects on intrinsic cooperation.

- source: Miraghaie, A. M., Pouretemad, H., Villa, A. E. P., Mazaheri, M. A., Khosrowabadi, R., & Lintas, A. (2022). Electrophysiological Markers of Fairness and Selfishness Revealed by a Combination of Dictator and Ultimatum Games. *Frontiers in Systems Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that distinct neural markers (N1, P2, MFN, LPC) are associated with behavioral profiles of fairness and selfishness in economic games. Selfish participants show neural signatures consistent with spiteful punishment (e.g., rejecting low offers in UG, resulting in zero payoffs for both), while fair participants show markers consistent with costly punishment (e.g., rejecting selfish allocations in DG). However, the paper does not report or analyze group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and does not manipulate or compare the presence/absence of punishment as a treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of punishment and fairness, but do not provide direct evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The games studied (DG and UG) are adjacent to PGGs but lack the group structure, repeated interaction, and explicit public goods provision. Punishment in UG is endogenous (rejection of unfair offers) and always results in zero payoff for both, but the study does not compare efficiency with and without punishment, nor does it report group-level payoff outcomes. The main value for prediction is in the mechanistic and neural understanding of individual differences in punishment behavior, not in quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Xiang, T., Lohrenz, T., & Montague, P. R. (2013). Computational Substrates of Norms and Their Violations during Social Exchange. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that social norm expectations, manipulated via preconditioning to low, medium, or high offers in a repeated ultimatum game, strongly affect rejection rates for identical offers. Subjects preconditioned to low offers (unfair) are less likely to reject medium offers than those preconditioned to high offers (hyper-fair), even though the offers are objectively the same. Subjective feelings about offers and neural responses (in regions such as vmPFC, striatum, and anterior insula) track these norm prediction errors. The results support a model in which norm violations, rather than simple payoff inequality, drive punishment behavior (rejection), and that these processes are reflected in both behavior and brain activity. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, and the outcome of interest is not efficiency but rather the psychological and neural mechanisms of norm-based punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, specifically regarding how expectations and norm adaptation modulate punishment (rejection) behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment (here, costly rejection) on behavior is highly sensitive to context and prior expectations, which may inform models of punishment in other social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as contextual or mechanistic evidence about the psychological drivers of punishment, not as a quantitative source for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Klapwijk, A., & Van Munster, L. M. (2011). How the shadow of the future might promote cooperation. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the mere anticipation of repeated interaction (10 rounds vs. 1 round) increases cooperation (measured as coins given to the partner) among individualists, but not among prosocials, in a sequential give-some dilemma. Prosocials maintain high cooperation regardless of repetition, while individualists reciprocate more when repeated interaction is expected. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of 'noise' in the translation of choices. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that repeated interaction (longer games) can increase cooperation among individualistically oriented participants, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. However, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or include punishment, so its relevance to the prediction of efficiency under punishment is limited. The main implication is that repeated interaction may substitute for some effects of punishment among individualists, but this is not tested in a payoff or efficiency framework.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are rational maximizers in an ultimatum game. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees, when tested in a mini-ultimatum game, do not systematically reject unfair offers, even when a fair alternative is available to the proposer. Proposers do not make fair offers, and responders accept almost all nonzero offers, only reliably rejecting offers where they receive nothing. This contrasts with human behavior, where unfair offers are often rejected as a form of costly punishment. The findings suggest that chimpanzees are rational maximizers and do not engage in costly punishment to enforce fairness. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses to fairness and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a mini-ultimatum game with chimpanzees and focuses on behavioral outcomes (offer choices, rejection rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative in showing that, in the absence of a willingness to punish unfairness (i.e., when punishment is not exercised), there is no mechanism to enforce fair or cooperative outcomes. This suggests that, in environments where punishment is not used or is not costly, efficiency gains from punishment are unlikely. The findings are adjacent to the prediction task and may inform models about the necessity of actual punishment behavior for efficiency effects.

- source: Camera, G., Casari, M., & Bigoni, M. (2013). EXPERIMENTAL MARKETS WITH FRICTIONS. *Journal of Economic Surveys*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The new experiment shows that allowing free-form chat among randomly matched strangers in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma leads to much higher cooperation rates than either no communication or structured, anonymous public messages. Structured communication does not significantly improve cooperation or coordination relative to no communication, likely due to the prevalence of deceptive use of messages. The study finds that public messages are informative about intentions, but deception dilutes their effectiveness. The experiment does not include a punishment treatment, but the literature review summarizes prior findings that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, especially in small groups. The main outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation rates, communication patterns), not payoff-based (efficiency or group earnings).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. The experimental design is an indefinitely repeated, random-matching prisoner's dilemma among strangers, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main manipulated dimension is communication: free-form chat vs. structured messages vs. no communication. The findings suggest that free-form communication can strongly increase cooperation, but structured, anonymous communication does not. The experiment does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The literature review section summarizes prior work showing that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but these are not tested in the new experiment. Thus, this paper is most informative about the effect of communication (chat) and monitoring (public/anonymous) on cooperation, and only indirectly about punishment or efficiency. It should be used as contextual or indirect evidence for prediction, especially regarding the role of communication and monitoring, but not as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Samu, F., & Takács, K. (2021). Evaluating mechanisms that could support credible reputations and cooperation: cross-checking and social bonding. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment found that allowing for cross-checking of gossip or opportunities for social bonding did not significantly improve cooperation rates compared to a control. High competition for reputation increased cooperation, but overall cooperation declined over time in all conditions. Gossip was generally honest but became more negative about rivals under high competition. Reputation scores and gossip valence influenced conditional cooperation, but the mechanisms tested did not create a reliable path to sustained high cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of communication, reputation, and competition on cooperation in repeated dyadic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not include explicit costly punishment or group-level payoff analysis. Findings suggest that mechanisms to improve the credibility of social information (cross-checking, social bonding) may not be sufficient to sustain cooperation, and that competition for reputation can increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper offers only contextual or indirect guidance.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners). However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2002). Crime in the lab-detecting social interaction. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates strong social interaction effects: subjects' willingness to steal increases with the observed or expected stealing of others. About half of the subjects are 'reciprocal' (conditional) types, while the rest are mostly selfish (always steal the maximum). The fraction of selfish types is higher when stealing is a pure transfer (no efficiency loss) than when it is inefficient. The study does not include a punishment or sanctioning phase, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding norm compliance and conditional cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment or on efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about the importance of social interaction and conditional norm violation: when individuals see more norm violation (stealing), they are more likely to violate norms themselves. The manipulation of the efficiency consequences of stealing (gamma) shows that more selfish behavior occurs when norm violation is less costly to the group, which may be relevant for understanding the impact of efficiency parameters in related games. However, because there is no punishment mechanism and no direct efficiency or payoff analysis, this paper should be considered only as indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Rilling, J. K., Goldsmith, D. R., Glenn, A. L., Jairam, M. R., Elfenbein, H. A., Dagenais, J. E., Murdock, C. D., & Pagnoni, G. (2008). The neural correlates of the affective response to unreciprocated cooperation. *Neuropsychologia*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that unreciprocated cooperation (being defected on after cooperating) in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma elicits strong negative emotions and increased neural activity in regions associated with aversive and social pain (anterior insula, hippocampus, amygdala). These neural responses are stronger when the unreciprocated cooperation follows a cooperative move by the subject, suggesting sensitivity to perceived partner intent. Functional connectivity between the anterior insula and lateral orbitofrontal cortex predicts whether the subject will defect in the next round. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma games. It does, however, provide evidence that unreciprocated cooperation (partner defection) triggers strong negative affect and neural responses that bias subjects toward defection in subsequent rounds. This suggests a psychological mechanism by which cooperation can unravel in the absence of punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that, without punishment, negative experiences with defectors can lead to reduced cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or payoff, nor does it test the effect of introducing punishment.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Ligon, E., & Schechter, L. (2012). Motives for sharing in social networks. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds high baseline sharing in anonymous, random-recipient dictator games, with small but significant increases in sharing when the dictator can choose the recipient or when the dictator's identity is revealed. The decomposition shows that preference-based motives (baseline, directed altruism) account for most mean sharing, but incentive-based motives (sanctions, reciprocity) explain a large share of the variance in sharing and are strongly associated with real-world gift-giving and social network connectedness. The effect of revealing identity (potential for social sanctions or reciprocity) is modest on average but important for explaining individual differences. The study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a set of dictator game variants, not a PGG or linear public goods game, and there is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism—only the possibility of social sanctions or reciprocity inferred from information structure. The findings suggest that the potential for social sanctions or reciprocity can modestly increase sharing, but the context and outcome measures are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency predictions. The results may be useful for understanding how information and recipient choice affect sharing motives, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kaba, M., Koyuncu, M., Schneider, S. O., & Sutter, M. (2024). Social norms, political polarization, and vaccination attitudes: Evidence from a survey experiment in Turkey. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both pro- and anti-vaxxers discriminate against each other in allocation tasks, with pro-vaxxers showing stronger outgroup bias (interpreted as 'punishment' of anti-vaxxers for norm violation). Political polarization amplifies this discrimination. Randomized informational treatments (reminders of pandemic health/economic costs or the Russia-Ukraine war) reduce outgroup bias, especially the war treatment among pro-vaxxers. However, the main outcome is behavioral (allocation bias), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The allocation tasks and modified public goods game measure outgroup discrimination, not group efficiency or total earnings.
