The findings may be useful for understanding how social identity and polarization affect norm enforcement and discrimination, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Seinen, I., & Schram, A. (2006). Social status and group norms: Indirect reciprocity in a repeated helping experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds strong evidence for indirect reciprocity: donors are significantly more likely to help recipients who have helped others in the past, as measured by the recipient's social status. When information about social status is available, cooperation rates are much higher (e.g., 70% in high-cost/information vs. 22% in high-cost/no-information). Individual strategies often use cut-points based on the recipient's or donor's own social status, and group-level norms about the minimum acceptable status to be helped emerge and are stable within groups but differ across groups. The paper does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but does provide some expected return calculations for different strategies. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; 'punishment' is indirect, in the sense that donors withhold help from low-status recipients.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effects of information and social norms on cooperation in repeated helping games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The design does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but the withholding of help from low-status individuals functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the rate of helping (cooperation), not efficiency or group payoff, though some expected returns are calculated. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that information about others' past behavior and the emergence of group norms can sustain high cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the efficiency effects of explicit punishment mechanisms. The design dimensions most relevant here are information availability, cost/benefit structure, and group size, but the absence of explicit punishment and the focus on individual rather than group payoffs limit direct applicability.

- source: West, S. A., Gardner, A., Shuker, D. M., Reynolds, T., Burton-Chellow, M., Sykes, E. M., Guinnee, M. A., & Griffin, A. S. (2006). Cooperation and the scale of competition in humans. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that human participants cooperate more frequently in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games when rewards are based on global competition (top scorers in the whole class) than when rewards are based on local competition (top scorer in each small group). This effect is robust across individuals, groups, and classes. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the scale of competition (local vs global) can strongly affect cooperation rates in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does highlight the importance of competitive structure as a moderator of cooperation. The findings suggest that contextual factors like competition scale may interact with other mechanisms (such as punishment) in affecting cooperation, but no direct evidence is provided for punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, W. J., Xu, Z. H., Hua, S. J., Cui, L. Q., Zhang, J. L., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Threshold-initiated spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that intergroup competition significantly increases the rate of volunteering in the volunteer's dilemma, even when there is no additional monetary incentive or change in the payoff structure. Risk (a 50% chance of public good failure) reduces volunteering rates, but intergroup competition mitigates this negative effect. Gender and risk aversion moderate these effects: females volunteer more in no-risk settings, males increase volunteering more under risky intergroup competition. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but notes in the discussion that over-volunteering (stimulated by intergroup competition) is socially inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about volunteering rates in a volunteer's dilemma, which is structurally adjacent to but not the same as a standard public goods game. The results suggest that intergroup competition increases pro-social action rates, especially under risk, but also that this can lead to inefficiency through over-volunteering. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the main takeaway is that mechanisms increasing pro-social action (like competition) may not always improve efficiency, especially in anti-coordination settings like the volunteer's dilemma. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the paper's predictive value for the target task.

- source: Mill, W., & Morgan, J. (2022). The cost of a divided America: an experimental study into destructive behavior. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that both Clinton and Trump voters are willing to destroy the payoff of another participant, but the probability of doing so is significantly higher when the other is an outgroup member (opposing voter). This effect is mainly driven by Clinton voters, who increase their probability of spiteful behavior by 34% when facing an outgroup member. Trump voters are generally more spiteful overall, but do not differentiate between ingroup and outgroup. Attitudinal measures (social distance, feeling thermometer) are strongly correlated with spiteful behavior. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on direct, antisocial destruction of another’s payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot, two-player allocation task with the option to destroy the other’s payoff at no benefit to self (pure spite), not a repeated public goods game with punishment. There is no group production, no opportunity for cooperation, and no efficiency or welfare outcome reported. The findings are relevant for understanding the prevalence and moderators of destructive behavior (spite) in polarized contexts, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The closest relevance is to the literature on antisocial punishment or joy-of-destruction, but the mapping to PGG-like efficiency outcomes is weak.

- source: Batsaikhan, M., & Putterman, L. (2019). An Honest Day's Pay: Cooperation among Entrepreneurs vs. Students, and Linkages to Real-World Business Success. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Entrepreneurs in Mongolia display higher and increasing cooperation in a repeated property rights dilemma game compared to students, as measured by more tokens allocated to production and collective protection and fewer to theft. The opportunity to vote for mandatory collective protection (akin to a tax) is more frequently chosen by entrepreneurs, but this does not substantially change their already high cooperation levels. The chat treatment (pre-scripted messages) is associated with higher collective protection among entrepreneurs. There is no formal punishment mechanism (costly sanctions) as in standard PGGs; theft can be used as retaliation, but this is not a direct analog. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but discusses how behavioral allocations relate to social optimum. More successful entrepreneurs in real life are more cooperative in the lab. The main outcomes are behavioral, not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. The experimental design lacks a formal costly punishment mechanism; instead, it features theft (which is a form of negative externality, not a sanction) and collective protection (akin to public good provision). While the paper discusses how cooperation (less theft, more production, more collective protection) would increase efficiency, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, nor does it manipulate punishment in the relevant sense. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, though it provides contextual evidence about cooperation, institutional choice, and behavioral trends in repeated social dilemmas.

- source: Harth, N. S., & Regner, T. (2017). The spiral of distrust: (Non-)cooperation in a repeated trust game is predicted by anger and individual differences in negative reciprocity orientation. *International Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that senders who receive feedback about low back transfers from trustees experience increased anger, which in turn leads to reduced cooperation (lower amounts sent) in subsequent rounds. Individuals with a high negative reciprocity orientation are more prone to anger and are less willing to cooperate, even in the absence of anger-eliciting feedback. The interaction between anger and negative reciprocity orientation further reduces cooperation. The absence of feedback results in lower anger and relatively higher cooperation, especially among those low in negative reciprocity orientation. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it studies a repeated trust game without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (amount sent/cooperation) and emotional (anger), not payoff-based. However, it provides indirect evidence that negative emotions and individual differences in reciprocity orientation can reduce cooperation in repeated interactions, especially when feedback about others' behavior is available. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only weakly informative and should not be weighted heavily unless the prediction context is specifically about trust games or the psychological mechanisms of cooperation.

- source: Andersson, O., & Wengström, E. (2012). Credible communication and cooperation: Experimental evidence from multi-stage Games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing only pre-play communication leads to higher cooperation rates in the first stage (prisoner's dilemma) than allowing both pre-play and intra-play communication. Sending or receiving messages that signal cooperative intentions has a strong positive effect on cooperation when only pre-play communication is possible, but this effect is significantly weakened when intra-play communication is allowed. The results support the theoretical prediction that renegotiation opportunities (via intra-play communication) undermine the credibility of punishment threats and thus reduce the effectiveness of communication in sustaining cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral (not payoff) effects of communication structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of communication structure (timing and frequency) on cooperation in multi-stage games with punishment/reward strategies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that communication structure can moderate the effectiveness of punishment/reward threats in sustaining cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency in similar environments. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma plus coordination game, and punishment is implemented via equilibrium threats rather than explicit costly punishment. The main actionable insight is that more communication opportunities (especially intra-play) can reduce the effectiveness of pre-play communication in sustaining cooperation, likely due to renegotiation.

- source: Cao, N. M., Miao, R. R., Sun, B. H., Ren, Z. R., & Yue, G. A. (2024). Exemplary Self-Discipline, Leniency towards Others: Competitive Contexts Amplify the Black Sheep Effect in Restoring Ingroup Trust. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that after a trust violation and apology, participants are more willing to restore trust (measured by increased investment) with out-group members than with in-group members, but this effect is only present in competitive contexts. In cooperative contexts, trust is restored equally for both in-group and out-group members. The results are interpreted as evidence for the 'black sheep effect' in trust repair, moderated by the nature of the interaction (competition vs. cooperation). The outcome is investment behavior, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a repeated trust game (not a PGG), and the main outcome is trust restoration (investment behavior) rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; the 'punishment' is implicit in reduced trust/investment after a violation. The findings may be relevant for understanding how group identity and context affect willingness to restore cooperation after norm violations, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with explicit punishment options.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Mancini, A., Betti, V., Panasiti, M. S., Pavone, E. F., & Aglioti, S. M. (2011). Suffering Makes You Egoist: Acute Pain Increases Acceptance Rates and Reduces Fairness during a Bilateral Ultimatum Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that acute pain leads to higher acceptance rates of unfair offers and lower fairness in offers made, suggesting that pain induces more self-interested behavior in both roles of the Ultimatum Game. The effect is specific to pain (not just heat) and is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The intensity of pain predicts acceptance rates for these offers. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to PGGs, and focuses on the effect of pain (a state manipulation) rather than institutional punishment. The outcomes are behavioral (acceptance, offer size) rather than payoff-based. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Terai, S., Kiyonari, T., Mifune, N., & Kanazawa, S. (2007). The social exchange heuristic - Managing errors in social exchange. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in one-shot PDGs is significantly higher when participants perceive themselves to be in an ongoing social exchange relationship (i.e., when the SEH is activated) compared to when they do not. In Experiment 1, participants who deliberated about the game and their partner before being assigned to a specific exchange relationship (delayed-play) cooperated less than those who made decisions immediately after being assigned a partner (immediate-play). In Experiment 2, participants who made their contribution decision after being told they were matched with a specific partner (partner-specified) contributed more than those who made their decision before being matched (partner-unspecified). The study attributes these effects to the activation of the SEH, which frames the game as an Assurance Game and increases conditional cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency. However, it does provide informative evidence that cues of social exchange and perceived interdependence can substantially increase cooperation rates in dyadic social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that psychological framing and the presence of cues indicating ongoing relationships may be important moderators of cooperation, but the paper does not address punishment or efficiency directly.

- source: Baik, K. H., Chowdhury, S. M., & Ramalingam, A. (2021). Group size and matching protocol in contests. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Tullock contests, partner matching (fixed pairs) leads to lower bids and less overbidding compared to random stranger matching, but only in two-player groups. In three-player groups, matching protocol has no significant effect on bids or overbidding. Bid dispersion is also lower in smaller groups. The results suggest that identifiability and repeated interaction facilitate tacit collusion in small groups, reducing wasteful overbidding. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral measures (bids and overbidding) rather than direct efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in contest-like environments, especially regarding the effects of group size and repeated interaction (partner matching). While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the finding that partner matching reduces overbidding in two-player groups suggests that efficiency losses from overbidding may be mitigated in such settings. For prediction tasks, the results imply that small group size and repeated interaction can reduce wasteful resource expenditure (overbidding), potentially increasing efficiency, but this is inferred from bid behavior rather than direct efficiency measurement. The absence of punishment or public goods framing limits direct transfer to PGG-like environments.

- source: Müller-Leinss, J. M., Enzi, B., Flasbeck, V., & Brüne, M. (2018). Retaliation or selfishness? An rTMS investigation of the role of the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex in prosocial motives. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases both retaliation against previously unfair opponents and selfish behavior (less fairness) towards previously fair opponents in a Dictator Game following an Ultimatum Game. The manipulation did not affect rejection rates in the UG. The findings suggest the right DLPFC is involved in controlling selfish motives, but the design does not distinguish between retaliation and pure selfishness. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or direct punishment paradigm.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent: it uses sequential UG and DG with role reversal and rTMS manipulation, focusing on individual-level retaliation/selfishness rather than group-level cooperation or efficiency. There is no manipulation of punishment institutions, costs, or group payoff structure as in PGGs. The main outcomes are behavioral (fair/unfair offers), not efficiency or payoff. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for the prediction task, except as context for the neural basis of retaliation/selfishness in economic games.

- source: Han, X., Cao, S. N., Shen, Z. S., Zhang, B. Y., Wang, W. X., Cressman, R., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). Emergence of communities and diversity in social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in static network structures (both regular and random bipartite networks), proposers form stable local communities with diverse offers, while in randomly rewired networks, proposers become homogeneous. The diversity in offers (SD of p) is much higher in structured networks. Responders' behavior is heterogeneous but not affected by network structure. The mechanism is that rational proposers best-respond to their local responder 'leaders,' whose heterogeneous minimum acceptance levels drive local agreement. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include a public goods game or explicit costly punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experiment uses the ultimatum game, where 'punishment' is the rejection of offers, not a costly sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer diversity, community formation), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. It may provide some indirect context about how local interaction structure and heterogeneity can affect behavioral diversity, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., & Thielmann, I. (2025). Toward a (more) parsimonious account of the link between 'dark' personality and social decision-making in economic games. *Judgment and Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 10 large-sample, hypothetical studies using eight different economic games (including the Prisoner's Dilemma, Dictator Game, Ultimatum Game, Faith Game, Chicken Game, Stag Hunt, and Uncostly Retaliation Game), the common core of dark personality traits (D) consistently predicts antisocial choices (i.e., lower prosociality). Single dark tetrad traits also predict antisocial choices, but these effects are almost entirely accounted for by D. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and does not include public goods games with or without punishment. The main contribution is to clarify the role of personality in prosocial/antisocial decision-making, not to inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include any games with a punishment or sanctioning phase, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about individual differences in prosocial choice, not about institutional or design features such as punishment, reward, or their impact on efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Townsend, C., Aktipis, A., Balliet, D., & Cronk, L. (2020). Generosity among the Ik of Uganda. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Ik participants in unframed Dictator Games give on average 23.3% of their endowment, which is within the expected range for similar societies. Framing the game to invoke both need and supernatural punishment significantly increases giving (to 43.2%), while either frame alone does not have a significant effect. The results challenge the claim that the Ik are uniquely selfish and show that cultural norms and beliefs can increase generosity. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not include punishment as a costly sanctioning option, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a Dictator Game, not a PGG or its variant, and the 'punishment' manipulation is a framing invoking supernatural punishment, not a real, costly, player-administered sanction. The outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how cultural beliefs about punishment can increase generosity, but they do not provide evidence about the efficiency effects of actual punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hoffmann, L. (2022). Cooperation in the name of God? Experimental evidence from Ghana and Tanzania. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that religious reminders of punishment or reward decrease cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma among Christian and Muslim participants in Ghana and Tanzania. The punishment prime reduces cooperation by about 8 percentage points, and the reward prime by about 6 percentage points, relative to the control. There is no evidence that religious primes increase cooperation. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and beliefs/expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma (not a repeated PGG), and the 'punishment' and 'reward' treatments are religious primes, not institutionalized peer punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. Thus, the paper is only weakly informative for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that not all forms of punishment/reward framing increase cooperation, and that context and mechanism matter.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., Rincke, J., & Tuset-Cueva, A. (2019). Rent extraction and prosocial behavior. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when an administrator actively chooses to extract a 10% rent from contributions, average contributions to non-profit associations drop significantly (by 41%) compared to a control where the same share is assigned passively. This effect is robust to framing and is interpreted as a motivational effect: rent extraction undermines contributors' general willingness to give, rather than triggering targeted negative reciprocity. The effect persists even when contributors interact with a new, uninvolved administrator in a subsequent round, indicating a spillover on generosity. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the design is a donation game with some similarities to public goods games but is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that visible anti-social actions by an authority figure (rent extraction) can sharply reduce prosocial contributions in a donation context, even when the price of giving is held constant. However, because the outcome is contribution rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a donation game rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The result suggests that introducing a salient, self-serving authority or punishment-like mechanism that is perceived as unfair or anti-social may reduce cooperation and thus efficiency, but the direct quantitative effect on efficiency in a PGG is not measured here.

- source: Jones, M. T. (2014). Strategic complexity and cooperation: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Increasing the complexity of strategies required to sustain cooperation in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma reduces the frequency of cooperation and increases the prevalence of simple selfish strategies. This effect is observed both when the cooperate action and when the defect action are made state-dependent, increasing the information processing required for cooperation or punishment, respectively. The study also finds that higher cognitive ability is associated with greater cooperation in the baseline, but this advantage is reduced when complexity is increased. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures such as cooperation rates and strategy inference.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cognitive or strategic complexity of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas reduces cooperation rates, and by implication, would likely reduce efficiency or group payoff if measured. However, the study is not a public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on endogenous punishment via repeated game strategies (e.g., Grim Trigger). The findings are most relevant for predicting how complexity or cognitive burden might moderate the effect of punishment or cooperation-supporting mechanisms in repeated games, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games. Use this paper as informative indirect evidence about the role of complexity and cognitive ability in sustaining cooperation, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Bigoni, M., Potters, J., & Spagnolo, G. (2019). Frequency of interaction, communication and collusion: an experiment. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that, without communication, cooperation (collusion) rates are low and not significantly affected by the frequency of interaction (flexibility). When pre-play communication is allowed, cooperation rates increase dramatically across all treatments, and the non-monotonic effect of flexibility predicted by theory (higher cooperation at intermediate flexibility) becomes more apparent, but is still secondary to the effect of communication. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and there is no explicit punishment mechanism; instead, punishment is implemented via Nash reversion (endogenous). The findings suggest that communication is a much stronger facilitator of cooperation than flexibility in this setting with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that communication (chat) has a very strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a repeated game with imperfect monitoring, even in the absence of explicit punishment. However, because the game is a Cournot duopoly (not a public goods game), there is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that enabling communication can greatly increase cooperation, but the effect of flexibility (frequency of interaction) is non-monotonic and only emerges when communication is present. The results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but suggest that communication is a key moderator.

- source: Brevers, D., Noël, X., Ermer, E., Dabiri, D., Verbanck, P., & Kornreich, C. (2013). Unfairness sensitivity and social decision-making in individuals with alcohol dependence: A preliminary study. *Drug and Alcohol Dependence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Alcohol-dependent individuals are more likely to reject unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game compared to healthy controls, suggesting greater emotional reactivity to unfairness and reduced ability to make self-advantageous decisions in social contexts involving frustration. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and the design does not include a punishment phase or a repeated public goods structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to the target environment, as rejection of unfair offers can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the context is one-shot, dyadic, and does not involve group cooperation or repeated interactions. The findings may inform understanding of individual differences in punishment sensitivity, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or the effect of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.

- source: Lu, X. Q., Li, T., Li, B. M., & Yang, Z. (2025). The influence of third-party's power on multi-player fairness decision-making. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that participants care about fairness towards third parties in three-person ultimatum games, but their concern is modulated by whether the third party has decision power. When the third party is powerless, participants enforce fairness norms and show both disadvantageous and advantageous inequity aversion. When the third party has power, participants incorporate both fairness and social comparison norms, showing greater disadvantageous inequity aversion and even advantageous inequity preference towards the third party. The presence of a powerful third party leads to diffusion of responsibility, reducing the participant's willingness to punish unfairness. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to efficiency prediction in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and norm enforcement mechanisms may operate in multi-player bargaining contexts, especially regarding the psychological mechanisms (inequity aversion, responsibility, social comparison) that underlie acceptance/rejection of unfair offers. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that the structure of power and responsibility among players can modulate willingness to punish norm violations, which may, in turn, affect efficiency in related but not identical game structures.

- source: Stiff, C. (2008). Are They Bothered? How the Opportunity to Damage a Partner's Reputation Influences Giving Behavior in a Trust Game. *Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity to leave feedback (damage a partner's reputation) increases the amount participants are willing to give in a one-shot trust game, but only when the partner is perceived to care about their reputation. This effect is strongest when the partner's future interaction group consists of strangers rather than friends. The effect of feedback opportunity and group familiarity on giving is mediated by the participant's perceived control over the partner's reputation. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total welfare, and the design is a dyadic trust game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based indirect sanctions (opportunity to leave feedback) can increase cooperative behavior (giving/trust) in dyadic trust games, especially when the target is reputation-sensitive. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of reputation-based sanctions depends on the perceived concern for reputation and the social context of future interactions. For prediction tasks, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that indirect, reputation-based sanctions can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency effects or direct evidence for public goods games with explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Tanaka, H., Ohtsuki, H., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2016). The price of being seen to be just: an intention signalling strategy for indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that participants in a dyadic donation game use a costly signalling option (abandoning a resource) to manage their reputation, especially after justified defection or implementation error. Others interpret this signal as a sign of cooperative intent and are more likely to cooperate with signallers. The signalling option allows players to maintain a good reputation without requiring second-order information. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that such signalling can stabilize cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The experimental design is a dyadic donation game, not a multi-player PGG, and the 'punishment' is a costly self-imposed signal (abandoning a resource) rather than a punishment directed at others. The main outcomes are behavioral (signalling and cooperation rates), not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings suggest that providing players with a costly signalling option can help maintain cooperation in the absence of second-order information, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms that stabilize cooperation in related environments. The results do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Liu, W. X., Wang, H., Zhu, H. J., Zhu, X. Y., He, X. Y., & Zhang, W. (2022). Morality is Supreme: The Roles of Morality, Fairness and Group Identity in the Ultimatum Paradigm. *Psychology Research and Behavior Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, responders are more likely to accept offers (including unfair ones) from proposers with positive moral backgrounds than from those with negative moral backgrounds. This effect is robust across fair, moderately unfair, and extremely unfair offers. Group identity, manipulated via moral priming, has only a partial effect: positive or negative moral priming increases acceptance rates for positive moral proposers in unfair allocations, but does not significantly affect responses to negative moral proposers. The gender of the proposer does not significantly affect acceptance rates. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are not directly transferable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in ultimatum game settings, the perceived morality of the proposer strongly influences acceptance rates, even for unfair offers. However, because the outcome measured is acceptance rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the ultimatum game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, costs, or efficiency, so it does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social/moral context in responder behavior, which may inform broader models of cooperation and norm enforcement.

- source: Bengart, P., Gruendler, T., & Vogt, B. (2021). Acute tryptophan depletion in healthy subjects increases preferences for negative reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that lowering serotonin via ATD increases negative reciprocity in the Hawk-Dove game: participants in the ATD group are more likely to choose Hawk in response to an expected Hawk, compared to placebo. This effect is specific to negative reciprocity and does not extend to positive reciprocity or beliefs about the opponent's action. The incentive for mutual cooperation (payoff for mutual Dove) reduces Hawk choices. Risk attitude and mood are unaffected by ATD and do not predict strategy choice. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that biological factors (serotonin availability) can modulate negative reciprocity in a competitive, dyadic game, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or sanctions in public goods or similar games. The Hawk-Dove game is structurally adjacent to public goods and punishment games, but lacks explicit punishment or sanctioning stages, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, the findings are only weakly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The main relevance is in showing that negative reciprocity (costly, non-payoff-maximizing responses to perceived unkindness) can be increased by serotonin depletion, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff in multi-player, repeated, or sanction-enabled games is limited.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Schram, A., & Charness, G. (2015). Inducing Social Norms in Laboratory Allocation Choices. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that providing impartial advice about what one 'ought to do' (shared understanding) only affects behavior when choices are made public, i.e., when social (not just moral) norms are salient. In the advice + public condition, dictators are significantly less selfish than in other conditions. Advice alone (private) does not change behavior, nor does public observability alone without advice. The effect is interpreted as the creation of a social norm, enforced by the threat of social disapproval (shame), not by material punishment. There is no direct measurement or analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. The findings are relevant for understanding norm induction and the role of observability, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments. It does not implement punishment, rewards, or repeated interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how social norms (via advice and observability) can shift behavior toward less selfish choices in allocation games. For prediction tasks, the main takeaway is that nonmaterial social sanctions (public observability) combined with shared understanding can induce prosocial behavior, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes and the lack of a PGG or punishment mechanism limit its direct applicability.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: 
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that across eight diverse societies, individuals who believe their gods are more punitive and knowledgeable are more likely to allocate coins impartially to distant co-religionists, rather than favoring themselves or local co-religionists. This association is robust to controls for economic and demographic variables and is specific to moralistic gods (not local gods). The effect is interpreted as support for the hypothesis that belief in supernatural punishment can expand the radius of impartial prosociality. However, the experimental task is a random allocation game measuring norm compliance and impartiality, not a public goods game with group production or explicit efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that belief in supernatural punishment is associated with more impartial, less parochial allocation behavior in a norm compliance context. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. The design lacks group production, peer punishment, or explicit efficiency measures. Thus, while the findings may inform hypotheses about the psychological mechanisms underlying cooperation and norm compliance, they do not directly support prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGG-like games with or without punishment. The evidence is best interpreted as informative about the potential for supernatural punishment beliefs to reduce parochialism and increase impartiality, not about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in economic games.

- source: Rand, D. G., Brescoll, V. L., Everett, J. A. C., Capraro, V., & Barcelo, H. (2016). Social Heuristics and Social Roles: Intuition Favors Altruism for Women but Not for Men. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that promoting intuition (via time pressure, conceptual priming, or cognitive load) increases altruistic giving in the Dictator Game for women, but not for men. This effect is further moderated by gender role identification: women who identify with masculine attributes become less altruistic when deliberating, while those who identify with feminine attributes are unaffected by deliberation. Men are generally less altruistic, and their giving is not significantly affected by cognitive processing mode or gender role identification. The study does not involve strategic interaction, punishment, or group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is the Dictator Game, which is a unilateral giving game with no strategic interaction, no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and no group payoff or efficiency concept. While the paper discusses social norms and the internalization of altruism (and mentions that women are punished more for failing to be altruistic), it does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report any efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as very indirect context about gender, social heuristics, and norm compliance.

- source: Mellers, B. A., Haselhuhn, M. P., Tetlock, P. E., Silva, J. C., & Isen, A. M. (2010). Predicting Behavior in Economic Games by Looking Through the Eyes of the Players. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Players' self-defined want and ought motives predict their offers in ultimatum and dictator games; (2) in the ultimatum game, more players act according to what they believe is fair (ought), while in the dictator game, want and ought are equally influential; (3) positive mood induction increases the likelihood of making equal offers in the dictator game; (4) when given an exit option, players experiencing greater conflict between want and ought are more likely to exit. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment in public goods or repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment institutions, marginal per-capita return, or group efficiency. Its findings on rejections in the ultimatum game and exit behavior in the dictator game are only loosely related to the prediction task. The main value is in highlighting the psychological mechanisms (want/ought selves) that may underlie cooperation and sanctioning, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions.

Some sites show small positive trends, but overall effects are small, inconsistent, and not robust. The study also finds no robust interaction between perceived deity punitiveness, knowledge, and moral concern. The main outcomes are behavioral (coin allocations), not efficiency or group payoff. The study's main manipulation is the salience of supernatural moral concerns, not the presence or absence of a punishment institution. Thus, while the findings suggest that moralistic supernatural punishment beliefs do not reliably increase cooperation in these settings, they do not inform the effect of formal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGGs. The paper is best used as evidence that the mere salience of supernatural punishment (without institutionalized punishment options) does not robustly increase cooperative allocations in adjacent economic games.

- source: Kusakawa, T., Ogawa, K., & Shichijo, T. (2012). An experimental investigation of a third-person enforcement in a prisoner's dilemma game. *Economics Letters*.

  findings: The experiment shows that when a third party (M) can observe the actions of two players in a one-shot PD, the cooperation rate between those two players is significantly higher than when M cannot observe. This is attributed to the threat of future punishment by M in subsequent repeated PD games. The effect is measured in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or total payoff. The study provides evidence that third-party observation and the possibility of future sanctioning can facilitate cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a third-party observer with future sanctioning power can increase cooperation rates in a prisoner's dilemma setting. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or directly measure efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that third-party monitoring and the threat of future punishment can be a strong facilitator of cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly tested. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate standard punishment mechanisms (e.g., costly punishment within the group), so caution is warranted in transferring the findings.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
