  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that trust recipients allocate significantly less to trust givers who express selfish expectations than to those who express cooperative or no expectations. There is no significant difference between the cooperative and pure trust conditions. All allocations to the trust giver are above the minimum (preservation point) and below the equality point. The results are interpreted as evidence that explicit selfish expectations activate self-interest and reactance, leading to less cooperative responses, while cooperative or unstated expectations elicit more equal allocations. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it implement punishment as a game feature.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot allocation game with no repeated interaction, no group structure, and no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism. While the discussion references social sanctioning and norm enforcement, these are psychological constructs rather than implemented game features. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how communicated expectations affect allocation behavior, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Bica, A., & Cretu, R. Z. (2023). The carrot and the stick: How guilt and shame facilitate reciprocity-driven cooperation. *Cognition & Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Inducing guilt in participants led to more generous offers to fair partners (positive reciprocity), while inducing shame led to higher rejection rates of unfair offers and lower offers to unfair partners (negative reciprocity). Both emotions influenced fairness-related decision-making, but via different mechanisms. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses (acceptance, proposal amounts) in the Ultimatum Game. The findings suggest that moral emotions can facilitate cooperation, but the pathways differ: guilt promotes generosity toward fair actors, while shame motivates punishment of unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the behavioral mechanisms (reciprocity, punishment, reward) that can affect cooperation in economic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment-enabled games. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to public goods games, and the punishment here is endogenous (rejection of unfair offers) rather than a separate costly punishment mechanism. The findings may inform expectations about how emotional states or social context could moderate the impact of punishment or reciprocity on cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, Z. S., Krumhuber, E. G., & Chen, H. (2021). Money and flexible generosity. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the authors find that using money as the currency in DG/UG games reduces generosity compared to candy or food, but only when there are no sanctions (DG) or reputation concerns (private condition). When punishment is possible (UG) or when allocations are public (reputation at stake), the negative effect of money on generosity is reduced or eliminated. Imbuing money with prosocial meaning (charity) also attenuates the effect. The main outcome is generosity rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of sanctions or reputation mechanisms can offset the self-serving effects of money on prosocial behavior, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment or reputation mechanisms can mitigate the negative effect of monetary incentives on prosocial behavior, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. The results are most relevant for predicting how punishment or reputation might moderate the effect of monetary incentives on generosity or cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that punishment/reputation mechanisms may help maintain prosocial behavior even in monetary contexts, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Azevedo, R. T., Panasiti, M. S., Maglio, R., & Aglioti, S. M. (2018). Perceived warmth and competence of others shape voluntary deceptive behaviour in a morally relevant setting. *British Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the perceived warmth and competence of the target modulate the likelihood of lying for self-gain: participants lie less to high-warmth individuals and more to high-competence individuals. The risk of disclosure (reputation risk) reduces lying, particularly toward low-warmth/low-competence targets. These effects are interpreted as reflecting malleable personal moral standards and social evaluation processes. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the game is not a public goods game or direct variant. The outcomes are behavioral (lying frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social context, reputation risk, and target characteristics can modulate dishonest behavior in a zero-sum, two-player setting. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The manipulation of reputation risk is conceptually adjacent to punishment/sanction mechanisms, but there is no explicit punishment, and the outcome is not efficiency. Thus, the paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it may inform understanding of how social evaluation and disclosure risk affect norm-violating behavior.

- source: Chen, M. K., & Hauser, M. (2005). Modeling reciprocation and cooperation in primates: evidence for a punishing strategy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tamarins' cooperative behavior in repeated food-exchange games is best described by 'punish-2' and 'punish-3' strategies, which are more punishing than standard tit-for-tat. These strategies require two or more consecutive cooperative acts to restart cooperation after a breakdown, leading to stable but sometimes brittle cooperation. The analysis uses maximum-likelihood fitting of a suite of behavioral models, showing that these punishing strategies account for over 90% of the posterior model weight. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all results are based on behavioral patterns (pulling rates and model fits).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that more punishing strategies (requiring multiple acts of cooperation to restore cooperation) can stabilize cooperation in repeated dyadic interactions. The findings may inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment-like strategies affect cooperation dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The game is not a standard PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based.

- source: Gomes, C. M., Mulhinch, M. R., & McCullough, M. E. (2025). People decide whether to share social information-and whether to share it accurately-on the basis of self-interest. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that people are more likely to share reputational information (gossip) when they have behaved generously in a prior economic game, and less likely when they have behaved greedily. The tendency to share information honestly or dishonestly is strongly influenced by whether the participant stands to benefit from being chosen as a partner for a subsequent economic game. When lying is possible and being chosen is incentivized, greedy or indifferent participants are more likely to share dishonest, self-serving gossip. Generous participants are more likely to share honest information, especially when honesty is required and being chosen is incentivized. The results support reputation-based partner choice and social competition theories over indirect reciprocity theory. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it offers informative context about the strategic use of social information (gossip) in economic games with partner choice and reputation. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of information-sharing mechanisms (and their honesty) depends on participants' incentives and prior behavior, which may be relevant for understanding the role of reputation and information in environments with punishment or partner choice. Since no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, but may inform mechanistic expectations about when information-based interventions (including punishment or reputation) are likely to be effective or undermined by dishonesty.

- source: Zhang, Q. W., Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, X. R. (2024). Reputation-based disconnection-reconnection mechanism in Prisoner's Dilemma Game within dynamic networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that a reputation-based mechanism, where players can disconnect from low-reputation neighbors and reconnect with others, significantly increases the frequency of cooperation in repeated PDG simulations on dynamic networks. The effect is stronger when the reputation factor (alpha) is high and when the network's information connectivity (environmental degree r, rewiring probability p) is high. Conversely, when the selection intensity (sigma) is high, the effect of reputation is diminished and cooperation collapses. The results are consistent across both square lattice and small-world network topologies. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it shows that reputation-based dynamic linking mechanisms can strongly increase cooperation rates in repeated PDG environments. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like settings, mechanisms that allow for social selection or partner choice based on reputation may increase cooperation, which could plausibly translate into higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency changes. The results are most relevant for settings where dynamic partner selection or reputation-based exclusion is possible.

- source: Cruz, T. D. D., Beersma, B., Dijkstra, M. T. M., & Bechtoldt, M. N. (2019). The Bright and Dark Side of Gossip for Cooperation in Groups. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The studies find that (1) people are more likely to gossip to protect group members from norm violators, but also gossip to vent emotions; (2) being the target of true negative gossip increases short-term intended work effort, but being the target of gossip (true or false) reduces long-term cooperative intentions toward the gossipers; (3) false gossip does not increase short-term cooperation. The results highlight both a 'bright side' (short-term compliance) and a 'dark side' (long-term alienation) of gossip as a social sanction. However, no actual payoff, group earnings, or efficiency measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, suggest that informal social sanctions like gossip can increase short-term compliance (analogous to increased contribution) but may undermine long-term cooperation (analogous to group cohesion or repeated cooperation). The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and motivational effects of informal sanctions, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are not systematically manipulated or reported in a way that supports prediction for the target task.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2006). Image scoring and cooperation in a cleaner fish mutualism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that client fish preferentially associate with cleaner fish that are observed to behave cooperatively, providing evidence for image scoring in a non-human animal. Cleaner fish, in turn, behave more cooperatively (feed against their preference) when their actions are being observed by a potential client (image-scoring scenario) compared to when they are not observed. This supports the hypothesis that reputation-based partner choice can promote cooperation. However, the study does not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the experimental setup is not a public goods game but an asymmetric mutualism with partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms (image scoring) can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric mutualisms, which may be relevant for understanding the effects of reputation or partner choice in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The findings may inform predictions about the role of observation and reputation in promoting cooperation, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Gradual reputation dynamics evolve and sustain cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The experimental results show that people use a three-level (good, neutral, bad) reputation system and update reputations gradually: cooperation increases reputation by one level, defection decreases it by one, except that defection against a bad recipient (justified defection) leaves reputation unchanged. Theoretical evolutionary game modeling demonstrates that, under certain conditions, tolerant norms that cooperate with good and neutral and defect only against bad can dominate and sustain high cooperation rates. However, the study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency or payoff; it focuses on reputation dynamics and the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its findings are adjacent: it informs how nuanced reputation and justified defection (a form of social sanction) are perceived and how these perceptions can support cooperation in indirect reciprocity. However, it does not manipulate or measure punishment costs, punishment effectiveness, or group payoffs, nor does it use a PGG or similar economic game. Thus, it should be used only as indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of social sanctions and cooperation.

- source: Bandiera, O., Barankay, I., & Rasul, I. (2005). Cooperation in collective action. *Economics of Transition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in a workplace setting with relative incentives (where individual effort imposes a negative externality on co-workers), workers cooperate more (i.e., reduce their productivity) when in smaller groups, with a higher share of co-nationals, with more homogeneous ability, and with more co-workers who have more to lose from shirking. These group characteristics do not affect productivity under piece rates, where there is no incentive to cooperate. The literature review highlights that institutions enabling monitoring, communication, and punishment can increase cooperation in public goods and common pool resource settings, but monetary punishment often reduces group welfare due to its cost. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment and group characteristics on cooperation in collective action settings, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The empirical results are most relevant for understanding how group size, heterogeneity, and incentive structure affect cooperation (proxied by productivity), rather than efficiency. The literature review suggests that punishment can increase cooperation but may reduce efficiency due to its cost, and that communication and monitoring are important moderators. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's findings should be used as indirect signals about the likely direction of cooperation and the possible tradeoff between increased cooperation and the cost of punishment.

- source: Bshary, R., Grutter, A. S., Willener, A. S. T., & Leimar, O. (2008). Pairs of cooperating cleaner fish provide better service quality than singletons. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Pairs of cleaner fish provide higher service quality to clients than singletons, mainly because females behave more cooperatively in pairs. Theoretical modeling shows that cooperation in pairs can yield higher service quality if there is added search efficiency, and empirical data (field and lab) support this. Aggression by males toward cheating females may function as a form of punishment, but this is not systematically manipulated or measured as a treatment. The outcomes are behavioral (cheating, cooperation, aggression), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that, in a biological system with prisoner's dilemma-like structure, the presence of a partner (pair vs singleton) can increase cooperative behavior and service quality, especially when there is some mechanism for partner control (e.g., aggression/punishment). However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is only adjacent to the prediction task and should be used as weak, indirect support for the idea that social context and partner control can moderate cooperation and service quality.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2020). The role of third parties in norm enforcement in customary courts among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and ethnographic evidence that, in small-scale societies like the Enga, restorative justice and third-party compensation are preferred over punitive sanctions for norm enforcement. While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It provides qualitative, contextual evidence about how cooperation can be sustained in a real-world OLG setting with limited observability, but does not report or manipulate efficiency, payoff, or formal punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Glowacki, L., & von Rueden, C. (2015). Leadership solves collective action problems in small-scale societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in both the Tsimane and Nyangatom societies, leaders tend to have more 'capital' (age, strength, social connections), and that leadership is more likely to become institutionalized in larger, denser, or more conflict-prone communities. Leadership is associated with improved group coordination and conflict mediation, and leaders often incur higher costs (e.g., risk in battle, time spent mediating). However, there is no direct evidence that leaders or leadership increase group efficiency or total payoffs in a way that maps onto public goods game outcomes. The paper notes that leader-based monitoring and sanctioning may reduce free-riding and improve the efficiency of reward or punishment allocation, but this is discussed in general terms and not measured as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for understanding the social and ecological conditions under which leadership, monitoring, and sanctioning emerge to solve collective action problems. It provides indirect support for the idea that leadership (and by extension, centralized punishment or monitoring) can improve group coordination and reduce free-riding, especially as group size and conflict increase. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the efficiency impact of punishment in controlled game environments, this paper offers only contextual and theoretical guidance, not direct empirical input.

- source: Simpson, B., Harrell, A., & Willer, R. (2013). Hidden Paths from Morality to Cooperation: Moral Judgments Promote Trust and Trustworthiness. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three experiments, the authors show that (1) making moral judgments of others' immoral behavior increases subsequent trustworthiness in a trust game (measured by amount returned), (2) this effect is mediated by increased self-perceived moral identity, and (3) observers trust and prefer to interact with strong moral judges, perceiving them as more moral and trustworthy. The behavioral outcomes are individual-level trust and trustworthiness, not group-level efficiency or payoff. The design does not include public goods games, explicit punishment, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that making moral judgments (not material punishment) can increase trustworthiness in trust games, and that observers trust moral judges more. However, there is no manipulation of punishment, no public goods game, and no group payoff or efficiency outcome. The results may be relevant for understanding how non-material social judgments can affect prosocial behavior, but they do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Oria, P. A., Wijnands, M., Alaii, J., & Leeuwis, C. (2018). Options for sustaining solar-powered mosquito trapping systems on Rusinga Island, Western Kenya: a social dilemma analysis. *Bmc Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that community members prefer individual responsibility for maintaining the mosquito trapping systems, with selective support for small, self-selected group savings (not large-scale collective action). Distrust in leaders, fear of misappropriation, and lack of confidence in group sanctions are major barriers to collective solutions. Some success is seen in small, trusted women's savings groups that can impose sanctions (e.g., expelling non-compliant members), but large governing bodies or compulsory group contributions are widely distrusted. The study highlights the importance of trust, group size, and leadership in sustaining public goods, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides qualitative, contextual evidence about the challenges of sustaining public goods in real-world settings, especially regarding trust, group size, and sanctioning. It suggests that effective punishment or sanctioning is difficult without strong trust and group identity, and that collective action may fail in large, heterogeneous groups. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions of treatment efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social context and group structure as moderators of collective action and sanctioning effectiveness.

- source: Lyle, H. F. (2017). Volver a Nuestras Raices: The Reemergence and Adaptation of Traditional Forms of Andean Reciprocity. *Journal of Latin American and Caribbean Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of formal or institutionalized punishment, collective action in the community is maintained through a combination of symbolic capital (reputation for generosity, skill, and reliability) and indirect social exclusion (withholding support from free riders). Households that invest more in collective action have larger and more robust support networks, suggesting positive assortment among prosocial individuals. Free riding is tolerated to some extent, and there is no evidence of direct punishment or sanctions. The system is sustained by the interplay of modified traditional reciprocity practices (ayni and minga), which have adapted to local needs. There is no direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but the system appears stable and resilient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as there is no experimental manipulation of punishment, no formal sanctions, and no measurement of efficiency or group payoff. However, it does suggest that in real-world settings, cooperation can be maintained without formal punishment through social mechanisms like reputation and indirect exclusion. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful as contextual evidence that alternative mechanisms (symbolic capital, social networks) can substitute for formal punishment, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

  findings: The paper finds that economic games can be strategically designed to reveal either private-world or real-world preferences, depending on which constraints are relaxed (e.g., resource constraints, anonymity, framing). In the Colombian case study, allocation and punishment behaviors in the games map onto real-world social variables (e.g., kinship, ethnicity, need), but also reveal preferences that are masked in observational data due to real-world constraints. Costly punishment (costly reduction) is more likely to be directed at wealthy or high-status individuals, and less likely at marginalized individuals, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The main contribution is methodological: showing how game design choices affect what is measured and how results relate to real-world behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses costly punishment (costly reduction) games, the outcomes are about who is punished and why, not about group payoff or efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment behavior is shaped by social context and game design, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for methodological considerations and for understanding the importance of matching game design to research questions and local context.

The resource allocation game is structurally different from a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no experimental manipulation of punishment, nor are efficiency or welfare outcomes reported. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding the role of norm transmission and social mechanisms (like storytelling) in promoting cooperation, but they do not provide quantitative or causal evidence relevant to the prediction task as defined.

- source: Hill, K. (2002). Altruistic cooperation during foraging by the ache, and the evolved human predisposition to cooperate. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: Ache men and women spend about 10% of their foraging time in cooperative acts that primarily benefit others, with some days exceeding 50%. Most cooperation is directed toward helping others acquire game, and the acts are often short-term altruistic. Cooperation is not limited to kin and is linked to widespread food sharing. The paper argues that such cooperation and sharing are part of an evolved human predisposition, and draws parallels to findings from experimental economics (including public goods games with punishment), but does not itself test or manipulate punishment, nor does it measure efficiency or group payoff in a controlled game-theoretic sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is an observational ethnographic study of real-world foraging cooperation, not an experimental manipulation of game design dimensions. While it discusses the importance of cooperation, sharing, and the possible role of punishment in maintaining social norms, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing context for the prevalence and forms of cooperation in small-scale societies, and in supporting the plausibility of mechanisms (such as contingent sharing and informal sanctions) that are modeled in public goods game experiments.

- source: Eisenbarth, S., Graham, L., & Rigterink, A. S. (2021). Can Reminders of Rules Induce Compliance? Experimental Evidence from a Common Pool Resource Setting. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The SMS reminder intervention increased self-reported knowledge of forest use rules and the perceived probability of sanctions for rule-breaking, but did not increase actual scrutiny or sanctioning of others. There was no significant effect on compliance with forest use rules or on forest use (measured both by self-report and satellite data). The intervention did not systematically reduce resource extraction or improve compliance, and thus did not provide evidence that reminders of rules (and increased perceived sanctions) improve efficiency or group welfare in this common pool resource context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common pool resource games, as it does not manipulate or directly measure actual punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The intervention only increased perceived probability of sanctions, not actual sanctioning or compliance, and did not reduce resource use. Thus, the findings suggest that increasing perceived sanctions via reminders alone may not be sufficient to improve efficiency or reduce overuse in common pool resource settings. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that behavioral nudges increasing perceived sanctions (without changing actual punishment mechanisms or costs) are unlikely to have strong effects on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Wismer, S., Pinto, A. I., Vail, A. L., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2014). Variation in Cleaner Wrasse Cooperation and Cognition: Influence of the Developmental Environment?. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: Cleaner wrasse from continuous (socially complex) reefs performed better in laboratory cooperation and cognition tasks than those from patch (socially simple) reefs. Specifically, they were more likely to feed against their preference (cooperate), respond to image scoring (bystander) situations, and learn to prefer ephemeral (visitor) options in a biological market task. Juveniles from both environments performed similarly and better than adults from simple environments, suggesting ontogenetic (learning/experience) effects. No differences in aggressiveness or boldness were found between habitats. The study suggests that social environmental complexity shapes the development and expression of cooperative and cognitive behaviors, but does not directly address efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it uses game-theoretic tasks and discusses mechanisms like punishment, partner switching, and image scoring, the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, learning) rather than payoff-based. The findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how social environment complexity moderates the development of cooperative strategies, but do not quantify efficiency or group payoff. Thus, the paper offers only adjacent, indirect support for prediction tasks focused on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Montoya, L. A., Montoya, I., & González, O. D. S. (2015). Lessons from collaborative governance and sociobiology theories for reinforcing sustained cooperation: a government food security case study. *Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that sustained interagency cooperation in public policy (here, food security in Bogotá) depends on (1) strong sponsorship (e.g., from central government or consensus), (2) stability of staff in the coordination system, and (3) mechanisms to reward or punish collaboration levels. The absence of these factors leads to decreased collaboration. The paper draws analogies from sociobiology (e.g., reciprocal altruism, punishment, reward) and collaborative governance theory, suggesting that similar mechanisms are relevant in human institutional settings. However, the findings are qualitative and do not include quantitative measures of efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent to the prediction task, as it discusses the importance of reward and punishment mechanisms for sustaining cooperation in real-world, multi-agent institutional settings, and draws on sociobiological theory. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main value is in supporting the general idea that mechanisms for reward and punishment can help sustain cooperation, especially when other supports (like sponsorship or staff stability) are present. It does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: HEINSOHN, R., & PACKER, C. (1995). COMPLEX COOPERATIVE STRATEGIES IN GROUP-TERRITORIAL AFRICAN LIONS. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that female lions show consistent individual differences in their willingness to participate in group territorial defense, with some acting as leaders and others as laggards. Laggards are recognized by leaders, who become more cautious in their presence but do not punish them or withdraw cooperation. Some laggards adjust their participation based on the odds of group success, while others do not. The diversity of strategies suggests that cooperation in large groups is more complex than predicted by simple reciprocity or punishment models. No evidence of punishment or sanctioning of laggards is found.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the maintenance of cooperation in group settings without formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It suggests that in naturalistic, high-stakes group conflicts, cooperation can persist with a mix of unconditional and conditional cooperators and laggards, and that punishment is not always used to enforce cooperation. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency/payoff outcomes, it offers only adjacent, qualitative insight for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It highlights the possibility that cooperation can be stable without punishment, but does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for the prediction task.
